This reference synthesizes monitoring recommendations from authoritative associations for the CMS CCW 30 chronic conditions. It is a practice planning and education resource for administrators, physicians, and providers. Every recommendation is footnoted to its source so it can be verified and updated as guidelines evolve. Clinical decisions for individual patients remain with the treating clinician.

CMID — Clinical Monitoring Intelligence Database

A reference work of monitoring recommendations for the 30 chronic conditions tracked by the CMS Chronic Conditions Data Warehouse (CCW). One monograph per condition. Every recommendation is footnoted back to the originating association and source document so the content can be updated as guidelines evolve.

This is a practice planning and education resource for administrators, physicians, and providers. It does not replace clinical judgment, define the totality of service for any patient, or generate patient-specific recommendations.

What's here

cmid/ ├── README.md this file ├── index.md table of contents — conditions by prevalence and by category ├── bibliography.md master list of cited associations + source documents with URLs ├── conditions/ │ ├── _template.md the template every condition file follows │ ├── 01-hypertension.md │ ├── 02-hyperlipidemia.md │ └── ... (30 files, numbered by 2022 CCW prevalence rank) ├── render.py build script — produces out/cmid.html └── out/ └── cmid.html rendered single-file reference; the artifact for sharing

Synthesis rule

When multiple associations issue overlapping recommendations, the synthesized recommendation follows this authority order:

  1. CMS / federal agency (CMS, USPSTF, CDC, NIH/NHLBI/NIDDK/NCI)
  2. USPSTF specifically for screening intervals
  3. Specialty professional society (AHA/ACC, ADA, ASN, etc.)
  4. Disease foundation (NKF, ALA, Arthritis Foundation, etc.)

Conflicts are flagged, never silently resolved — the disagreement itself is often the signal a clinician needs. When sources agree on an item but disagree on interval, the more conservative (more frequent) interval wins.

Updating

Recommendations change. To update:

  1. Edit the relevant conditions/NN-name.md file.
  2. Update or add the citation in bibliography.md.
  3. Re-run python render.py to regenerate out/cmid.html.

Markdown is the source of truth. HTML is the artifact.

Rendering

pip install markdown python render.py

Open out/cmid.html in any browser.

Index

CMS CCW 30 chronic conditions, 2022 Medicare FFS prevalence (n ≈ 28.6M beneficiaries). Source: CCW Table B.2.a, April 2024.

Click any condition to open its full monograph (prose, footnoted recommendations, source citations).

Hypertension

ICD-10: I10 (essential), I11–I15 (secondary/complicated forms) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 66.8% — rank 1 of 30 Category: Metabolic / Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA, USPSTF, NHLBI, CMS, CDC

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — BMP, K+, eGFR* Lipid panel, UACR, fasting glucose / HbA1c — TSH
Imaging — — — — — ECG (baseline), Echo (Stage 2 or LVH), Renal US (resistant HTN)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, cardiac auscultation — — Fundoscopic exam ABI / peripheral pulse (if ≥65 or smoker) —
Functional / surveys — — — PHQ-9, 10-yr ASCVD risk (Pooled Cohort Equations) — —

* every 6 months if on ACE/ARB, diuretic, or MRA; otherwise annually.

Summary recommendation

Adults with diagnosed hypertension should have blood pressure measured at every clinical encounter, supplemented by out-of-office measurement (home BP monitoring or 24-hour ambulatory BP monitoring) at diagnosis and when treatment changes.12 The current ACC/AHA target is <130/80 mmHg for most adults.1 Baseline laboratory evaluation screens for secondary causes and end-organ damage; routine surveillance focuses on renal function and electrolytes (especially in patients on RAAS-acting agents or diuretics), lipid status, glycemic status, and urinary albumin. Annual reassessment of 10-year ASCVD risk informs concurrent statin and aspirin decisions.1 Echocardiography is reserved for new Stage 2 hypertension, suspected left ventricular hypertrophy, or clinical change. The most conservative interval among overlapping recommendations wins when sources disagree on cadence.

Laboratory

  • Basic Metabolic Panel (BMP) — annually if stable; every 6 months if on ACE inhibitor, ARB, or diuretic. Monitors potassium, sodium, creatinine, and bicarbonate.1
  • eGFR / serum creatinine — every 6 months on RAAS therapy or in patients with CKD; otherwise annually. Detects nephropathy and guides medication dosing.1
  • Serum potassium — every 6 months on potassium-affecting therapy (ACE/ARB, diuretic, MRA). Target 3.5–5.0 mmol/L.1
  • Fasting lipid panel — annually; informs concurrent ASCVD risk-driven statin decisions.1
  • Urinalysis with urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) — annually. Screens for early hypertensive nephropathy; ACR ≥30 mg/g indicates end-organ involvement.1
  • Fasting glucose or HbA1c — annually. Hypertension and dysglycemia frequently co-occur and concurrent diabetes lowers the BP target and intensifies monitoring.1
  • TSH — at diagnosis and as clinically indicated; consider in resistant hypertension to rule out thyroid contribution.1

Imaging

  • 12-lead ECG — at diagnosis (baseline LVH and rhythm assessment); repeat as clinically indicated.1
  • Echocardiogram — at diagnosis if Stage 2 hypertension or suspected LVH; otherwise as indicated by clinical change.1
  • Renal ultrasound or renal artery imaging — only when resistant hypertension or secondary cause suspected.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every clinical encounter, using validated technique (seated, after 5 min rest, appropriate cuff size, average of two readings).12
  • Out-of-office BP measurement — confirm diagnosis and titration with home BP monitoring or ambulatory BP monitoring; USPSTF specifically requires out-of-office confirmation before establishing the diagnosis.2
  • Weight and BMI — every visit; a 5–10 lb weight reduction lowers SBP ~4–5 mmHg.1
  • Cardiac auscultation — every visit; assess for S3, S4, murmurs.1
  • Peripheral pulse and ABI screening — every 2–3 years in patients ≥65 or with smoking history; screens for PAD.1
  • Fundoscopic exam — annually in chronic hypertension; assesses hypertensive retinopathy.1

Functional / surveys

  • 10-year ASCVD risk (Pooled Cohort Equations) — annually; drives concurrent statin and aspirin decision-making.1
  • PHQ-9 depression screen — annually; hypertension and depression are bidirectionally associated.1

Notes

  • Screening vs. surveillance. USPSTF (2021) addresses screening in asymptomatic adults ≥18 — annual screening if ≥40 or at increased risk, every 3–5 years for normotensive 18–39 year-olds at average risk — and requires out-of-office confirmation before diagnosis. ACC/AHA (2017) covers post-diagnosis management and surveillance.21
  • BP target disagreement. ACC/AHA (2017) sets <130/80 for most adults. Some specialty groups have used higher targets (e.g., older adult-specific thresholds). Where overlapping recommendations exist with different targets, the lower (more conservative) target is reflected here per the synthesis rule.1
  • Pending updates. ACC/AHA hypertension guideline updates are anticipated periodically; verify currency at refresh.

Footnotes

  1. Whelton PK et al. 2017 ACC/AHA Guideline for the Prevention, Detection, Evaluation, and Management of High Blood Pressure in Adults. Hypertension. 2018;71(6):e13-e115. See bibliography.md → aha-htn-2017. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. US Preventive Services Task Force. Screening for Hypertension in Adults: Reaffirmation Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2021;325(16):1650-1656. See bibliography.md → uspstf-htn-2021. ↩↩↩↩

Hyperlipidemia

ICD-10: E78.5 (unspecified), E78.0 (pure hypercholesterolemia), E78.1 (hypertriglyceridemia), E78.2 (mixed) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 65.5% — rank 2 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA, USPSTF, NLA, AACE, NHLBI

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lipid panel during titration (4–12 wk after start or change) — Lipid panel (stable patient), HbA1c (statin-associated DM surveillance) — Lp(a) once in adulthood, apoB (residual-risk question), ALT baseline (pre-statin), CK baseline (high SAMS risk only)
Imaging — — — — — Coronary artery calcium (CAC) score — borderline/intermediate risk where statin decision is uncertain
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, smoking status — — — — —
Functional / surveys Adherence review, diet / lifestyle counseling — — — ASCVD 10-year risk recalculation (every 4–6 years via PREVENT equations) —

Summary recommendation

Adults with diagnosed hyperlipidemia should have a follow-up lipid panel 4–12 weeks after starting or changing lipid-lowering therapy to confirm adherence and percent LDL-C reduction (≥50% on high-intensity statin, ≥30% on moderate-intensity), then every 3–12 months until stable, transitioning to annual surveillance once at goal on unchanged therapy.14 The 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline reintroduced explicit numeric LDL-C targets — <55 mg/dL for very-high-risk ASCVD, <70 mg/dL for high risk, <100 mg/dL for borderline/intermediate — and added one-time universal Lp(a) measurement as a Class I recommendation.2 Intensification (ezetimibe, then PCSK9 inhibitor, bempedoic acid, or inclisiran) is triggered when LDL-C remains above the risk-stratified threshold despite maximally tolerated statin. Routine ALT and CK surveillance is not recommended; check ALT once before statin initiation and CK only at baseline in patients at increased SAMS risk, then symptom-driven thereafter.1 Each visit should include adherence review, lifestyle counseling, BP/BMI/smoking assessment, and periodic recalculation of 10-year ASCVD risk using the AHA PREVENT equations.

Laboratory

  • Lipid panel (fasting or non-fasting) — 4–12 weeks after statin initiation or dose change to assess adherence and percent LDL-C reduction; every 3–12 months until stable; then annually.14
  • LDL-C against target — at each lipid panel. 2026 numeric thresholds: <55 mg/dL very-high-risk ASCVD, <70 mg/dL high risk, <100 mg/dL borderline/intermediate. 2018 ACC/AHA used percent-reduction goals plus threshold triggers (≥70 mg/dL, ≥100 mg/dL).21
  • Non-HDL cholesterol (calculated) — at each lipid panel; goal approximately 30 mg/dL above the LDL-C goal.42
  • Apolipoprotein B (apoB) — selectively when LDL-C and non-HDL-C goals are met but residual ASCVD risk is suspected; particularly cardiometabolic disease, hypertriglyceridemia, established ASCVD.2
  • Lipoprotein(a) [Lp(a)] — once in adulthood as universal measurement (new Class I in 2026); does not require repetition. Threshold ≥125 nmol/L (~50 mg/dL) signals elevated ASCVD risk.2
  • ALT (hepatic transaminases) — baseline once before statin initiation; routine surveillance not recommended without symptoms.1
  • CK (creatine kinase) — baseline only in patients at elevated SAMS risk; otherwise symptom-driven.1
  • HbA1c or fasting glucose — periodically per diabetes-screening guidance; statin therapy is continued despite incident DM, but the new diagnosis warrants management.13

Imaging

  • Coronary artery calcium (CAC) score — as indicated when 10-year ASCVD risk is 5–<20% and the statin decision is uncertain. CAC = 0 may justify deferral; CAC ≥100 or ≥75th percentile supports initiation. Role expanded in 2026 guideline.12

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure, weight / BMI, waist circumference, smoking status — at each clinical encounter as part of cardiovascular risk reassessment.1

Functional / surveys

  • Adherence and lifestyle assessment — at each follow-up visit. Heart-healthy diet, ≥150 minutes/week moderate-intensity activity, tobacco cessation; shared decision-making about therapy is Class I.1
  • 10-year ASCVD risk (AHA PREVENT equations) — every 4–6 years for adults 40–75 without ASCVD. PREVENT replaces Pooled Cohort Equations in the 2026 guideline. Risk strata: <5% low, 5–<7.5% borderline, 7.5–<20% intermediate, ≥20% high.21

Notes

  • Pending update — 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline supersedes the 2018 Cholesterol Guideline. Key changes: reintroduced numeric LDL-C goals (<55 / <70 / <100), universal one-time Lp(a) measurement, expanded selective apoB use, PREVENT replaces Pooled Cohort Equations, expanded CAC role.2
  • USPSTF vs. ACC/AHA scope mismatch. USPSTF 2022 addresses statin initiation for primary prevention only and is silent on monitoring cadence. Clinicians cannot derive surveillance intervals from USPSTF — ACC/AHA, NLA, and AACE fill that gap.31
  • Numeric targets vs. percent reduction. 2018 ACC/AHA emphasized percent LDL-C reduction; AACE 2017 and 2025 retained numeric goals; 2026 ACC/AHA returned to explicit numeric goals — US guidance now aligns more closely with international targets.562
  • Chronic-monitoring interval disagreement. ACC/AHA: "every 3–12 months as needed." NLA: "every 12 months" for stable patients. Practical synthesis (conservative wins): annual lipid panel for stable patients; quarterly during titration.14

Footnotes

  1. Grundy SM et al. 2018 AHA/ACC Guideline on the Management of Blood Cholesterol. Circulation. 2019;139(25):e1082-e1143. See bibliography.md → accaha-chol-2018. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. 2026 ACC/AHA/Multisociety Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia. Circulation. 2026. See bibliography.md → accaha-dyslip-2026. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. USPSTF. Statin Use for the Primary Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease in Adults: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2022;328(8):746-753. See bibliography.md → uspstf-statin-2022. ↩↩

  4. Jacobson TA et al. NLA Recommendations for Patient-Centered Management of Dyslipidemia. J Clin Lipidol. 2015. See bibliography.md → nla-2015. ↩↩↩↩

  5. Jellinger PS et al. AACE/ACE Guidelines for Management of Dyslipidemia and Prevention of Cardiovascular Disease. Endocr Pract. 2017. See bibliography.md → aace-2017. ↩

  6. AACE Task Force. Clinical Practice Guideline on Pharmacologic Management of Adults With Dyslipidemia. Endocr Pract. 2025. See bibliography.md → aace-2025. ↩

Rheumatoid Arthritis / Osteoarthritis

ICD-10: RA — M05.x, M06.x. OA — M15–M19. CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 36.2% — rank 3 of 30 (combined; CCW does not separate) Category: Musculoskeletal Primary associations consulted: ACR, EULAR, USPSTF (limited), NIAMS, Arthritis Foundation, AAO (HCQ retinopathy)

RA and OA are distinct diseases combined into a single CCW category. They share little in monitoring approach and are addressed separately below.

Rheumatoid Arthritis

At-a-glance schedule (RA)

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — CBC, ALT/AST, Cr (DMARD safety after stable); lipid panel + CBC at 4–8 wk then q3–6 mo on tocilizumab / sarilumab / JAK — TB screening (if ongoing exposure risk); vaccination status review (influenza, pneumococcal, RZV, COVID-19) — TB / HBV / HCV pre-biologic; HBV DNA q1–3 mo if HBcAb+ on biologic without prophylaxis; baseline labs pre-DMARD initiation
Imaging — — — Hand / wrist / foot radiographs (every 1–2 years to track erosive progression) — —
Physical exam 28-joint tender / swollen joint count — — — — —
Functional / surveys Disease activity (DAS28-CRP, CDAI, SDAI, RAPID3, or PAS-II); HAQ-DI or MDHAQ Disease activity every 3 mo until target reached Disease activity every 6 mo in sustained remission Dilated fundus + visual fields + SD-OCT (HCQ users starting year 5; sooner with risk factors) — —

Summary recommendation (RA)

Primary-care monitoring of RA follows a treat-to-target framework: at every visit (minimum every 3 months until low disease activity or remission, every 6 months thereafter), document a validated composite disease-activity score (DAS28-CRP, CDAI, SDAI, RAPID3, or PAS-II), a 28-joint count, and a functional assessment (HAQ-DI or MDHAQ).12 DMARD-safety labs (CBC, ALT/AST, creatinine) are obtained at baseline and every 2–4 weeks during the first 3 months of methotrexate, leflunomide, or sulfasalazine, every 8–12 weeks during months 3–6, then every 12 weeks; hydroxychloroquine requires only baseline labs but mandates annual ophthalmologic screening with visual fields and SD-OCT starting at 5 years of use (sooner with risk factors), with the dose capped at ≤5 mg/kg actual body weight.26 Biologics and JAK inhibitors require pre-treatment TB, HBV, and HCV screening; tocilizumab, sarilumab, and JAK inhibitors add lipid surveillance at 4–8 weeks and then every 3–6 months.2 Hand and foot radiographs at baseline and periodically (every 1–2 years) track erosive progression; vaccinations are reviewed annually.4

Laboratory (RA)

  • CBC, ALT/AST, serum creatinine — baseline; every 2–4 weeks during first 3 months of methotrexate, leflunomide, or sulfasalazine; every 8–12 weeks months 3–6; every 12 weeks thereafter.2
  • CBC, ALT/AST, Cr (intensified) — every 4–8 weeks during first 6 months, then every 3 months for patients with NAFLD or hepatic risk on methotrexate.1
  • Hydroxychloroquine monotherapy — baseline labs only; periodic only if clinically indicated.2
  • Lipid panel, ALT/AST, neutrophil count — 4–8 weeks after starting tocilizumab or sarilumab, then every 3–6 months (lipids every 24 weeks).2
  • Lipid panel, ALT/AST, CBC with neutrophils — 4–8 weeks after JAK inhibitor (tofacitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib) initiation, then every 3 months.2
  • CBC, platelets — every 2–4 months on rituximab.2
  • TB (IGRA or TST), HBV (HBsAg, anti-HBc, anti-HBs), HCV antibody — baseline before any biologic or tsDMARD; annual TB screening if ongoing exposure risk.21
  • HBV DNA — every 1–3 months during and 6–12 months after biologic / JAK in HBcAb-positive patients without antiviral prophylaxis.1

Imaging (RA)

  • Plain radiographs of hands, wrists, feet — baseline; periodically (every 1–2 years) to assess erosive progression, particularly seropositive disease.7

Physical examination (RA)

  • 28-joint tender / swollen joint count — at every visit; minimum every 3 months until target; every 6 months in remission.12

Functional / surveys (RA)

  • Disease-activity measure (DAS28-CRP, CDAI, SDAI, RAPID3, or PAS-II) — every visit. Targets: ACR/EULAR Boolean remission (TJC ≤1, SJC ≤1, CRP ≤1 mg/dL, PtGA ≤1), or SDAI ≤3.3 / CDAI ≤2.8.1
  • Functional status (HAQ-DI or MDHAQ) — at each visit or at least annually.3
  • Vaccination review — annually (influenza, pneumococcal, recombinant zoster, COVID-19, Tdap).4
  • Dilated fundus + visual fields + SD-OCT — baseline within first year of HCQ use; annual screening starting at year 5 (sooner if dose >5 mg/kg, renal disease, tamoxifen, or age).6

Osteoarthritis

At-a-glance schedule (OA)

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — ALT (chronic acetaminophen); CBC / Cr / BP (chronic NSAID periodic surveillance)
Imaging — — — — — Plain radiographs — diagnostic uncertainty or surgical planning; not recommended for routine surveillance
Physical exam Joint exam (ROM, effusion, alignment, crepitus); BMI / weight in overweight knee or hip OA — — — — —
Functional / surveys Pain (NRS / VAS); function (WOMAC or KOOS knee; HOOS hip; AUSCAN / FIHOA hand); exercise / self-management adherence — — — — Depression / anxiety / sleep / coping screen

Summary recommendation (OA)

OA surveillance is symptom- and function-driven, not test-driven. At each visit reassess pain (NRS or VAS) and function using a validated instrument (WOMAC or KOOS for knee, HOOS for hip, AUSCAN or FIHOA for hand), confirm engagement in the strongly recommended exercise and self-management program, and (in knee or hip OA) measure BMI to track the clinically meaningful 5% weight-loss target.5 Hepatotoxicity surveillance with ALT is advised for chronic acetaminophen users, particularly at doses approaching 3 g/day; CBC, creatinine, and blood pressure should be checked periodically in chronic oral NSAID users.5 The 2019 ACR/AF guideline does not recommend routine surveillance imaging — radiographs are reserved for diagnostic uncertainty or to evaluate worsening symptoms and surgical candidacy. Mental health, sleep, and coping should be screened given their high comorbid burden and direct effect on pain perception.5

Laboratory (OA)

  • ALT — periodically in chronic acetaminophen users, particularly at doses approaching 3 g/day.5
  • CBC, creatinine / eGFR, blood pressure — periodically (commonly every 6–12 months) in chronic oral NSAID users.5

Imaging (OA)

  • Plain radiograph (weight-bearing knee, AP pelvis for hip, PA hand) — at diagnosis if uncertainty; not recommended for routine follow-up. Repeat only with clinical change or to evaluate surgical candidacy.5

Physical examination (OA)

  • Joint exam (range of motion, effusion, alignment, crepitus) — every visit.5
  • BMI and weight — at each visit for overweight / obese hip and knee OA; target ≥5% body-weight loss.5

Functional / surveys (OA)

  • Pain (NRS or VAS) — each clinical encounter; reassess after each therapeutic trial.5
  • Function (WOMAC or KOOS for knee; HOOS for hip; AUSCAN or FIHOA for hand) — each clinical encounter.5
  • Self-management / exercise adherence; gait, balance, ADL screening — ongoing.5
  • Mental-health screening (depression, anxiety, sleep, coping) — periodically.5

Notes

  • Methotrexate lab cadence disagreement. EULAR 2019 and some real-world data support every-3-month monitoring after stability; ACR / FDA labeling specifies every 8–12 weeks. Emerging evidence suggests 6-month intervals may be safe for stable patients but is not yet in ACR guidance — conservative cadence (every 8–12 weeks) wins.2
  • Disease-activity instrument choice. ACR endorses any of five measures without preference; EULAR prefers DAS28; primary-care literature favors RAPID3 for feasibility.1
  • HCQ retinopathy screening. AAO recommends starting annual screening at year 5 for low-risk patients; ACR / rheumatology practice often defaults to baseline + annual when risk factors are present.6
  • OA imaging. ACR/AF 2019 does not recommend routine follow-up radiographs; OARSI 2019 similarly de-emphasizes surveillance imaging. There is no major US guideline endorsing scheduled OA imaging surveillance, in contrast with common clinical practice.5
  • Acetaminophen for OA disagreement. ACR/AF 2019 conditionally recommends it with hepatotoxicity monitoring; NICE 2022 (UK) recommends against routine acetaminophen — an internationally relevant divergence.5

  2. Singh JA et al. 2015 ACR Guideline for the Treatment of Rheumatoid Arthritis. Arthritis Care Res. 2016;68(1):1-25. See bibliography.md → acr-ra-2015. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  4. Bass AR et al. 2022 ACR Guideline for Vaccinations in Patients With Rheumatic and Musculoskeletal Diseases. Arthritis Care Res. 2023. See bibliography.md → acr-vax-2022. ↩↩

Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia

ICD-10: N40.0, N40.1, N40.2, N40.3 CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 29.1% (males only) — rank 4 of 30 Category: Other (urologic; males only) Primary associations consulted: AUA, USPSTF (PSA screening only), NIDDK, AAFP, Urology Care Foundation

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — Urinalysis (baseline; repeat with new hematuria / UTI / worsening symptoms); PSA (baseline if would change management, pre–5-ARI, per shared decision for PCa screening — USPSTF every 2–4 yr if 55–69); PSA reassessment 6 mo after starting 5-ARI; serum creatinine (red-flag features only)
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam — — — DRE (NIDDK: annually or more often; AUA: baseline + clinical-judgment thereafter) — —
Functional / surveys IPSS (AUA-SI) at each follow-up visit — — IPSS annual reassessment (watchful waiting); 5-ARI response at 3–6 months — Post-void residual (PVR — retention or pre-surgical); uroflowmetry (pre-procedure or discordant symptoms); 4–12 wk response visit after starting alpha-blocker / PDE5i / beta-3 agonist / anticholinergic; post-procedural follow-up at 1, 3, 6, 12 months

Summary recommendation

For a male with diagnosed BPH/LUTS, surveillance is built around the IPSS administered at baseline and at every follow-up; mild or non-bothersome symptoms (IPSS ≤7) are managed by watchful waiting with annual reassessment including symptom score, focused history, urinalysis as indicated, and DRE.134 Patients started on an alpha-blocker, PDE5 inhibitor, beta-3 agonist, or anticholinergic should be reassessed at 4–12 weeks (typically ≈4 weeks) for response and tolerability; those on a 5-ARI are reassessed at 3–6 months, with a new PSA baseline established at ~6 months and measured PSA doubled thereafter for cancer-risk interpretation.1 Worsening IPSS, elevated or rising PVR, recurrent UTI, hematuria, retention, renal impairment, or stones trigger urology referral. PSA in the BPH-monitored patient serves two distinct purposes that must not be conflated: a BPH-management role (estimates prostate volume; cutoff >1.5 ng/mL guides 5-ARI candidacy) and a prostate-cancer screening role (USPSTF restricts to shared decision-making in men 55–69 every 2–4 years; recommends against in men ≥70).12

Laboratory

  • Urinalysis — baseline; repeat as indicated (new hematuria, dysuria, suspected UTI, worsening symptoms).1
  • PSA — offered at baseline when prostate-cancer diagnosis would change management, or when PSA would change BPH management (5-ARI candidacy: PSA >1.5 ng/mL supports prostate enlargement); repeat per shared-decision PCa screening cadence (USPSTF: every 2–4 years for ages 55–69).12
  • PSA reassessment on 5-ARI — baseline before starting; repeat at ~6 months to establish new nadir, then periodically. Expect ~50% reduction by 6 months; any rise above nadir warrants urologic evaluation; double measured PSA for cancer-risk interpretation.1
  • Serum creatinine — as indicated (not routine). Obtain with elevated PVR, hydronephrosis, recurrent UTI, hematuria, or suspected obstructive uropathy.1

Imaging

No routine imaging is recommended for surveillance.1

Physical examination

  • Digital rectal examination (DRE) — baseline. NIDDK patient guidance states "annually or more often" during ongoing BPH care; AUA treats DRE as a baseline assessment with repetition driven by clinical change (reliably detects glands >50 mL).134

Functional / surveys

  • IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score / AUA-SI) — baseline; every follow-up visit; annual minimum in watchful waiting. Bands: mild 0–7, moderate 8–19, severe ≥20. A ≥3–4-point change is clinically meaningful and triggers treatment reassessment.1
  • Post-void residual (PVR) — as indicated at baseline if obstruction or retention suspected; reassess if symptoms persist or worsen, before surgical referral, and in post-procedural follow-up.1
  • Uroflowmetry — optional at baseline; may be repeated at follow-up (4–12 weeks post-treatment) or before surgical decision. Qmax <10 mL/s suggests bladder outlet obstruction.1
  • Treatment-response reassessment — 4–12 weeks after starting medical therapy (≈4 weeks for alpha-blockers, beta-3 agonists, PDE5 inhibitors, anticholinergics; 3–6 months for 5-ARIs).1
  • Watchful-waiting reassessment — annually for men with mild symptoms (IPSS ≤7) or non-bothersome moderate symptoms.14
  • Post-procedural follow-up (TURP, PVP, HoLEP / ThuLEP, WVTT / Rezum, PUL / UroLift, PAE, TIPD) — early post-op visit, then periodic IPSS / PVR / uroflow at intervals defined by procedure (commonly 1, 3, 6, 12 months) with ongoing annual symptom reassessment.1

Notes

  • PSA frequency in the BPH patient — conflicting frames. AUA endorses offering PSA at BPH workup when it would change management (including before 5-ARI) and supports periodic monitoring during 5-ARI therapy. USPSTF frames PSA strictly as asymptomatic screening every 2–4 years for ages 55–69, against in men ≥70 — USPSTF does not address symptomatic BPH patients. The gap is closed via shared decision-making.12
  • DRE cadence disagreement. NIDDK patient material says "annually or more often." AUA treats DRE as baseline with clinical-judgment repetition. AAFP notes limited routine utility (reliable only for glands >50 mL).134
  • PVR and uroflowmetry as routine surveillance. AUA lists both as conditional / optional, used selectively for diagnostic uncertainty or pre-procedural planning. Some primary-care reviews include them as routine annual measures; AAFP 2023 explicitly notes PVR is not useful for routine monitoring.14

Footnotes

  1. Sandhu JS et al. Management of Lower Urinary Tract Symptoms Attributed to Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia: AUA Guideline Amendment 2023. J Urol. 2024;211(1):11-19. See bibliography.md → aua-bph-2023. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. USPSTF. Final Recommendation Statement: Prostate Cancer Screening (2018). See bibliography.md → uspstf-pca-2018. ↩↩↩

  3. NIDDK. Enlarged Prostate (Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia). See bibliography.md → niddk-bph. ↩↩↩

  4. Ng M, Leslie SW, Baradhi KM. Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia: Rapid Evidence Review. Am Fam Physician. 2023;107(6):613-622. See bibliography.md → aafp-bph-2023. ↩↩↩↩↩

Diabetes Mellitus (Type 2)

ICD-10: E11.x (Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus, with or without complications) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 26.4% — rank 5 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: ADA, AACE, USPSTF, KDIGO, AGS, CMS, NIDDK

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs CGM Time-in-Range review (if applicable) HbA1c (if not at goal or after therapy change) HbA1c (stable, at goal); Cr / eGFR (CKD G3); 4–12 wk lipid panel post statin start / change UACR, Cr / eGFR (no CKD), lipid panel (on statin), ALT / AST + FIB-4 (MASLD screen), TSH (periodic) — B12 (long-term metformin); K+ (on ACEi / ARB / MRA / finerenone); risk-tier CKD labs (G3 q6–12 mo, G4 q3–5 mo, G5 q1–3 mo)
Imaging — — — — — DEXA bone density (≥5 yr post-diagnosis; q2–3 yr per fracture risk; SGLT2i / TZD users)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI / waist, hypoglycemia review — — Comprehensive foot exam, dilated eye exam (q1–2 yr if no retinopathy and at glycemic goal; annual if retinopathy), distal polyneuropathy assessment (10-g monofilament + pinprick / temperature / vibration), dental exam — Foot exam every visit if prior ulcer / sensory loss
Functional / surveys Smoking / tobacco / e-cigarette + cessation counseling — — ASCVD 10-yr risk (PREVENT), PHQ-2 / PHQ-9, GAD-7, diabetes distress (DDS or PAID), disordered eating screen, SDoH screen, vaccination review, DSMES reinforcement; cognitive screen + function / falls / frailty (≥65); malnutrition screen (weight-loss pharmacotherapy or post-metabolic surgery) — MNT (Medicare NCD 180.1: initial 3 hr + 2 hr / yr); DSMES at diagnosis, annually, with new complications, at care transitions (Medicare DSMT: 10 hr initial + 2 hr / yr)

Summary recommendation

Primary-care monitoring of an adult with Type 2 Diabetes is built around visits every 3–6 months — quarterly when not at glycemic goal or after a therapy change, every 6 months when stable and at goal.1 At every visit document weight / BMI, blood pressure (target <130/80; consider <120 systolic in high CV / kidney risk), hypoglycemia events, medication adherence and tolerability, tobacco use, and (when on CGM) time-in-range (target >70%).1 The annual lab panel includes HbA1c (twice yearly minimum; target <7% for most nonpregnant adults), UACR, serum creatinine / eGFR, lipid panel (annually once on a statin), ALT / AST with FIB-4 for MASLD, and TSH periodically; B12 is checked periodically on long-term metformin and potassium when on ACEi / ARB / MRA / finerenone.12 Complications surveillance comprises annual comprehensive foot exam (every visit if prior ulcer or sensory loss), distal polyneuropathy assessment, dilated eye exam at diagnosis and every 1–2 years thereafter if low risk, and CKD risk-tier-driven kidney monitoring (1–4× yearly).1 Cardiovascular surveillance includes annual ASCVD risk estimation (PREVENT), lipid optimization (LDL <70 primary prevention, <55 with ASCVD), and BP control. Psychosocial and geriatric surveillance — at least annual depression, diabetes distress, anxiety, disordered eating, SDoH, and (≥65) cognitive and function / fall-risk screening — round out the visit. Immunizations are reviewed annually; DSMES and MNT are reinforced at diagnosis, yearly, with new complications, or at care transitions.

Laboratory

  • HbA1c — every 6 months if at goal and stable; quarterly if not at goal or after therapy change. Target <7% for most nonpregnant adults; individualized (older adults / AGS: 7.5–8% typical).15
  • CGM-derived Time in Range (TIR) — reviewed at each visit if patient uses CGM. TIR >70% (70–180 mg/dL); time <70 mg/dL <4% (<1% for older adults).1
  • Fasting lipid panel — at diagnosis and initial evaluation; every 5 years if <40 and not on lipid therapy; annually on lipid therapy; 4–12 weeks after statin start or dose change. Targets: LDL <70 mg/dL primary prevention age 40–75; LDL <55 mg/dL with ASCVD (or ≥50% reduction).1
  • Serum creatinine / eGFR — annually if eGFR ≥60 and normal albuminuria; risk-tier-driven if CKD present (G3 every 6–12 months; G4 every 3–5 months; G5 every 1–3 months).12
  • Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) — annually at minimum; 1–4× yearly per CKD heat-map risk category; 2 of 3 abnormal over 3–6 months to confirm. Bands: <30 normal, 30–299 moderately increased, ≥300 severely increased.12
  • Serum potassium — at initiation, dose change, and periodically on ACEi / ARB / MRA / finerenone.1
  • TSH — at diagnosis and as clinically indicated.1
  • ALT / AST with FIB-4 (MASLD screen) — at diagnosis and annually; FIB-4 <1.3 low risk.1
  • Vitamin B12 — periodically on long-term metformin (particularly with anemia or neuropathy).1

Imaging

  • DEXA bone density — ≥5 years post-diagnosis, then every 2–3 years per fracture risk (older adults, long duration, on SGLT2 inhibitor or TZD).1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every routine visit; home BP encouraged. Target <130/80; consider <120 systolic if high CV / kidney risk.1
  • Weight, height, BMI, waist circumference — every routine visit.1
  • Comprehensive foot exam (inspection, pulses, 10-g monofilament + one of pinprick / temperature / vibration) — at least annually; every visit if prior ulcer / amputation / sensory loss.1
  • Dilated comprehensive eye exam (or validated retinal imaging) — at diagnosis; every 1–2 years if no retinopathy and at glycemic goal; annually if retinopathy; more often if progressing.1
  • Distal symmetric polyneuropathy assessment — annually starting at diagnosis.1
  • Dental / oral examination — at least annually.1

Functional / surveys

  • ASCVD 10-year risk estimation (PREVENT) — annually; informs statin intensity for adults 40–75 without ASCVD.1
  • Hypoglycemia risk and event review — every visit on insulin or sulfonylurea. Target no level 2 / 3 events.1
  • Cognitive screening (Mini-Cog, MoCA) — annually for adults ≥65.15
  • Functional status / fall risk / frailty — annually for adults ≥65.15
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) — at least annually.1
  • Diabetes distress (DDS or PAID) — at least annually.1
  • Anxiety / fear of hypoglycemia (GAD-7) — at least annually.1
  • Disordered eating screen — at least annually.1
  • Smoking / tobacco / e-cigarette assessment + cessation counseling — every visit.1
  • Malnutrition screen — periodically on weight-loss pharmacotherapy or after metabolic surgery (new 2025 recommendation).1
  • Vaccination review (influenza annually; pneumococcal; HBV if <60; RZV ≥50; Tdap; COVID-19; RSV ≥60) — annually per ACIP / ADA Table 4.4.1
  • DSMES — at diagnosis, annually, with new complications, and at care transitions. CMS DSMT benefit: 10 hours initial + 2 hours / year follow-up.16
  • Medical Nutrition Therapy (RD-delivered) — initial 3 hours + 2 hours / year follow-up under Medicare NCD 180.1.61
  • Social determinants of health screen — routinely.1

Notes

  • A1c target disagreement. ADA: <7% for most nonpregnant adults; AACE: ≤6.5% when safely achievable; AGS: 7.5–8% for most older adults, with explicit caution against <6.5% in this group.145
  • Older-adult target stratification. ADA Section 13: <7.0–7.5% (healthy older), <8.0% (intermediate health), avoid stringent targets (poor health). AGS sets a higher default regardless of health status.15
  • Albuminuria cadence in established DKD. KDIGO 2022 emphasizes risk-tier-driven monitoring (up to every 1–3 months in red zones) and confirmation with 2 of 3 abnormal specimens; ADA aligns but allows simpler annual monitoring in stable patients with normal eGFR and UACR.21
  • Screening age. USPSTF (2021): adults 35–70 with overweight / obesity every 3 years. ADA: universal screening at age ≥35 (earlier with risk factors) regardless of BMI.31
  • BP target. ADA 2025: <130/80 with consideration of <120 systolic in high-risk patients; AACE / ACC-AHA align at <130/80 but do not universally endorse <120 for diabetes.1

  4. Samson SL et al. AACE Consensus Statement: Comprehensive Type 2 Diabetes Management Algorithm — 2023 Update. Endocr Pract. 2023. See bibliography.md → aace-dm-2023. ↩

  5. AGS. Guidelines for Improving the Care of Older Adults with Diabetes Mellitus: 2013 Update. J Am Geriatr Soc. 2013. See bibliography.md → ags-dm-2013. ↩↩↩↩↩

  6. CMS. Medicare DSMT (MLN909381) and MNT (NCD 180.1) coverage. See bibliography.md → cms-dsmt. ↩↩

Cataract

ICD-10: H25.x (age-related), H26.x (other / secondary) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 26.4% — rank 6 of 30 Category: Ophthalmic Primary associations consulted: AAO, USPSTF, NEI, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — —
Imaging — — — — — Optical biometry (axial length, keratometry, anterior chamber depth) — once preoperatively for IOL calculation
Physical exam — — — Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (age ≥65); annual if comorbid diabetes regardless of cataract status Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (age 55–64, asymptomatic) — every 1–3 yr Post-op exam day 1 (or within 24–48 hr); post-op week 1–2; post-op month 1
Functional / surveys — — — Best-corrected visual acuity (BCVA) + refraction, IOP, slit-lamp at each comprehensive exam — Functional impairment assessment (VF-14 or ADL-specific: reading, driving, TV, vocational / recreational tasks) for surgical-referral documentation

Summary recommendation

A patient with a diagnosed non-surgical cataract should receive a comprehensive dilated medical eye evaluation every 1–2 years if age ≥65 (every 1–3 years if 55–64), per AAO Preferred Practice Pattern and NEI guidance; comorbid diabetes mandates annual dilated exams.214 At each visit BCVA with refraction, IOP, and slit-lamp biomicroscopy of the lens, anterior segment, and dilated fundus are standard.1 Surgical referral is not driven by a fixed visual-acuity threshold but by patient-specific functional impairment — inability to perform ADLs (reading, driving, TV, work, recreation) despite tolerable refractive correction — and is supported by Medicare LCDs and the AAO Cataract in the Adult Eye PPP.51 Standard post-operative follow-up is a three-visit cadence: day 1 (or within 24–48 hours for low-risk cases), week 1–2, and month 1 — checking VA, IOP, wound integrity, IOL position, and inflammation. PCPs should re-refer when patients report new or worsening functional vision impairment, post-operative symptoms (pain, vision loss, redness, photophobia), or have lapsed the 1–2 year interval.1

Laboratory

No laboratory monitoring is recommended for cataract surveillance.

Imaging

  • Optical biometry (axial length, keratometry, anterior chamber depth) — once preoperatively for IOL power calculation. Documented IOL calculation must be signed and dated in the chart, within the timeframe required by the Medicare administrative contractor (commonly 3 months).15

Physical examination

  • Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (history, VA, external exam, pupils, motility, IOP, slit-lamp biomicroscopy, dilated lens / fundus exam) — every 1–2 years if age ≥65; every 1–3 years if 55–64 (asymptomatic, no risk factors); annual if comorbid diabetes regardless of cataract status.2
  • Slit-lamp biomicroscopy (cornea, anterior chamber, iris, lens, vitreous, macula, peripheral retina, optic nerve) — at preoperative evaluation and at each routine eye exam; required preoperatively to confirm cataract as the etiology of visual loss.1
  • Intraocular pressure (tonometry) — at every comprehensive eye exam and at each post-operative visit.21
  • Postoperative day 1 (or within 24–48 hr) — VA, IOP, slit-lamp, wound integrity (Seidel), IOL position, anterior-chamber inflammation. Day 1 for complicated / high-risk cases; within 48 hours acceptable for uncomplicated low-risk.1
  • Postoperative week 1–2 — routine recovery check.1
  • Postoperative month 1 — final refraction, BCVA, IOP, slit-lamp, fundus exam; expect stable refraction, no inflammation, normal IOP.1

Functional / surveys

  • Best-corrected visual acuity (BCVA) with refraction — at each comprehensive eye exam; required preoperatively (most MACs within 3 months of surgery).15
  • Functional impairment assessment (VF-14, VF-8R, or patient-specific ADL impact: reading, driving, TV, vocational / recreational tasks; glare and contrast complaints) — at the preoperative evaluation. Documentation must be patient-specific (not cloned).15

Notes

  • USPSTF vs. AAO disagreement on venue. USPSTF (2022, Grade I — insufficient evidence) addresses primary-care visual-acuity screening in asymptomatic older adults; AAO PPP and NEI recommend routine comprehensive eye exams by eye-care professionals. The disagreement is about venue, not the value of eye exams in older adults.324
  • CMS coverage policies vary by MAC. Most MACs reject a fixed visual-acuity cutoff for cataract surgery; one (First Coast) documents 20/40 as a threshold. AAO supports functional-impairment documentation rather than a numeric VA threshold.51
  • Post-operative cadence. AAO PPP supports a three-visit cadence (day 1 or 24–48 hr; week 1–2; month 1) for routine cases; complicated cases require day 1.1

Footnotes

  1. American Academy of Ophthalmology. Cataract in the Adult Eye Preferred Practice Pattern. Ophthalmology. 2022;129(1):P1-P126. See bibliography.md → aao-cat-2021. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. American Academy of Ophthalmology. Comprehensive Adult Medical Eye Evaluation Preferred Practice Pattern. 2020 (reaffirmed). See bibliography.md → aao-eye-eval-2020. ↩↩↩↩

  3. USPSTF. Screening for Impaired Visual Acuity in Older Adults: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2022;327(21):2123-2128 (Grade I). See bibliography.md → uspstf-vision-2022. ↩

  4. National Eye Institute. Cataracts. NIH. See bibliography.md → nei-cat. ↩↩

  5. CMS. Local Coverage Determinations for Cataract Surgery (L34413, L37027). See bibliography.md → cms-lcd-cataract. ↩↩↩↩↩

Ischemic Heart Disease

ICD-10: I20–I25 (chronic coronary disease spectrum; stable angina, prior MI, post-revascularization) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 21.8% — rank 7 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA (2023 CCD Guideline), USPSTF, CMS, NHLBI

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lipid panel during titration (4–12 wk post statin start / change) — Lipid panel (stable), HbA1c (if DM), influenza + COVID-19 vaccination review — —
Imaging — — — — — Stress imaging (SPECT / PET / CMR / stress echo), coronary CTA, echocardiogram, or invasive angiography — only with new / worsening symptoms; routine surveillance is Class 3 (No Benefit)
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, physical activity assessment — — — — —
Functional / surveys Adherence review (aspirin 75–100 mg or DAPT through indicated window; high-intensity statin; β-blocker if indicated; ACEi / ARB if indicated; SGLT2i / GLP-1 RA in eligible); tobacco use + cessation counseling — — PHQ-2 / PHQ-9 depression screen, ASCVD risk — Cardiac rehabilitation referral after MI, PCI, CABG, stable angina, valve repair / replacement, or transplant (up to 36 sessions over 36 weeks, CMS-covered)

Summary recommendation

For a primary-care patient with clinically stable chronic coronary disease (CCD), the 2023 ACC/AHA CCD Guideline frames surveillance around guideline-directed medical therapy, lifestyle support, and risk-factor monitoring rather than routine repeat cardiac imaging.1 Outpatient visits are individualized — commonly every 4–6 months and at least annually once stable — and at each visit clinicians should measure BP (target <130/80 mm Hg), weight and physical activity, assess tobacco use, screen for depression with PHQ-2 / PHQ-9, and verify adherence to aspirin 75–100 mg (or DAPT through the post-PCI / ACS window), high-intensity statin, ACEi / ARB if indicated, and SGLT2i / GLP-1 RA in eligible patients.1 A lipid panel is checked 4–12 weeks after any statin change and then every 3–12 months, targeting ≥50% LDL-C reduction; the 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline adds an LDL-C <55 mg/dL secondary-prevention goal, with escalation to ezetimibe, bempedoic acid, or a PCSK9 inhibitor when unmet.2 Cardiac rehabilitation should be referred after MI, PCI, CABG, or for stable angina — CMS covers up to 36 sessions over 36 weeks.3 Routine surveillance stress testing, coronary CTA, echocardiography, or angiography is not recommended in asymptomatic stable patients (Class 3); escalate to stress imaging or angiography only when symptoms or functional capacity change despite optimized medical therapy.1

Laboratory

  • Fasting lipid panel — 4–12 weeks after statin initiation or dose change, then every 3–12 months. Target ≥50% LDL-C reduction on high-intensity statin (30–49% on moderate-intensity); LDL-C <55 mg/dL for secondary prevention per 2026 Dyslipidemia Guideline.12
  • HbA1c (if diabetic) — per diabetes monitoring cadence. SGLT2i is Class I in CCD + HFrEF (LVEF ≤40%); GLP-1 RA / SGLT2i indicated for residual cardiometabolic risk.1
  • Annual influenza vaccination and current COVID-19 vaccination — review annually.1

Imaging

  • Stress imaging (SPECT, PET, CMR, stress echo), coronary CTA, or invasive angiography — as indicated only when symptoms or functional status change despite optimized medical therapy. Routine periodic surveillance imaging is NOT recommended (Class 3, No Benefit).1
  • Echocardiogram (LVEF reassessment) — as indicated by change in clinical status (new HF symptoms / signs); not routine. Identifies LVEF ≤40% for GDMT including SGLT2i.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every visit. Target <130/80 mm Hg.1
  • Weight / BMI and physical activity assessment — every visit. Routine aerobic activity supported; ~1,000 additional steps/day associated with ~10% mortality reduction.1

Functional / surveys

  • Adherence review — aspirin 75–100 mg daily (or DAPT through indicated post-PCI / ACS window); high-intensity statin; β-blocker only if indicated (post-MI <1 year, LVEF ≤50%, or other indication); ACEi / ARB if indicated; SGLT2i / GLP-1 RA in eligible patients.1
  • Tobacco use and cessation counseling — every visit. Behavioral + pharmacotherapy first-line; not e-cigarettes.1
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 → PHQ-9 if positive) — periodically.1
  • Cardiac rehabilitation referral — post-MI, post-PCI, post-CABG, stable angina, heart-valve repair / replacement, or transplant. CMS covers up to 36 sessions over 36 weeks (NCD 20.10).13
  • DAPT (post-ACS or post-PCI) — typically 12 months after ACS; shorter durations (1–3 months) acceptable if elevated bleeding risk per current guidance.1

Notes

  • Routine surveillance imaging is Class 3 (No Benefit). A significant shift from prior practice — the 2023 CCD Guideline removed routine periodic stress testing every 2–3 years from accepted surveillance.1
  • β-blocker duration post-MI. The 2023 CCD Guideline departed from long-standing tradition: long-term β-blocker beyond 1 year post-MI is not beneficial unless LVEF ≤50% or another indication is present (Class IIb against indefinite use).1
  • LDL secondary-prevention target. 2023 CCD emphasizes percent reduction (≥50%); 2026 ACC/AHA Dyslipidemia Guideline adds explicit numeric goal <55 mg/dL for very-high-risk ASCVD — US guidance now aligns more closely with ESC.12
  • DAPT duration. Standard post-ACS is 12 months; guideline supports shorter durations when bleeding risk is elevated.1
  • Depression screening cadence unspecified. CCD Guideline endorses screening without specifying an interval; USPSTF also endorses without defined interval.1

Footnotes

  1. Virani SS et al. 2023 AHA/ACC/ACCP/ASPC/NLA/PCNA Guideline for the Management of Patients With Chronic Coronary Disease. Circulation. 2023;148:e9-e119. See bibliography.md → accaha-ccd-2023. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. 2026 ACC/AHA/Multisociety Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia. Circulation. 2026. See bibliography.md → accaha-dyslip-2026. ↩↩↩

  3. CMS. National Coverage Determination 20.10: Cardiac Rehabilitation Programs. See bibliography.md → cms-cr-ncd. ↩↩

Anemia

ICD-10: D50.x (iron-deficiency), D51.x (B12 deficiency), D52.x (folate deficiency), D53.x (other nutritional), D63.x (anemia in chronic disease) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 21.7% — rank 8 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: KDIGO, ASH, AGA, AAFP, CDC, NIDDK, USPSTF

Anemia is a finding common to many underlying conditions; surveillance is subtype-specific. The sections below cover the dominant primary-care subtypes: iron deficiency anemia (IDA), anemia of chronic kidney disease, B12 / folate deficiency, and anemia in older adults / chronic disease.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — CBC + iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) q3 mo on ESA for CKD anemia; CBC q3 mo during first year of oral iron replacement CBC screening Hgb (CKD G4–G5 non-dialysis) CBC screening Hgb (CKD G3); follow-up CBC after iron repletion CBC every 5–10 yr in women of childbearing age (CDC) CBC + reticulocyte count, ferritin, B12, folate, eGFR, LDH / haptoglobin / bilirubin (workup); Hgb q2–4 wk on ESA initiation; CBC 1 mo after starting oral iron; reticulocyte 1 wk after B12 replacement; MMA / homocysteine 1 wk to 3 mo (B12 response); CBC at 8 wk (B12 replacement); GI evaluation (EGD + colonoscopy) for adult IDA in men and postmenopausal women
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam — — — — — Neurologic exam (vibration, proprioception, gait, cognition) at diagnosis and follow-up for B12 deficiency with neurologic findings
Functional / surveys Symptom assessment (fatigue, dyspnea, exercise tolerance, pica, restless legs) during active treatment — — — — Transfusion threshold assessment (restrictive Hgb 7–8 g/dL); avoid transfusion in CKD when possible to preserve transplant candidacy

Summary recommendation

Primary-care surveillance of known anemia is subtype-specific. In iron deficiency anemia on oral iron, check a CBC at ~4 weeks (expect ≥1 g/dL Hgb rise), then every 3 months for one year, continue iron approximately 3 months after Hgb normalizes, and verify repleted stores with a follow-up ferritin.23 Persistent or recurrent IDA in adult men or postmenopausal women warrants GI evaluation (EGD + colonoscopy).36 In anemia of CKD, KDIGO 2012 anchors monitoring: screen Hgb annually in CKD G3 and every 6 months in G4–G5 non-dialysis; once on an ESA or HIF-PHI, monitor Hgb every 2–4 weeks during initiation and every 3 months in maintenance, with iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) every 3 months on ESA, targeting Hgb 10–11.5 g/dL while avoiding both Hgb <9 and >11.5 g/dL.1 For B12 deficiency on replacement, confirm response with reticulocytosis at 1 week, MMA / homocysteine normalization at 1 week to 3 months, and CBC normalization by 8 weeks; serial neurologic exams track recovery, and routine repeat B12 testing is not recommended once replacement is established.4 Folate deficiency monitoring parallels B12 (CBC at 8 weeks). For older adults with anemia of chronic disease or unexplained anemia, periodic CBC, iron studies, B12, eGFR, and TSH reclassify subtype; hemolysis labs (LDH, haptoglobin, bilirubin, peripheral smear) are added when Hgb drops without bleeding.5

Laboratory — Iron Deficiency Anemia

  • CBC — at 1 month after starting oral iron (expect ≥1 g/dL Hgb rise); every 3 months for 1 year; then 12 months later.23
  • Serum ferritin — at end of iron therapy course and 6–8 weeks after IV iron infusion. Target ferritin ≥30 ng/mL (AGA suggests ≥50 ng/mL).35
  • Diagnostic ferritin threshold for iron deficiency — ≤30 ng/mL (AGA 2020; ASH draft 2025 explicitly recommends against the ≤15 threshold for menstruating people).73
  • GI evaluation (EGD + colonoscopy) — at IDA diagnosis in adult men and postmenopausal women; repeat if IDA recurs after repletion. ~62% of older adults have a GI source.365

Laboratory — Anemia of CKD

  • Hemoglobin (CBC) — at least annually for CKD G3; every 6 months for G4–G5 non-dialysis; every 3 months for G5D. Anemia defined as Hgb <13.0 g/dL (men), <12.0 g/dL (women).1
  • Hemoglobin (on ESA or HIF-PHI) — every 2–4 weeks during initiation / dose change; every 3 months during maintenance.1
  • Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — every 3 months during ESA therapy; more often when starting / increasing ESA, after IV iron course, or with blood loss. Trial IV iron if TSAT ≤30% and ferritin ≤500 ng/mL (KDIGO ceiling).1
  • Hgb target — initiate ESA when Hgb 9.0–10.0 g/dL; do not maintain >11.5 g/dL; avoid Hgb falling <9.0 g/dL.1
  • Initial anemia workup — CBC with reticulocyte count, serum ferritin, B12, folate to characterize anemia before ESA decisions.1

Laboratory — B12 / Folate Deficiency

  • Reticulocyte count — 1 week after B12 replacement; expect reticulocytosis.4
  • Methylmalonic acid (MMA) and / or homocysteine — 1 week post-replacement to confirm response; or 3–6 months after starting oral therapy (borderline B12 200–350 pg/mL).45
  • CBC with MCV — 8 weeks after starting B12 replacement; expect normalization of Hgb, MCV, WBC, platelets.4
  • Serum B12 — not routinely retested after replacement initiated (British Society of Haematology, cited by AAFP); may repeat at ≥2 months for adherence / response.4

Laboratory — Hemolysis Workup (As Indicated)

  • LDH, indirect bilirubin, haptoglobin, peripheral smear — when hemolysis suspected (Hgb drop without bleeding, elevated reticulocytes).5

Laboratory — Screening / Surveillance (General)

  • CBC screening — every 5–10 years through childbearing years at routine exam (CDC 1998 recommendation for nonpregnant women).6
  • eGFR / serum creatinine — at anemia evaluation and at CKD-stage-appropriate intervals to identify CKD as cause.18

Physical examination

  • Neurologic exam (vibration, proprioception, gait, cognition) — at diagnosis and follow-up visits during 6 weeks–3 months of replacement for B12 deficiency with neurologic findings. Some deficits are irreversible.4

Functional / surveys

  • Symptom assessment (fatigue, dyspnea, exercise tolerance, pica, restless legs) — every visit during active treatment.12
  • Transfusion threshold — restrictive Hgb 7–8 g/dL for symptomatic anemia; avoid transfusion in CKD when possible to preserve transplant candidacy.15

Notes

  • Ferritin diagnostic cutoff for iron deficiency. WHO / older CDC used ≤15 ng/mL; AAFP cites <19 in older adults; AGA 2020 and ASH 2025 draft suggest ≤30 ng/mL — ASH explicitly recommends against the ≤15 threshold for menstruating people.37
  • Ferritin target after IV iron in CKD. KDIGO 2012 allows trial up to ferritin ≤500 ng/mL with TSAT ≤30%, higher than non-CKD targets. KDOQI 2013 US Commentary cautioned against routinely exceeding 500.1
  • Hgb target in CKD. KDIGO 2012 caps at 11.5 g/dL; the draft KDIGO 2025/2026 update moves toward more individualized targets and incorporates HIF-PHIs.1
  • Repeat B12 testing. British Society of Haematology recommends against routine retesting after replacement begins; other sources suggest retesting MMA / homocysteine at 3–6 months in malabsorption.4
  • USPSTF has no current recommendation for or against IDA screening in nonpregnant adults; the 2024 statement on pregnancy is an "I" (insufficient evidence).9

Footnotes

  2. Short MW, Domagalski JE. Iron Deficiency Anemia: Evaluation and Management. Am Fam Physician. 2013;87(2):98-104. See bibliography.md → aafp-ida-2013. ↩↩↩

Hypothyroidism

ICD-10: E03.x (primary hypothyroidism; includes congenital, autoimmune, post-treatment) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 21.3% — rank 9 of 30 Category: Metabolic Primary associations consulted: ATA, AACE, USPSTF, NIDDK

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — TSH every 4–8 wk during titration / dose change / formulation switch TSH at 6 mo after stabilization (then transition to annual); TSH q6–12 mo while observing subclinical hypothyroidism TSH (stable on levothyroxine); fasting lipid panel (untreated subclinical hypothyroidism) — Free T4 (dose adjustment, suspected non-adherence, central hypothyroidism, pregnancy); TPO antibodies once at subclinical-hypothyroidism workup; TSH q4 wk through ~20 wk gestation, then once between 26–32 wk; confirmatory repeat TSH at 2–3 mo for newly abnormal value before initiating therapy
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam Weight, heart rate, blood pressure (each visit during titration); at least annually once stable — — — — —
Functional / surveys Symptom assessment — residual hypothyroidism + signs of overtreatment (tachycardia, weight loss, tremor) — — — — —

Summary recommendation

An adult with stable primary hypothyroidism on levothyroxine should have TSH checked 4–8 weeks after any dose change or formulation switch, again at approximately 6 months once stable, and annually thereafter — or sooner with new symptoms, pregnancy, weight change >10%, or addition of medications that affect absorption (PPI, calcium, iron, estrogen).1 The TSH target is 0.4–4.0 mIU/L for most adults, with an age-adjusted upward shift to 4–6 mIU/L in patients aged 70–80 and tolerance up to ~7 mIU/L in those ≥80 to avoid iatrogenic thyrotoxicosis, atrial fibrillation, and fracture.12 Free T4 is not required at every visit but is checked during titration, in suspected non-adherence, in central hypothyroidism, and routinely in pregnancy. Pregnancy demands immediate dose increase of ~20–30% at confirmation, TSH every 4 weeks through midgestation with a target below ~4.0 mIU/L (ideally between the lower reference limit and 2.5 mIU/L when treated), and reversion to pre-pregnancy dose postpartum with rechecking at 6 weeks.3 For untreated subclinical hypothyroidism, repeat TSH (with free T4 and TPO antibody) at 2–3 months to confirm persistence, then monitor every 6–12 months; check a fasting lipid panel and weigh treatment more strongly when TSH is >10 mIU/L, TPO Ab positive, or cardiovascular risk elevated.2 Each visit should include weight, heart rate, blood pressure, and a symptom review for both residual hypothyroidism and signs of overtreatment.

Laboratory

  • Serum TSH (titration) — every 4–8 weeks during dose change, formulation switch, or initial titration.1
  • Serum TSH (stable maintenance) — at 6 months after stabilization, then annually. Target 0.4–4.0 mIU/L general adult; 4–6 mIU/L acceptable age 70–80; up to ~7 mIU/L acceptable for those ≥80.12
  • Serum TSH (pregnancy) — every 4 weeks through ~20 weeks gestation, then at least once between 26 and 32 weeks. Target: trimester-specific reference or below ~4.0 mIU/L if no local range; aim 0.4–2.5 mIU/L when on replacement.3
  • Serum TSH (subclinical hypothyroidism, observed) — every 6–12 months while observed; sooner if symptoms develop.2
  • Confirmatory repeat TSH — 2–3 months after any newly abnormal TSH before initiating therapy.52
  • Free T4 — as indicated; routinely co-measured during dose titration, in suspected non-adherence, central hypothyroidism, and pregnancy. Not required at every follow-up in stable primary hypothyroidism.13
  • Free or total T3 — not routinely recommended; only when combination LT4 / LT3 therapy is used or clinical concern.14
  • TPO antibodies (anti-thyroid peroxidase) — once at diagnosis of subclinical hypothyroidism or unexplained TSH elevation; predicts progression. Pregnancy workup if TSH >2.5 mIU/L.23
  • Fasting lipid panel — at baseline in untreated subclinical hypothyroidism and after treatment normalization; treatment of subclinical hypothyroidism may improve lipids.2

Imaging

No routine imaging is recommended for surveillance of treated primary hypothyroidism.

Physical examination

  • Weight, heart rate, blood pressure — each visit during dose titration; at least annually once stable. Watch for iatrogenic thyrotoxicosis (tachycardia, weight loss, tremor).1

Functional / surveys

  • Symptom assessment (fatigue, cold intolerance, constipation, mood, cognition; and hyperthyroid symptoms suggesting overtreatment) — every visit.12

Notes

  • USPSTF vs. specialty societies on screening. USPSTF 2015 finds insufficient evidence (Grade I) to screen nonpregnant asymptomatic adults; AACE / ATA 2012 endorses aggressive case-finding in higher-risk adults (pregnancy planning, age >60, autoimmune disease, prior neck irradiation).52
  • Subclinical hypothyroidism treatment threshold. AACE / ATA 2012 supports considering treatment for TSH 4.5–10 mIU/L when symptomatic, TPO Ab positive, or with CV disease. TRUST trial and ETA-influenced commentary argue against treating older adults with TSH ≤10 mIU/L.2
  • Combination LT4 / LT3. ATA 2014 recommends against routine use (strong). The 2021 ATA / BTA / ETA Joint Consensus softened this, allowing a trial in selected symptomatic patients with biochemical euthyroidism, while reaffirming LT4 monotherapy as standard.14
  • TSH upper limit by age. ATA endorses an age-shifted target in older adults (4–6 mIU/L at 70–80; up to ~7 at ≥80). Some laboratory references still apply a uniform 4.5 mIU/L cutoff.1
  • Pregnancy TSH upper limit. 2017 ATA revision raised the trimester-1 upper limit from 2.5 to ~4.0 mIU/L when no local trimester-specific range exists, departing from the prior 2011 ATA position.3

Footnotes

  2. Garber JR et al. Clinical Practice Guidelines for Hypothyroidism in Adults — AACE / ATA. Endocr Pract. 2012;18(6):988-1028. See bibliography.md → aace-ata-hypo-2012. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. Alexander EK et al. 2017 ATA Guidelines for Diagnosis and Management of Thyroid Disease During Pregnancy and Postpartum. Thyroid. 2017;27(3):315-389. See bibliography.md → ata-preg-2017. ↩↩↩↩↩

  4. Jonklaas J et al. Evidence-Based Use of Levothyroxine / Liothyronine Combinations — ATA / BTA / ETA Consensus. Thyroid. 2021;31(2):156-182. See bibliography.md → ata-bta-eta-combo-2021. ↩↩

Depression / Bipolar / Mood Disorders

ICD-10: F32.x (MDD single episode), F33.x (MDD recurrent), F31.x (bipolar disorder), F34.x (persistent mood) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 19.7% — rank 10 of 30 Category: Mental Health Primary associations consulted: USPSTF, APA, VA/DoD, AGS, CMS, SAMHSA, ADA/APA Consensus

CCW groups depression, bipolar disorder, and other mood disorders into a single category. Depression is the dominant component; bipolar-specific surveillance and medication-monitoring sections are flagged below.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lithium level q3–6 mo (stable); valproate or carbamazepine level + CBC + LFTs q3–6 mo (stable); weight on SGA quarterly after first 12 wk Lithium Cr + TSH q6–12 mo (stable); CBC + LFTs (valproate / carbamazepine stable) BP + fasting glucose + lipids (SGA users, annually after the 12-wk visit) — Lithium level twice weekly during initiation, within 5–7 d of dose change; carbamazepine CBC + LFTs q2–4 wk first 2 mo; weight on SGA at baseline / 4 / 8 / 12 wk
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam Weight / BMI (SGA users — baseline, 4, 8, 12 wk, then quarterly); BP (SGA users) AIMS q6 mo (q3 mo high-risk) on antipsychotic therapy — BP + AIMS annually (SGA stable) — —
Functional / surveys Adherence + adverse-effect review; suicide-risk reassessment (C-SSRS "Since Last Visit" or PHQ-9 item 9 follow-up) during active treatment PHQ-9 monthly during acute treatment; every 2–4 wk in continuation; periodically in maintenance / recovery; GDS-15 or PHQ-9 in older adults; PHQ-9 / GDS q6 mo in nursing-home residents (2–4 wk post-admission, then q6 mo) — PHQ-2 / PHQ-9 screening per USPSTF 2023 for previously unscreened adults — MDQ at initial depression workup (rule out bipolar); CoCM / BHI registry tracking

Summary recommendation

Primary-care monitoring of diagnosed depression follows a measurement-based-care framework: administer the PHQ-9 at baseline and at least monthly during acute treatment (weekly to biweekly in many systems), every 2–4 weeks during continuation, and periodically through maintenance, targeting response (≥50% PHQ-9 reduction) and remission (PHQ-9 ≤4).12 Suicide risk is reassessed at every visit during active treatment using PHQ-9 item 9 plus a structured tool such as the Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale "Since Last Visit," with more frequent contact in the first 4–12 weeks after starting or changing an antidepressant.16 Older adults are tracked with the GDS-15 or PHQ-9; nursing-home residents are reassessed 2–4 weeks after admission and every 6 months.7 Medication-specific monitoring varies by agent: SSRIs / SNRIs need baseline metabolic and tolerability review with reassessment at 2 weeks and at dose changes; lithium requires 12-hour trough levels (target 0.6–1.2 mEq/L; 0.4–0.8 in older adults) with creatinine and TSH every 6–12 months in stable patients (more often early); valproate and carbamazepine require periodic drug levels, CBC, and LFTs; second-generation antipsychotics used for bipolar or augmentation require ADA / APA metabolic monitoring of weight (baseline, 4 / 8 / 12 weeks, then quarterly), BP and fasting glucose and lipids (baseline, 12 weeks, then annually), and AIMS every 6 months.35 The MDQ should be administered when a new depressive episode is being evaluated to avoid missing bipolar disorder.3 Practices using the CMS Collaborative Care Model (CoCM, CPT 99492–99494) or Behavioral Health Integration (CPT 99484) embed all of this through a care-manager registry with monthly psychiatric caseload review.8

Laboratory — Mood-Stabilizer / Antipsychotic Monitoring

  • Serum lithium level (12-hour trough) — twice weekly until stable; every 3–6 months in stable maintenance; within 5–7 days of any dose change. Maintenance 0.6–1.2 mEq/L (consensus 0.6–0.8 mEq/L; 0.4–0.6 in older adults).3
  • Serum creatinine / eGFR (lithium) — baseline; every 2–3 months for first 6 months; every 6–12 months stable. Investigate rises >25%.3
  • TSH (lithium) — baseline; once or twice in first 6 months; every 6–12 months.3
  • Valproate level, CBC, LFTs — VPA level after titration and with dose changes; CBC and LFTs baseline, then every 6–12 months. Target VPA 50–125 mcg/mL.3
  • Carbamazepine level, CBC with platelets, LFTs, sodium — level with dose changes and every 6–12 months; CBC and LFTs every 2–4 weeks for first 2 months, then every 3–6 months. Target 4–12 mcg/mL.3
  • Fasting plasma glucose or HbA1c (SGA users) — baseline, 12 weeks, then annually. FPG target <100 mg/dL.5
  • Fasting lipid panel (SGA users) — baseline, 12 weeks, then every 5 years (every 2 years if abnormal). Many systems and APA bipolar guidance now favor annual.53

Physical examination

  • Weight / BMI (SGA users) — baseline, 4, 8, 12 weeks, then quarterly. Weight gain ≥5% triggers intervention.5
  • Blood pressure (SGA users) — baseline, 12 weeks, then annually (more often if elevated). Target <130/80 mm Hg.5
  • Abnormal Involuntary Movement Scale (AIMS) — baseline, then every 6 months (every 3 months for high-risk patients) on antipsychotic therapy. Detect tardive dyskinesia early.3

Functional / surveys

  • PHQ-9 (Patient Health Questionnaire-9) — baseline; at least monthly during acute treatment or any treatment change; every 2–4 weeks in continuation; periodically in maintenance / recovery. Response = ≥50% reduction from baseline; remission = total score ≤4 (some sources use <5).1
  • PHQ-9 or PHQ-2 (screening, USPSTF 2023) — at least once for previously unscreened adults including pregnant / postpartum and ≥65; clinician judgment for re-screening intervals. PHQ-2 ≥2 triggers PHQ-9; PHQ-9 ≥10 indicates probable MDD.4
  • Geriatric Depression Scale (GDS-15) or PHQ-9 — baseline and to monitor treatment response in adults ≥65; every 6 months in nursing-home residents (2–4 wk post-admission, then q6 mo). GDS-15 ≥5 positive.7
  • Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS "Since Last Visit") — at each visit during active treatment; whenever PHQ-9 item 9 is positive or new risk factors emerge.16
  • Mood Disorder Questionnaire (MDQ) — once at initial depression workup to rule out bipolar; repeat if hypomanic / manic features emerge. MDQ ≥7 with functional impairment suggests bipolar spectrum.3
  • Adherence and adverse-effect review — every visit during the acute phase; at every PHQ-9 administration.1
  • Collaborative Care Model registry / caseload review — continuous; care manager contacts and stepped review at least monthly until improvement, then maintenance contacts. Covered as CMS CoCM (CPT 99492 / 99493 / 99494) or general BHI (99484).8

Notes

  • Screening vs. ongoing monitoring. USPSTF 2023 addresses only undiagnosed adults and states evidence is insufficient on optimal re-screening intervals; APA and VA/DoD pick up at diagnosis and prescribe systematic measurement-based care. No federal recommendation exists on PHQ-9 cadence in already-diagnosed patients.41
  • Universal suicide-risk screening. USPSTF 2023 issued an "I" (insufficient) statement; VA/DoD, APA, and Joint Commission NPSG 15.01.01 require structured suicide-risk assessment at every visit for patients in active depression treatment.41
  • Lithium monitoring interval. 2002 / 2005 APA bipolar guideline suggests serum lithium every 6–12 months in stable patients; more recent ISBD / IGSLI consensus and most US systems favor every 3–6 months. Therapeutic target has tightened to 0.6–0.8 mEq/L (0.4–0.6 in older adults).3
  • SGA metabolic monitoring intensity. ADA / APA 2004 calls for fasting lipids every 5 years; subsequent guidance (APA bipolar, NCQA HEDIS SMD / SMC) effectively requires annual — a de facto stricter standard.5
  • PHQ-9 remission threshold. VA/DoD and HEDIS DRR-E define remission as PHQ-9 <5; some APA materials and Kroenke validation use ≤4. Clinically negligible but affects quality-measure reporting.1

Footnotes

  2. APA. Practice Guideline for the Treatment of Patients with Major Depressive Disorder, 3rd ed. 2010. See bibliography.md → apa-mdd-2010. ↩

  3. APA. Practice Guideline for the Treatment of Patients with Bipolar Disorder, 2nd ed. 2002 (Guideline Watch 2005). See bibliography.md → apa-bipolar-2002. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  4. USPSTF. Screening for Depression and Suicide Risk in Adults: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2023;329(23):2057-2067. See bibliography.md → uspstf-depr-2023. ↩↩↩

  5. ADA / APA / AACE / NAASO. Consensus Development Conference on Antipsychotic Drugs and Obesity and Diabetes. Diabetes Care. 2004;27(2):596-601. See bibliography.md → ada-apa-metab-2004. ↩↩↩↩↩↩

Chronic Kidney Disease

ICD-10: N18.x (CKD stages 1–5), N19 (unspecified renal failure) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 18.7% — rank 11 of 30 Category: Renal Primary associations consulted: KDIGO, NKF KDOQI, USPSTF (insufficient evidence), CMS, ASN, NIDDK

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs BMP (Na, K, HCO3, BUN, Cr / eGFR) on RAAS / SGLT2i / MRA or eGFR <30 Cr / eGFR + UACR for G4 (≈3×/yr), G3b A3, G5 (≈4×/yr); Ca / PO4 / PTH for G4 (q3–6 mo) and G5 (q1–3 mo) Cr / eGFR + UACR for G3a A2, G3b A1–A2; Hgb (CBC) for G4–G5 non-dialysis; Ca / PO4 / PTH for G3a–G3b Cr / eGFR + UACR for G1–G2 A1–A2 and G3a A1; Hgb (CBC) for G3; HbA1c ≥2×/yr if diabetic; vaccination review (pneumococcal, influenza, HBV, COVID-19) — Cystatin C with eGFRcr-cys when clinical decision hinges on accuracy; urinalysis at diagnosis or change in status; lipid panel once at diagnosis (no routine repeat); K + Cr at 2–4 wk after starting / titrating ACEi / ARB / SGLT2i / MRA; iron studies if anemia evaluated; 25-OH vitamin D at baseline; alkaline phosphatase q12 mo G4–G5
Imaging — — — — — —
Physical exam Standardized office BP (5-min rest, validated cuff, multi-step protocol, average of readings); target SBP <120 mm Hg — — — — —
Functional / surveys Medication reconciliation (NSAID avoidance, contrast safety, dose adjustment); lifestyle counseling (Na <2 g/d, ≥150 min/wk activity, plant-rich diet, smoking cessation, weight) — — KFRE 2- and 5-year risk recalculation — Nephrology referral when 5-yr KFRE ≥3–5%, 2-yr ≥10%, eGFR <30, persistent A3 albuminuria, refractory hypertension / hyperkalemia / anemia / CKD-MBD, or unexplained decline; transplant / dialysis-access workup

Summary recommendation

Primary-care surveillance of established CKD is driven by the KDIGO 2024 heat map: combine current eGFR (race-free CKD-EPI 2021 creatinine equation, adding cystatin C when clinical decisions hinge on accuracy) with UACR, then plot on the G1–G5 / A1–A3 grid.1 Low-risk patients (G1–G2 A1) need annual eGFR + UACR; moderate-risk (e.g., G1–G2 A2, G3a A1) twice yearly; high-risk (G3a A2, G3b A1–A2, any G1–G2 A3) two to three times yearly; very-high-risk (G3b A3, all G4, all G5) three to four times yearly, with BP, electrolytes, and bicarbonate at each draw.1 At G3a add CKD-MBD labs — calcium, phosphate, PTH, alkaline phosphatase every 6–12 months, tightening to every 3–6 months at G4 and every 1–3 months at G5.4 Screen hemoglobin annually in G3 and twice yearly in G4–G5 non-dialysis (KDIGO 2012 Anemia in CKD).5 Check a lipid panel once at diagnosis for risk stratification, then "fire-and-forget" — start a statin (or statin / ezetimibe) in all adults ≥50 with eGFR <60 without routinely retesting LDL.6 In diabetics, HbA1c twice yearly (quarterly if unstable), individualized 6.5–8.0%, with SGLT2i (eGFR ≥20), GLP-1 RA for residual cardiometabolic risk, and non-steroidal MRA (finerenone) for persistent albuminuria with serum K <5.3 Target standardized office SBP <120 mm Hg.2 ACEi or ARB at maximum tolerated dose in A2–A3, continued even when eGFR falls <30 unless intolerable. Recheck K and creatinine 2–4 weeks after starting / titrating RAAS, SGLT2i, or MRA. Refer to nephrology when 5-year KFRE ≥3–5%, 2-year ≥10%, eGFR <30, persistent A3 albuminuria, or refractory complications.1

Laboratory

  • Serum creatinine with eGFR (CKD-EPI 2021 race-free) — risk-tier cadence per KDIGO 2024 heat map: G1A2 annual; G1A3 ~3×/yr; G3aA1 annual; G3aA2 2×/yr; G3bA1–A2 2×/yr; G3bA3 3×/yr; G4 any A ~3×/yr; G5 ~4×/yr. A change >20% triggers evaluation.1
  • Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR), spot, first-morning preferred — same risk-tier cadence as eGFR. A1 <30 mg/g, A2 30–300, A3 >300. Doubling on repeat exceeds variability and warrants evaluation.1
  • Cystatin C with eGFRcr-cys — as indicated when creatinine-based estimate affects clinical decisions (drug dosing, referral, transplant eligibility).1
  • Serum electrolytes / BMP (Na, K, HCO3, BUN) — with each eGFR draw; more often on ACEi / ARB / SGLT2i / MRA or eGFR <30. Target HCO3 ≥22 mmol/L; K within normal range.1
  • Serum calcium and phosphate — G3a–G3b every 6–12 months; G4 every 3–6 months; G5 non-dialysis every 1–3 months.4
  • Intact PTH — G3a–G3b based on baseline and progression; G4 every 6–12 months; G5 non-dialysis every 3–6 months. No single non-dialysis CKD target; treat persistently rising or markedly elevated values.4
  • Alkaline phosphatase — every 12 months in G4–G5; more often if PTH elevated.4
  • 25-hydroxyvitamin D — at baseline; repeat based on level and intervention.4
  • Hemoglobin (CBC) — G3 at least annually; G4–G5 non-dialysis at least twice yearly; G5D every 3 months. Anemia defined as Hgb <13.0 g/dL (men), <12.0 g/dL (women).5
  • Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — at anemia diagnosis and every 3 months during ESA therapy.5
  • Lipid panel — once at CKD diagnosis to assess 10-year ASCVD risk; routine follow-up lipid testing NOT recommended ("fire-and-forget"). Statin (or statin / ezetimibe) in adults ≥50 with eGFR <60 (G3a–G5 non-dialysis / non-transplant); statin in adults 18–49 with CAD / DM / prior CVA / 10-yr CHD risk >10%.6
  • HbA1c (if diabetic) — at least twice yearly meeting target and stable; quarterly if therapy changing or not at target. Individualized 6.5–8.0%; less reliable as eGFR <30.3
  • K + Cr at 2–4 weeks — after starting / titrating ACEi / ARB / SGLT2i / MRA. Continue ACEi / ARB even when eGFR falls <30 unless intolerable; tolerate creatinine rise up to 30% and initial eGFR dip up to 30% on SGLT2i.12
  • Urinalysis (dipstick + microscopy as indicated) — at diagnosis and as indicated for change in status, hematuria evaluation.1

Imaging

No routine imaging is recommended for surveillance.1

Physical examination

  • Standardized office blood pressure — every visit (5-min rest, validated cuff, multi-step protocol, average of readings). Target SBP <120 mm Hg when tolerated (KDIGO 2021).2

Functional / surveys

  • Kidney failure risk equation (KFRE), 2- and 5-year — annually for G3–G5; before referral decisions. Refer to nephrology when 5-yr KFRE ≥3–5% or 2-yr ≥10%.1
  • Medication reconciliation (NSAID avoidance, contrast safety, dose adjustment) — each visit.1
  • Lifestyle counseling (sodium <2 g/day, physical activity ≥150 min/wk, plant-rich diet, smoking cessation, weight) — each visit.12
  • Vaccination status (pneumococcal, influenza, hepatitis B, COVID-19) — annually review. HBV serology if planning dialysis or transplant.1

Notes

  • BP target disagreement. KDIGO 2021 / 2024 endorse standardized-office SBP <120 mm Hg (Grade 2B). ACC/AHA 2017 sets <130/80; ESC / ESH allows 130–139 systolic; NICE 2019 keeps <140/90. The KDIGO target is contingent on SPRINT-style measurement technique.2
  • Race-free eGFR uptake. KDIGO 2024 fully endorses the 2021 CKD-EPI race-free creatinine equation and broader cystatin C use; uptake in US labs has been uneven.1
  • Monitoring cadence. KDIGO 2024's risk-grid cadence is a practice point, not a graded recommendation; KDOQI 2024 US commentary supports it but notes lack of RCT data.1
  • Lipid follow-up paradigm difference. KDIGO 2013 / 2024 "fire-and-forget" omits routine LDL retesting; ACC/AHA endorses periodic monitoring for adherence and titration — different paradigms in the same patient.67
  • Hgb target in CKD anemia. KDIGO 2012 caps ESA-treated Hgb at 11.5 g/dL; the draft KDIGO 2025 / 2026 update adds individualization and HIF-PHI considerations.5
  • USPSTF vs. specialty. USPSTF maintains an "I" statement for asymptomatic-adult CKD screening; KDIGO and NKF recommend opportunistic case-finding in diabetes, hypertension, age >60, CVD, and family history.1
