Glaucoma

ICD-10: H40.x (glaucoma — POAG, NTG, angle-closure, suspect) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 15.0% — rank 12 of 30 Category: Ophthalmic Primary associations consulted: AAO, USPSTF, NEI, Glaucoma Research Foundation

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — —
Imaging — — OCT of peripapillary RNFL / optic nerve head / macular GCC (every 6–12 mo; complementary to visual field) OCT (stable, annual) — Stereo disc photography or imaging at baseline + as indicated
Physical exam Tonometry (IOP); medication adherence + ocular surface assessment Comprehensive eye exam (every 1–3 mo if progressing despite control; every 1–2 mo if progressing and uncontrolled) Comprehensive eye exam (every 3–6 mo if recently controlled) Comprehensive eye exam (every 6–12 mo if stable target IOP, no progression >6 mo); gonioscopy (every 1–5 yr or with change) — Central corneal thickness (pachymetry) at baseline; post-LPI / post-surgical follow-up
Functional / surveys — — Automated perimetry (Humphrey 24-2 or 30-2; every 6–12 mo); 10-2 for advanced disease Visual field (stable) — Up to 2×/year visual field in first 2 years post-diagnosis (≥6 tests in 2 yr to establish progression rate); target IOP setting + adjustment

Summary recommendation

USPSTF (2022, Grade I) finds insufficient evidence to recommend for or against routine primary-care screening for glaucoma in asymptomatic adults — but does not say "don't refer."3 NEI and AAO recommend a baseline comprehensive dilated eye exam at age 40 for all adults, and every 1–2 years for higher-risk groups (Black adults ≥40, all adults ≥60, Hispanic / Latino ≥60, family history of glaucoma, diabetes); PCPs refer these populations on that cadence and send any patient with elevated IOP, suspicious cup-to-disc ratio, or family history for a glaucoma-specific evaluation.41 Once a patient carries a diagnosis of POAG, NTG, or angle-closure glaucoma, ophthalmology owns surveillance per the AAO POAG PPP 2025 follow-up grid: every 6–12 months if target IOP achieved and stable >6 months; every 3–6 months if recently controlled; every 1–3 months if progressing despite control; and every 1–2 months if progressing and uncontrolled.1 Target IOP is individualized at 20–30% below untreated baseline, with stricter targets for advanced disease (also reflected in MIPS Measure #141, which requires 20% IOP reduction or documented plan of care).15 The PCP's continuing role is reinforcing drop adherence, reviewing systemic effects of topical β-blockers (bradycardia, bronchospasm) and prostaglandin analogs, flagging missed ophthalmology visits, and recognizing red flags — acute eye pain with halos / nausea (acute angle closure, emergent referral), rapid vision loss, or new field defect.

Laboratory

No laboratory monitoring is recommended for glaucoma surveillance.

Imaging

  • OCT of peripapillary RNFL, optic nerve head, and macular ganglion cell complex — at baseline and approximately every 6–12 months; complementary to visual field for progression detection (structural change often precedes detectable functional change in early disease).1
  • Stereo disc photography or imaging — at baseline and as indicated to document optic nerve appearance.1

Physical examination

  • Comprehensive medical eye evaluation (visual acuity, slit-lamp biomicroscopy, IOP, gonioscopy, dilated optic nerve / fundus exam) — cadence by control / progression status: every 6–12 mo if target IOP achieved and stable >6 mo; every 3–6 mo if recently controlled (≤6 mo); every 1–3 mo if progressing despite IOP control; every 1–2 mo if progressing and target IOP not achieved.1
  • Tonometry (Goldmann applanation preferred) — every visit. Target 20–30% reduction below untreated baseline (often <18 mm Hg moderate, <15 mm Hg advanced).1
  • Gonioscopy — at initial evaluation; repeat periodically (every 1–5 years, or when angle-closure component suspected, anterior chamber shallowing, or unexplained IOP change).12
  • Central corneal thickness (pachymetry) — at baseline; repeat after corneal surgery or unexplained IOP change.1
  • Dilated optic nerve / stereo disc evaluation — at each follow-up; document neuroretinal rim, cup-to-disc ratio, RNFL.1
  • Post–laser peripheral iridotomy (LPI) follow-up — within weeks after LPI, then long-term periodic surveillance every 6–12 months for progressive PAS and glaucomatous optic neuropathy.2
  • Post-surgical follow-up (trabeculectomy / tube shunt / MIGS) — early post-op per surgeon; long-term every 3–6 months minimum.1
  • Medication adherence and ocular surface assessment — every visit.1

Functional / surveys

  • Automated standard automated perimetry / visual field testing (Humphrey 24-2 or 30-2; 10-2 for advanced disease) — at diagnosis baseline; ≥6 tests in first 2 years to establish progression rate; then every 6–12 months; up to 2×/year for progressing eyes.1
  • Target IOP setting and documentation — at diagnosis; reassessed at each visit; adjust downward if progression occurs. MIPS Measure #141 requires 20% IOP reduction or documented plan of care.15
  • Population-level eye-exam cadence (primary-care referral) — baseline at age 40; every 1–2 years for high-risk groups (Black ≥40, all ≥60, Hispanic / Latino ≥60, family history, diabetes).41

Notes

  • USPSTF vs. AAO / NEI on venue. USPSTF (2022, I-statement) declines to endorse population screening in primary care; AAO and NEI recommend periodic comprehensive eye exams by an eye-care professional particularly for high-risk adults. The disagreement is about venue, not whether high-risk patients benefit from evaluation.34
  • OCT vs. visual field as progression metric. AAO POAG PPP 2025 calls them complementary; OCT often precedes functional change in early disease, while visual field is more informative in moderate–advanced disease where OCT "floors out."1
  • Gonioscopy cadence. PPP recommends gonioscopy at baseline and periodically without a fixed interval; real-world practice varies (every 1–5 years).1
  • Target IOP magnitude. AAO recommends 20–30% reduction below baseline; OHTS / EMGT data support ≥20% threshold; MIPS Measure #141 uses 20%. Advanced disease often requires >30% reduction, but no single universal target exists.15

  2. AAO. Primary Angle-Closure Disease Preferred Practice Pattern. 2025. See bibliography.md → aao-angle-2025. ↩↩

  3. USPSTF. Screening for Primary Open-Angle Glaucoma: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2022;327(20):1992-1997. See bibliography.md → uspstf-glaucoma-2022. ↩↩

  4. National Eye Institute. Statement on Detection of Glaucoma and Adult Vision Screening. See bibliography.md → nei-glaucoma. ↩↩↩

  5. CMS / MIPS Quality Measure #141. Primary Open-Angle Glaucoma: Reduction of IOP by 20% or Documentation of Plan of Care. 2025. See bibliography.md → mips-141. ↩↩↩

Atrial Fibrillation and Flutter

ICD-10: I48.0–I48.92 (paroxysmal, persistent, chronic, atypical, typical flutter) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 14.1% — rank 13 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: ACC/AHA/ACCP/HRS, USPSTF, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — INR on warfarin (at least monthly once stable; every 3–6 mo as a floor); BMP / Cr / eGFR / hepatic on DOAC at lower CrCl (≈every CrCl/10 months), age ≥75, frailty — Cr / eGFR (DOAC stable); HbA1c (if DM); lipid panel; CBC (Hgb) for occult bleeding — INR weekly during initiation / change / interacting drug or illness; Cr / hepatic / CBC for symptoms or bleeding
Imaging ECG / rhythm strip if symptom or rhythm change — — — — TTE at diagnosis; repeat with clinical change / new murmur / pre-procedure; TEE / cardiac CT pre-cardioversion (if <3 wk OAC) and pre-ablation / pre-LAAO; ambulatory rhythm monitoring (Holter / patch / event / ILR) post-ablation, symptom-driven
Physical exam BP, HR + rhythm assessment, weight / BMI — — — — —
Functional / surveys Symptoms (EHRA / CCS-SAF or palpitations / dyspnea / fatigue / exercise tolerance); physical-activity counseling; alcohol; tobacco — — CHA2DS2-VASc reassessment; HAS-BLED reassessment for modifiable bleeding risk; OSA screen (annual at minimum); CPAP adherence — LAAO (Watchman) candidacy reassessment when OAC contraindicated or major bleed; structured weight-loss program for BMI ≥27

Summary recommendation

Primary-care monitoring of established atrial fibrillation / flutter is anchored in annual (and event-driven) reassessment of CHA2DS2-VASc thromboembolic risk and modifiable bleeding-risk factors via HAS-BLED (used to identify, not to withhold, anticoagulation).1 DOACs are preferred over warfarin in non-valvular AF; warfarin patients require ongoing INR monitoring (target 2.0–3.0; goal time-in-therapeutic-range >70%), with conversion to a DOAC considered when TTR cannot be maintained.1 DOAC patients need baseline and at least annual creatinine / eGFR (more frequent — approximately every CrCl/10 months — at lower CrCl, age ≥75, frailty, or acute illness), plus periodic hepatic and hemoglobin assessment; FDA-labeled dosing must be used (non-evidence-based dose reductions are explicitly discouraged, Class 3 Harm).1 Every visit should include pulse / rhythm assessment with ECG when needed; rate control accepts resting HR <110 bpm (lenient, per RACE II) when symptoms are controlled and LV function preserved, with stricter HR <80 bpm if symptomatic or tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy suspected. TTE is obtained at diagnosis and repeated for clinical change or pre-procedure. Post-ablation patients are followed with a 3-month blanking period before judging success, with symptom-driven ambulatory rhythm monitoring; OAC continues based on CHA2DS2-VASc regardless of apparent ablation success.1 The 2023 guideline elevates lifestyle and risk-factor modification to a co-equal pillar: ≥10% weight loss for BMI ≥27, ~210 min/wk moderate-vigorous exercise, BP <130/80, optimized glycemic and lipid management, alcohol minimization, tobacco cessation, and OSA screening / treatment.

Laboratory

  • INR (warfarin) — at least monthly once stable; more often during initiation, dose changes, or interacting drug / illness. Target 2.0–3.0; goal TTR >70%; consider switch to DOAC if TTR cannot be maintained.1
  • Renal function (serum creatinine, eGFR / Cockcroft-Gault CrCl) for DOAC dosing — at baseline and at least annually; more frequent at CrCl <60 mL/min, age ≥75, frailty, intercurrent illness (every CrCl/10 months).1
  • Hepatic function (AST / ALT, bilirubin) — baseline; periodically thereafter (per package-insert guidance). Avoid DOAC in moderate–severe hepatic impairment.1
  • CBC (hemoglobin) — at least annually; sooner with symptoms. Investigate unexplained Hgb drop on OAC.1
  • Diabetes (HbA1c) and lipid panel — per ADA / ACC-AHA prevention cadence.1

Imaging

  • 12-lead ECG / rhythm strip — at every clinical visit when rhythm assessment indicated; for symptoms or change.1
  • Transthoracic echocardiogram — at initial AF diagnosis; repeat for change in clinical status, new murmur, suspected progression of LV dysfunction, or before / after cardioversion / ablation / LAAO.1
  • TEE or pre-procedure cardiac CT — prior to cardioversion when <3 weeks of therapeutic OAC; pre-ablation; pre-LAAO per protocol.1
  • Ambulatory rhythm monitoring (Holter, patch, event monitor, ILR) — routine clinical follow-up at ≥3 months post-ablation; arrhythmia within the 3-month blanking period not counted as failure; ongoing intermittent monitoring as symptoms dictate.1

Physical examination

  • Resting heart rate / pulse and rhythm — every visit. Rate-control target resting HR <110 bpm (lenient) reasonable when asymptomatic and LVEF preserved; stricter HR <80 bpm if symptoms persist or tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy.1
  • Blood pressure — every visit. Target <130/80 mm Hg.1
  • Weight / BMI — every visit; structured weight-loss program for BMI ≥27 (target ≥10% body-weight reduction).1

Functional / surveys

  • CHA2DS2-VASc — annually and with any change in clinical status. Anticoagulate when score ≥2 (men) or ≥3 (women); shared decision for score 1 (men) / 2 (women).1
  • HAS-BLED (or equivalent bleeding-risk score) — annually and when risk profile changes. Used to flag modifiable factors (uncontrolled BP, NSAIDs, alcohol, labile INR), NOT to withhold OAC.1
  • Symptom assessment (palpitations, dyspnea, fatigue, exercise tolerance; EHRA / CCS-SAF) — every visit; drives rate-vs.-rhythm-control decisions and escalation.1
  • Physical-activity counseling — every visit. Target ~210 min/week moderate-vigorous activity.1
  • Alcohol-use assessment and reduction counseling — every visit. Minimize or eliminate (≤1 drink/day, abstinence preferred for rhythm-control goals).1
  • Tobacco-use assessment and cessation counseling — every visit.1
  • OSA symptom screen, with sleep study and CPAP adherence review if diagnosed — at diagnosis and at least annually; treatment adherence reviewed each visit.1
  • LAA occlusion (Watchman) candidacy — at any contraindication / major bleed on OAC; CHA2DS2-VASc ≥3 (men) / ≥4 (women); CMS NCD 20.34 coverage-with-evidence-development applies.13

Notes

  • Screening for AF in asymptomatic adults. USPSTF 2022 issued an I (insufficient evidence) statement for screening ≥50 without prior AF; 2023 ACC/AHA/ACCP/HRS endorses opportunistic pulse / rhythm screening in adults ≥65 (Class 2a) and is more permissive about consumer wearables.12
  • Heart-rate target lenient vs. strict. RACE II established non-inferiority of lenient (<110 bpm) vs. strict; 2023 guideline endorses lenient as reasonable (Class 2a). Stricter remains appropriate for reduced LV function, persistent symptoms, or tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy.1
  • LAAO (Watchman) eligibility. 2023 guideline upgraded LAAO to Class 2a (broader than 2019 Class 2b). CMS NCD 20.34 adds CED requirements (operator / site volumes, registry enrollment).13
  • DOACs in advanced CKD / ESRD. 2023 guideline allows labeled-dose apixaban (and warfarin) in stage 4–5 CKD / dialysis (Class 2a–2b); earlier nephrology positions preferred warfarin. Randomized data remain limited.1

Footnotes

  1. Joglar JA et al. 2023 ACC/AHA/ACCP/HRS Guideline for the Diagnosis and Management of Atrial Fibrillation. Circulation. 2024;149(1):e1-e156. See bibliography.md → accaha-af-2023. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. CMS. National Coverage Determination 20.34: Percutaneous Left Atrial Appendage Closure. See bibliography.md → cms-laac-ncd. ↩↩

Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease

ICD-10: J44.x (COPD; with / without exacerbation; with emphysema; with chronic bronchitis) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 12.8% — rank 14 of 30 Category: Respiratory Primary associations consulted: GOLD, USPSTF, ATS, CMS, CDC, ALA

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — Spirometry (post-bronchodilator FEV1 / FVC and FEV1 % predicted — at diagnosis + at least annually to detect rapid decliners); vaccination review (influenza annually; SARS-CoV-2 updated per CDC; PCV20 or PCV21 single dose; RSV ≥60 or comorbid; Tdap; zoster ≥50) — Alpha-1 antitrypsin (AAT) once in lifetime at diagnosis; blood eosinophil count at baseline + when considering ICS escalation / de-escalation; ABG when SpO2 ≤92% or for LTOT qualification
Imaging — — — — — Chest CT (low-dose) for persistent exacerbation, suspected lung cancer / emphysema / bronchiectasis; annual LDCT lung-cancer screening if 20+ pack-year smoking history, age 50–80 (USPSTF Grade B); LVR / transplant evaluation
Physical exam Pulse oximetry (SpO2); smoking status + cessation counseling; BMI / nutritional status — — — — ECG / cardiovascular risk assessment at baseline; reassess after exacerbations (GOLD 2025 cardiopulmonary risk emphasis)
Functional / surveys CAT (COPD Assessment Test); mMRC dyspnea; exacerbation history (12-mo lookback); GOLD A / B / E reclassification — — Pulmonary-rehab eligibility review (after exacerbation hospitalization initiate within 3 wk of discharge; stable mMRC ≥2 / CAT ≥10) — 6-Minute Walk Test at pulmonary-rehab entry / exit; LTOT assessment (resting PaO2 ≤55 / SpO2 ≤88 — or ≤60 / 88 with cor pulmonale / polycythemia — confirm twice ≥3 wk apart)

Summary recommendation

US primary-care COPD surveillance under GOLD 2025 emphasizes structured, symptom- and exacerbation-driven monitoring rather than dense laboratory testing.1 At every visit, clinicians should assess symptoms with the CAT (≥10 = "more symptoms") and mMRC dyspnea scale (≥2 triggering pulmonary-rehab referral), document moderate and severe exacerbations over the past 12 months to reclassify GOLD A / B / E groupings, measure SpO2, and reinforce smoking cessation.1 Spirometry is performed at diagnosis and at least annually thereafter (or with significant clinical change) to identify rapid decliners. Alpha-1 antitrypsin testing should be obtained once in every COPD patient, and a baseline blood eosinophil count informs ICS and biologic (dupilumab) decisions, with ≥300 cells/µL favoring ICS / biologic therapy and <100 cells/µL arguing against ICS.1 Vaccinations (annual influenza, updated SARS-CoV-2, PCV20 or PCV21, RSV if ≥60 or comorbid, Tdap, zoster) follow CDC ACIP and should be reviewed yearly. Pulmonary rehabilitation should be initiated within 3 weeks of any exacerbation-related hospitalization (ATS 2023 strong recommendation) and offered to stable patients with mMRC ≥2; CMS NCD 240.8 covers up to 36 sessions for GOLD II–IV COPD.26 Long-term oxygen therapy is initiated when stable resting PaO2 ≤55 mm Hg or SpO2 ≤88% (or ≤60 mm Hg / 88% with cor pulmonale or polycythemia), confirmed on two measurements ≥3 weeks apart, with a treatment target SpO2 88–92%.13

Laboratory

  • Spirometry (post-bronchodilator FEV1 / FVC, FEV1 % predicted) — at diagnosis; at least annually thereafter to detect rapid decliners and with significant clinical change. Post-BD FEV1 / FVC <0.70 confirms airflow limitation.1
  • Pulse oximetry (SpO2) — every visit; ABG if SpO2 ≤92% or for LTOT qualification.1
  • Arterial blood gas (ABG) — as indicated (SpO2 ≤92%, LTOT assessment, suspected hypercapnia / NIV consideration). PaO2 ≤55 mm Hg (or ≤60 with cor pulmonale / polycythemia) qualifies for LTOT; confirm twice ≥3 weeks apart.13
  • Alpha-1 antitrypsin (AAT) — once in lifetime at COPD diagnosis (also adult-onset asthma with persistent obstruction, unexplained bronchiectasis). Serum AAT <11 µmol/L (<57 mg/dL) = severe deficiency.1
  • Blood eosinophil count — baseline and when considering ICS escalation / de-escalation. ≥300 favors ICS and supports dupilumab add-on (with chronic bronchitis + ongoing exacerbations); 100–300 supports ICS if exacerbating; <100 argues against ICS.1

Imaging

  • Chest CT (low-dose) — as indicated for persistent exacerbations, suspected emphysema / bronchiectasis / lung cancer; for LVR or transplant evaluation; annual LDCT for lung-cancer screening if 20+ pack-year smoking history, age 50–80 (USPSTF 2021 Grade B).15

Physical examination

  • Pulse oximetry, smoking status, BMI / nutritional status — every visit. Identify low BMI (<21 kg/m²) and unintentional weight loss as poor-prognosis markers.1
  • ECG / cardiovascular risk assessment — at baseline; reassess after exacerbations (new GOLD 2025 emphasis on cardiopulmonary risk).1

Functional / surveys

  • CAT (COPD Assessment Test) — every clinic visit. CAT ≥10 = "more symptoms" (group B or E); change ≥2 is clinically meaningful.1
  • mMRC dyspnea scale — every clinic visit. mMRC ≥2 = "more symptoms" and a trigger for pulmonary-rehab referral.1
  • Exacerbation history (moderate / severe events in past 12 months) — every visit. Defines group E (≥2 moderate or ≥1 hospitalization).1
  • Smoking-cessation counseling — every visit (behavioral + pharmacotherapy first-line).1
  • Vaccination status review — annually; aligned to CDC ACIP (influenza annually; SARS-CoV-2 per CDC; PCV20 or PCV21 single dose; RSV ≥60 or comorbid; Tdap; zoster ≥50).1
  • 6-Minute Walk Test (6MWT) — at pulmonary-rehab entry / exit; LTOT assessment; LVR / transplant evaluation.12
  • Pulmonary rehabilitation referral — after any exacerbation hospitalization (initiate within 3 weeks of discharge); stable patients with mMRC ≥2 / CAT ≥10. CMS NCD 240.8 covers 36 sessions for GOLD II–IV.216

Notes

  • Screening vs. case-finding. USPSTF 2022 (Grade D) recommends against spirometric screening of asymptomatic adults. GOLD 2025 endorses opportunistic case-finding via spirometry in adults reporting cough, sputum, dyspnea, recurrent LRI, or significant tobacco / biomass exposure. Both agree against screening truly asymptomatic adults.41
  • Eosinophil thresholds for ICS. GOLD uses a graded approach (≥300 favors, <100 disfavors). Some specialty bodies emphasize a single ≥300 cells/µL cut-point and weight exacerbation history over eosinophils.1
  • Pulmonary-rehab eligibility timing. ATS 2023 strongly recommends PR within 3 weeks of hospital discharge. CMS NCD 240.8 restricts coverage to GOLD II–IV (moderate–very severe); does not cover GOLD I, even though GOLD / ATS would consider PR for symptomatic mild disease.26
  • Annual spirometry. GOLD 2025 advises at least annual spirometry to detect rapid decline; some primary-care guidance considers it optional in stable, mildly symptomatic patients.1

Footnotes

  1. GOLD. 2025 Global Strategy for Prevention, Diagnosis and Management of COPD. See bibliography.md → gold-2025. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. Rochester CL et al. Pulmonary Rehabilitation for Adults with Chronic Respiratory Disease: An Official ATS Clinical Practice Guideline. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2023;208(4):e7-e26. See bibliography.md → ats-pr-2023. ↩↩↩↩

  3. Jacobs SS et al. Home Oxygen Therapy for Adults with Chronic Lung Disease: ATS Clinical Practice Guideline. Am J Respir Crit Care Med. 2020;202(10):e121-e141. See bibliography.md → ats-ltot-2020. ↩↩

  4. USPSTF. Screening for Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease: Reaffirmation Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2022;327(18):1806-1811 (Grade D). See bibliography.md → uspstf-copd-2022. ↩

  5. USPSTF. Screening for Lung Cancer: Recommendation Statement. 2021. See bibliography.md → uspstf-lung-2021. ↩

Heart Failure

ICD-10: I50.x (heart failure — systolic, diastolic, mixed; with / without RHF) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 12.3% — rank 15 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: AHA/ACC/HFSA, HFSA, CMS, NHLBI

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — BMP (Na, K, BUN, Cr / eGFR, Mg) within 1–2 wk after starting / titrating ACEi / ARB / ARNI / MRA / SGLT2i / diuretic; every 3–6 mo when stable BMP every 3–6 mo (stable) Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — HFrEF periodic; lipid panel; HbA1c (if DM); TSH; CBC; urinalysis if clinically indicated — Initial labs at diagnosis (CBC, urinalysis, BMP, LFTs, lipid, TSH, HbA1c, iron studies); BNP / NT-proBNP at presentation, admission, and predischarge; troponin with acute change
Imaging — — — — — TTE at diagnosis; repeat with unexplained clinical change, after intervention likely to alter LV function (3–6 mo after GDMT optimization for HFimpEF), or pre-procedure; ECG at initial eval + with symptom change or device consideration
Physical exam BP, HR, weight, JVP, peripheral edema, lung exam (volume status); NYHA functional class — — — — —
Functional / surveys NYHA class; weight diary / RPM review KCCQ (Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire) every 3–6 mo or with clinical change — Depression screen (PHQ-2 → PHQ-9); cardiac-rehab participation review — Cardiac-rehab referral (CMS NCD 20.10.1: LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV, OMT ≥6 wk, no hospitalization / procedure within 6 wk); RPM / CardioMEMS as appropriate

Summary recommendation

US primary-care surveillance of established adult heart failure (HFrEF, HFmrEF, HFpEF) under the 2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA guideline (with 2023 / 2024 ACC ECDPs and 2024 Performance Measures) follows a structured cadence.12 Every visit captures BP, HR, weight, volume status (JVP / edema / lungs), and NYHA class; KCCQ is recommended at baseline and serially to track patient-reported health status (Class 2a).1 During GDMT initiation or titration of ACEi / ARB / ARNI / MRA / SGLT2i or diuretic, a BMP (Na, K, Cr / eGFR, BUN, Mg) is checked within 1–2 weeks, then every 3–6 months once stable; stable patients are otherwise seen every 3–6 months.12 Initial workup includes CBC, BMP, LFTs, lipids, TSH, HbA1c, iron studies (ferritin / TSAT), urinalysis, ECG, and 2D echocardiogram with Doppler (Class 1).1 BNP or NT-proBNP is Class 1 for diagnosis and for hospital admission / predischarge prognostication; outpatient serial measurement to titrate therapy is not endorsed but values inform prognosis and decongestion. Iron studies are repeated periodically in HFrEF; IV iron is indicated when ferritin <100 ng/mL or 100–300 with TSAT <20%. Echocardiography is repeated only with unexplained clinical change or after an intervention expected to alter LV function (e.g., to document HFimpEF after 3–6 months of GDMT) — routine surveillance imaging is not recommended. CMS covers cardiac rehabilitation for stable HFrEF (LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV) under NCD 20.10.1, and supports RPM (weight, BP, PA pressure) for chronic HF management.4

Laboratory

  • Initial labs (at diagnosis) — CBC, urinalysis, BMP (Na, K, BUN, Cr, eGFR, glucose), LFTs, lipid panel, TSH, iron studies (ferritin, TSAT), HbA1c. Identify comorbidities and reversible causes.1
  • BNP or NT-proBNP — diagnostic — at presentation with dyspnea or suspected HF.1
  • BNP or NT-proBNP — prognostic — at hospital admission and predischarge; selectively in outpatient follow-up.1
  • BMP (Na, K, Cr, eGFR, BUN, Mg) post-GDMT — within 1–2 weeks after starting or titrating ACEi / ARB / ARNI / MRA / SGLT2i / diuretic; then every 3–6 months when stable. K 4.0–5.0 mEq/L; tolerate up to ~30% rise in Cr on RAAS initiation; address hyperkalemia >5.5.12
  • Iron studies (ferritin, TSAT) — at diagnosis and periodically (e.g., annually) in HFrEF; reassess after IV iron repletion. IV iron indicated when ferritin <100 ng/mL or 100–300 ng/mL with TSAT <20%.1
  • Troponin — with acute clinical change or suspected ACS.1

Imaging

  • 2D transthoracic echocardiogram with Doppler — at initial diagnosis; repeat with significant unexplained change in clinical status or after intervention likely to alter LV function (e.g., 3–6 months after GDMT optimization to document HFimpEF). NOT routine surveillance. Classify LVEF (HFrEF ≤40%, HFmrEF 41–49%, HFpEF ≥50%; HFimpEF if ≥10-point rise to >40%).1
  • 12-lead ECG — at initial evaluation; repeat with symptom change, arrhythmia, or QRS-related device consideration. Identify rhythm, conduction (QRS ≥150 ms LBBB for CRT), ischemia.1
  • Implantable pulmonary artery pressure sensor (CardioMEMS) — continuous monitoring with weekly clinician review for NYHA III with HF hospitalization in prior 12 months despite GDMT (Class 2b in 2022 guideline; value uncertain).1

Physical examination

  • BP, HR, weight, JVP, peripheral edema, lung exam — every visit (every 1–2 weeks during GDMT titration; every 3–6 months when stable). Target euvolemia.1

Functional / surveys

  • NYHA functional class — every visit.1
  • KCCQ (Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire) — baseline and serially (every 3–6 months or with clinical change). Score 0–100; <50 indicates poor-to-fair health status.1
  • Cardiac rehabilitation participation — periodic exercise-capacity reassessment. CMS NCD 20.10.1 covers LVEF ≤35%, NYHA II–IV on optimal therapy ≥6 weeks, no hospitalization / procedure within 6 weeks.14
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 → PHQ-9) — periodically.5

Notes

  • Routine echocardiography. 2022 guideline explicitly rejects routine serial echo in stable patients; community practice often obtains annual echoes. Echo at 3–6 months after GDMT optimization is supported only to document HFimpEF or guide device decisions.1
  • Serial NT-proBNP for therapy titration. 2022 guideline states evidence is insufficient for biomarker-guided titration (GUIDE-IT trial neutral); some HFSA expert pathways still endorse trend monitoring.1
  • SGLT2i in HFpEF. 2022 guideline gave Class 2a (EMPEROR-Preserved); 2023 ACC ECDP for HFpEF and 2024 Performance Measures effectively moved practice toward universal use absent contraindication (DELIVER), while the formal CoR in the parent guideline remains 2a pending next focused update.13
  • Iron deficiency thresholds. AHA/ACC/HFSA 2022 and ESC 2021 both use ferritin <100 OR (100–300 with TSAT <20%), but newer analyses argue TSAT <20% with serum iron <13 µmol/L better predicts response — threshold under debate.1
  • CardioMEMS / implantable PA monitoring. Class 2b "value uncertain" reflects discordant CHAMPION (positive) and GUIDE-HF (neutral) results; specialty HF groups push for broader use in NYHA III.1

Footnotes

  1. Heidenreich PA et al. 2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA Guideline for the Management of Heart Failure. Circulation. 2022;145(18):e895-e1032. See bibliography.md → accaha-hf-2022. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. Maddox TM et al. 2024 ACC Expert Consensus Decision Pathway for Treatment of HFrEF. J Am Coll Cardiol. 2024;83(15):1444-1488. See bibliography.md → acc-hfref-ecdp-2024. ↩↩↩

  3. Kittleson MM et al. 2023 ACC Expert Consensus Decision Pathway on Management of HFpEF. J Am Coll Cardiol. 2023;81(18):1835-1878. See bibliography.md → acc-hfpef-ecdp-2023. ↩

  4. CMS. NCD 20.10.1: Cardiac Rehabilitation Programs for Chronic Heart Failure. See bibliography.md → cms-hf-cr. ↩↩

  5. Lichtman JH et al. Depression and Coronary Heart Disease — AHA Science Advisory. Circulation. 2008;118:1768-1775. See bibliography.md → aha-depr-cvd-2008. ↩

Osteoporosis

ICD-10: M80.x (osteoporosis with current pathologic fracture), M81.x (osteoporosis without fracture) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 11.5% — rank 16 of 30 Category: Musculoskeletal Primary associations consulted: USPSTF, AACE/ACE, BHOF (formerly NOF), Endocrine Society, ACP, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Serum Ca prior to each IV zoledronate or denosumab dose — 25-OH vitamin D (after repletion, then annually); calcium-intake assessment — Baseline secondary-causes workup (Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alk phos, Cr / eGFR, P, Mg, TSH, CBC, 24-hr urine Ca; SPEP / celiac / testosterone if indicated); CTX or P1NP bone-turnover markers (baseline + 3–6 mo after starting / changing therapy, and during drug holiday)
Imaging — — — — Central DXA every 2 years (Medicare-coverable when medically necessary; USPSTF acknowledges 4–8 yr intervals may suffice in low-risk untreated women) Central DXA every 1–2 yr on therapy (AACE) or 1–3 yr (Endo Society) until stable, then longer intervals; Vertebral Fracture Assessment (VFA) if height loss ≥1.5 in historical / ≥0.8 in prospective, kyphosis, recent low-trauma fracture, chronic glucocorticoids, or T-score ≤ −2.5
Physical exam — — — Standing height measurement; fall-risk screen (Timed Up and Go, fall history, medication review, vision, gait / balance) — Height drop ≥2 cm prospective or ≥4 cm historical triggers VFA
Functional / surveys — — Denosumab continuity check (no gap >7 mo; if discontinuing, transition to bisphosphonate to prevent rebound vertebral fractures) FRAX 10-yr fracture risk recalculation; calcium intake (target 1000 mg/d men 50–70, 1200 mg/d women ≥51 / men ≥71; vit D 800–1000 IU/d) — Drug-holiday eligibility reassessment after 5 yr oral bisphosphonate or 3 yr IV zoledronate; anabolic-to-antiresorptive sequencing at end of teriparatide / abaloparatide (18–24 mo) or romosozumab (12 mo)

Summary recommendation

Primary-care osteoporosis surveillance combines BMD imaging, fracture-risk recalibration, drug-class-specific safety checks, and lifestyle / fall assessment. Per USPSTF 2025 (Grade B), screening DXA is recommended for women ≥65 and for postmenopausal women <65 at increased risk; men remain an I-statement.1 Once on pharmacotherapy, AACE 2020 endorses repeating central DXA every 1–2 years until stable, with longer intervals thereafter; the Endocrine Society 2019 supports 1–3 years; ACP 2023 and BHOF 2022 take a more skeptical stance on within-treatment DXA because fracture-risk reduction is decoupled from measured BMD change.2356 FRAX should be recalculated when risk factors change and at drug-holiday decision points. Drug-class monitoring is critical: denosumab must not be discontinued without immediate bisphosphonate transition (rebound vertebral fractures); oral bisphosphonates warrant drug-holiday consideration after 5 years (IV zoledronate after 3 years) in low-to-moderate-risk patients; anabolic agents (teriparatide, abaloparatide, romosozumab) require sequencing to an antiresorptive at course end.32 All patients need baseline secondary-causes labs (Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alkaline phosphatase, creatinine, TSH, ±24-hr urine Ca), pre-dose calcium checks for IV antiresorptives, and ongoing calcium 1000–1200 mg/d plus vitamin D 800–1000 IU/d. Annual height measurement, fall-risk screening, and vertebral fracture assessment when triggered round out primary-care surveillance. CTX / P1NP markers offer an inexpensive adherence and response check 3–6 months after therapy change and during drug holidays.

Laboratory

  • Secondary-causes workup at diagnosis — serum Ca, 25-OH vitamin D, PTH, alkaline phosphatase, Cr / eGFR, phosphorus, magnesium, TSH, CBC, 24-hour urine Ca; consider SPEP, celiac serology, testosterone (men). Identify reversible causes; correct hypocalcemia and vitamin D deficiency before antiresorptive therapy.263
  • 25-OH vitamin D — at baseline; repeat after repletion until adequate, then annually. Target ≥30 ng/mL.26
  • Serum calcium (corrected) — prior to each IV zoledronate or denosumab dose; before initiating any antiresorptive. Avoid hypocalcemia, especially with CKD.23
  • Bone-turnover markers — serum CTX (antiresorptive) or P1NP (anabolic) — baseline; 3–6 months after starting / changing therapy; during drug holiday. Suppression of CTX with antiresorptive; rise in P1NP with anabolic indicates response; rise during holiday signals waning effect.236

Imaging

  • Central DXA (lumbar spine + hip / femoral neck) for screening — every 2 years (Medicare-coverable when medically necessary; USPSTF 2025 acknowledges 4–8 year intervals may suffice in untreated low-risk women).17
  • Central DXA for treatment monitoring — every 1–2 years (AACE) or 1–3 years (Endocrine Society) until stable, then less frequently. ACP 2023 and BHOF 2022 do not endorse routine repeat DXA during the initial 5-year bisphosphonate course because fracture benefit is decoupled from measured BMD change.2356
  • Vertebral Fracture Assessment (VFA) via DXA or lateral spine radiograph — at baseline if height loss ≥1.5 in (4 cm) historical or ≥0.8 in (2 cm) prospective, kyphosis, recent low-trauma fracture, chronic glucocorticoids, or T-score ≤ −2.5; repeat as clinically warranted.62

Physical examination

  • Standing height measurement (stadiometer) — annually for all postmenopausal women and men ≥50. Trigger VFA if loss ≥2 cm prospective or ≥4 cm historical.62
  • Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up and Go, history of falls, medication review, vision, gait / balance) — annually. TUG <12 sec; identify and mitigate modifiable risks.62

Functional / surveys

  • FRAX 10-year fracture risk recalculation — at baseline and as indicated when risk factors change; at drug-holiday decision points. Major osteoporotic ≥20% or hip ≥3% triggers treatment.126
  • Calcium-intake assessment (dietary ± supplement) — at baseline and annually. Target 1000 mg/d men 50–70, 1200 mg/d women ≥51 / men ≥71; avoid >1200–1500 mg/d.623
  • Drug-holiday eligibility reassessment — after 5 years oral bisphosphonate or 3 years IV zoledronate in low-to-moderate ongoing fracture risk. Holiday up to 5 yr (oral) or 3 yr (zoledronate) with reassessment every 2–4 yr. Do NOT holiday high-risk or denosumab patients.3265
  • Denosumab continuity check — every 6 months at dosing visits. No gap >7 months; if discontinuing, transition to bisphosphonate to prevent rebound vertebral fractures.326
  • Anabolic-to-antiresorptive sequencing — at end of 18–24 mo anabolic course (teriparatide / abaloparatide) or 12 mo romosozumab. Follow with bisphosphonate or denosumab to preserve BMD gains.426

Notes

  • Screening starting age in younger postmenopausal women. USPSTF 2025 limits Grade B screening to postmenopausal women <65 with elevated risk identified by clinical tool (now favoring DXA over tool-only triage); AACE and BHOF screen all postmenopausal women ≥50 with risk factors and all women ≥65 regardless of risk.126
  • Men. USPSTF 2025 issues an I-statement for men; AACE, BHOF, and Endocrine Society support screening men ≥70 (and ≥50 with risk factors).12
  • DXA on therapy. Endocrine Society 1–3 years; AACE 1–2 years until stable; ACP 2023 and BHOF 2022 recommend against routine BMD monitoring during initial 5-year bisphosphonate course because fracture benefit accrues independent of BMD change.2356
  • Bone-turnover markers. AACE and Endocrine Society endorse CTX / P1NP for response / adherence monitoring; ACP does not recommend routine use; BHOF describes them as an optional adjunct.235
  • Screening interval untreated. USPSTF acknowledges evidence-poor area, with modeling suggesting 4–8 year repeat intervals in low-risk women; CMS reimburses every 2 years (every 23 months) and more often if medically necessary.17

Prostate Cancer

ICD-10: C61 (malignant neoplasm of prostate) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 9.9% (males only) — rank 17 of 30 Category: Cancer (urologic; males only) Primary associations consulted: NCCN, AUA/SUO, ASCO, ASTRO, USPSTF

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — PSA on active surveillance (q3–6 mo); testosterone on ADT (q3–6 mo, target <50 ng/dL); PSA on ADT / AR-pathway inhibitor; abiraterone LFTs / K+ / BP (q2 wk × 3 mo then monthly); mCRPC baseline labs (CBC, CMP, ALP, LDH, Hgb) each cycle PSA post-curative (q6–12 mo first 5 yr) PSA post-curative (after 5 yr stable, annual); lipid panel + fasting glucose / HbA1c (on ADT); Ca / 25-OH vit D (on ADT, on bone-modifying agents) DEXA on ADT (baseline + every 1–2 yr); MRI prostate on AS (every 1–3 yr) Confirmatory biopsy on AS at 6–12 mo, then every 2–5 yr or as triggered; PSMA PET/CT at BCR with negative conventional imaging or for unfavorable / high-risk staging; conventional CT abd/pelvis + Tc-99m bone scan at BCR / progression; imaging in mCRPC at least annually; eGFR / Cr per agent
Imaging — — — — DEXA on ADT (every 1–2 yr); mpMRI on AS (every 1–3 yr) CT abd/pelvis + bone scan at BCR / progression; PSMA PET/CT; mCRPC imaging q6–12 mo (sooner if PSADT short or symptoms)
Physical exam BP and cardiovascular assessment on ADT / AR-pathway inhibitor — — DRE on active surveillance; DRE post-curative (may omit if PSA undetectable post-RP) — —
Functional / surveys Distress screen (NCCN Distress Thermometer); hot flashes / vasomotor symptoms (on ADT); mood / depression; sexual function review — — Urinary continence (EPIC, IPSS); erectile function (SHIM / IIEF); bowel function post-RT; age-appropriate second-cancer screening (CRC, skin) per USPSTF — Urology referral for bothersome incontinence or persistent rectal bleeding

Summary recommendation

The dominant rhythm of prostate cancer surveillance is PSA every 3–6 months with risk-adapted intensification. On active surveillance, NCCN v1.2025 and AUA/ASTRO 2022 endorse PSA every 6 months (often q3–6 mo in the first year), annual DRE, multiparametric MRI roughly every 1–3 years, and a confirmatory biopsy within 6–12 months of diagnosis followed by surveillance biopsies every 2–5 years or when triggered by PSA kinetics, new DRE findings, or rising PI-RADS.12 After curative therapy, PSA is checked every 6–12 months for 5 years and then annually; the operative definitions of recurrence are PSA ≥0.2 ng/mL (confirmed) post-prostatectomy and PSA nadir + 2 ng/mL (Phoenix) post-radiation. PSMA PET is now first-line at biochemical recurrence when conventional imaging is negative.31 For men on ADT or AR-pathway inhibitors (apalutamide, enzalutamide, darolutamide, abiraterone), primary-care-relevant monitoring includes serum testosterone (goal <50 ng/dL), DEXA at baseline and every 1–2 years with FRAX-guided bisphosphonate or denosumab use, calcium / vitamin D replacement, annual lipid and glycemic screening, blood pressure at every visit, and structured assessment of hot flashes, fatigue, mood, sexual function, and cognition.36 In mCRPC, imaging is performed at least annually even with stable PSA. Survivorship priorities the PCP owns include urinary and sexual function, depression and PSA anxiety, cardiometabolic risk on ADT, bone health, and continued age-appropriate screening for second cancers.67

Laboratory

  • PSA on active surveillance — every 3–6 months. Rising PSA or PSA density >0.15 ng/mL/cc triggers re-evaluation.12
  • PSA post-radical prostatectomy — every 6–12 months for 5 years, then annually. Target undetectable (<0.1 ng/mL; ultrasensitive <0.01); BCR = 2 consecutive PSA ≥0.2 ng/mL.12
  • PSA post-radiation — every 6–12 months for 5 years, then annually. BCR = PSA nadir + 2 ng/mL (Phoenix).12
  • Testosterone on ADT — every 3–6 months. Target <50 ng/dL (castrate); castrate level required for CRPC definition.31
  • PSA on ADT / AR-pathway inhibitor — every 3–6 months. Declining / stable expected; rising PSA = progression to CRPC if T<50.31
  • mCRPC baseline labs (CBC, CMP, ALP, LDH, Hgb) — at baseline and with each treatment cycle / visit. ALP rise suggests bone progression.3
  • Abiraterone monitoring (LFTs, potassium, BP) — every 2 weeks × first 3 months, then monthly.3
  • Lipid panel + fasting glucose / HbA1c — baseline and annually on ADT and AR-pathway inhibitors.63
  • Calcium and 25-OH vitamin D — baseline and periodically on ADT / bone-modifying agents. Replete; supplement Ca 1000–1200 mg + vit D 1000 IU daily.31
  • eGFR / creatinine — per agent (zoledronic acid, abiraterone, chemotherapy).3

Imaging

  • Multiparametric MRI prostate — every 1–3 years on active surveillance; confirmatory at 6–12 months. Stable PI-RADS; no new ≥PI-RADS 4 lesion.12
  • Confirmatory / surveillance prostate biopsy — within 6–12 months of AS enrollment; then every 2–5 years or as triggered. No upgrading to Grade Group ≥2 (high-volume) or ≥3.12
  • DEXA / BMD on ADT — baseline before ADT; every 1–2 years on ADT (or per FRAX risk). FRAX 10-yr hip ≥3% or major osteoporotic ≥20% triggers bone-protective therapy.31
  • Conventional imaging (CT abd/pelvis + Tc-99m bone scan) — at biochemical recurrence; at progression; every 6–12 months in mCRPC.31
  • PSMA PET/CT — at BCR with negative or equivocal conventional imaging; for initial staging of unfavorable intermediate / high-risk; periodic restaging in advanced disease.31
  • Imaging in mCRPC without PSA progression — at least annually.3

Physical examination

  • DRE on active surveillance — annually. No new nodularity.12
  • DRE post-curative treatment — annually (may omit if PSA undetectable post-RP).16
  • Blood pressure / cardiovascular assessment — every visit on ADT or AR-pathway inhibitor.36

Functional / surveys

  • Urinary continence / LUTS assessment (EPIC, IPSS) — every follow-up visit, particularly first 2 years post-treatment.62
  • Erectile / sexual function (SHIM / IIEF) — at baseline and periodically. Offer PDE5i, penile rehabilitation, referral.62
  • Distress / depression / PSA-anxiety screen — initial visit and at appropriate intervals (PHQ-9 or NCCN Distress Thermometer).6
  • Hot flash / vasomotor symptom assessment — each visit on ADT. Options: venlafaxine, gabapentin, medroxyprogesterone.6
  • Bowel function — each visit post-RT. Refer for persistent rectal bleeding or urgency.6
  • Age-appropriate second-cancer screening (CRC, skin) — per USPSTF.67

Notes

  • PSA screening framework disagreement. USPSTF 2018 grade C (shared decision 55–69) and grade D (≥70) is more restrictive than AUA/SUO Early Detection 2023, which endorses baseline PSA at 45–50 and risk-stratified screening through age 70+ in healthy men.54
  • Active-surveillance biopsy trigger. NCCN relies on PSA kinetics and MRI changes; AUA emphasizes MRI plus periodic surveillance biopsy — MRI does not replace biopsy.12
  • PSMA PET utilization. AUA 2023 update strongly supports PSMA PET for staging unfavorable intermediate / high-risk disease and BCR; CMS coverage is broad for FDA-approved tracers, but local contractor policies vary.3
  • Imaging in mCRPC without PSA progression. AUA requires at least annual imaging; NCCN allows symptom- and PSA-driven imaging, creating a real-world cadence gap.31

Footnotes

  2. Eastham JA et al. Clinically Localized Prostate Cancer: AUA/ASTRO Guideline. J Urol. 2022. See bibliography.md → aua-astro-pca-2022. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. Lowrance W et al. Updates to Advanced Prostate Cancer: AUA/SUO Guideline (2023). See bibliography.md → aua-apc-2023. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  4. Wei JT et al. Early Detection of Prostate Cancer: AUA/SUO Guideline (2023). See bibliography.md → aua-pca-detection-2023. ↩

  5. USPSTF. Screening for Prostate Cancer: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2018;319(18):1901-1913. See bibliography.md → uspstf-pca-2018. ↩

  6. Skolarus TA et al. American Cancer Society Prostate Cancer Survivorship Care Guidelines; ASCO endorsement. J Clin Oncol. 2015. See bibliography.md → ascoacs-pca-2015. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

Non-Alzheimer's Dementia

ICD-10: F01.x (vascular dementia), F02.x (dementia in other diseases — DLB, FTD), G31.x (other degenerative diseases — DLB, FTD, PSP) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 8.7% — rank 18 of 30 Category: Neurologic Primary associations consulted: AAN, AGS, APA, USPSTF, CMS, Alzheimer's Association

CCW separates Alzheimer's disease (rank 22) from non-Alzheimer's dementia. This monograph covers vascular dementia, dementia with Lewy bodies (DLB), frontotemporal dementia (FTD), and mixed dementias. Surveillance overlaps substantially with Alzheimer's care; subtype-specific cautions are flagged.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — Caregiver burden assessment (Zarit) every 6–12 mo TSH, vitamin B12 at diagnosis (and as clinically indicated); CMP / CBC for delirium workup — Repeat labs only with acute cognitive worsening (rule out delirium)
Imaging — — — — — Non-contrast MRI preferred (CT if MRI contraindicated) once at diagnosis; repeat only with atypical course or focal change
Physical exam Medication review with AGS Beers Criteria (anticholinergic burden, sedative-hypnotics, antipsychotics); antipsychotic appropriateness review if on agent — — Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up & Go, gait / balance); driving safety evaluation (CDR-based) — Antipsychotic taper attempt within 4 months of initiation (APA)
Functional / surveys Neuropsychiatric / behavioral symptom screen (NPI-Q, BEHAVE-AD, PHQ-2); vascular risk-factor control (BP, lipids, glycemia, smoking, AFib anticoagulation) — — Cognitive testing (MoCA, Mini-Cog, MMSE, or SLUMS); ADL / IADL (Katz / Lawton-Brody); dementia staging (FAST or CDR); depression screen (GDS-15 or PHQ-9); advance care planning / goals-of-care discussion; CMS Cognitive Assessment & Care Plan Service (CPT 99483, billable every 180 days) — Health-related social needs (HRSN) screening; GUIDE Model enrollment for community-dwelling patients

Summary recommendation

US guidelines converge on a structured longitudinal surveillance pattern for established non-Alzheimer's dementia anchored by the annual Medicare Annual Wellness Visit (mandated cognitive impairment detection) and, when indicated, the CMS Cognitive Assessment & Care Plan Service (CPT 99483, billable every 180 days), which packages the core surveillance bundle.8 The bundle includes cognitive testing (MoCA / Mini-Cog / MMSE / SLUMS), ADL / IADL functional review, FAST or CDR staging, neuropsychiatric symptom screen (NPI-Q, BEHAVE-AD, or PHQ-2), high-risk medication reconciliation under AGS Beers 2023 (anticholinergics, benzodiazepines, antipsychotics), home and driving safety evaluation, caregiver-needs assessment, advance care planning, and a written care plan.85 AAN practice parameters (dementia diagnosis 2001, reaffirmed 2024; MCI 2018; driving 2010) and the 2024 Alzheimer's Association DETeCD-ADRD guideline support cognitive testing at least annually, depression screening (GDS / PHQ-9), TSH / B12 at diagnosis, and one-time structural MRI / CT.16 The APA 2016 antipsychotic guideline frames behavioral-symptom management with non-pharmacologic first-line care, low-dose second-generation antipsychotics only for severe / dangerous symptoms, attempted taper within 4 months, and avoidance in DLB due to neuroleptic sensitivity.4 CMS's GUIDE Model (July 2024–2032) layers care navigation, 24/7 caregiver support, respite, and HRSN screening on top of the visit-based surveillance.9 Fall risk (TUG), driving risk (CDR-based), and vascular risk-factor control receive distinct emphasis in vascular and DLB phenotypes.

Laboratory

  • TSH, vitamin B12 — at diagnosis to identify reversible contributors; repeat as clinically indicated. Target B12 >300 pg/mL; TSH within reference range.16
  • CMP, CBC — at diagnosis; with acute cognitive change to rule out delirium.1

Imaging

  • Structural neuroimaging (non-contrast MRI preferred; CT if MRI contraindicated) — once at diagnosis. Identify vascular burden (white matter, infarcts) in vascular dementia, focal atrophy in FTD, relatively preserved hippocampi in DLB. Repeat only with atypical course or focal change.16

Physical examination

  • Medication review with AGS Beers Criteria — every visit (required element of CPT 99483). Avoid / deprescribe anticholinergics, benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, first-generation antihistamines; address cumulative anticholinergic burden.58
  • Antipsychotic appropriateness and deprescribing review — every visit if on agent. Taper attempt within 4 months of initiation. CAUTION: avoid antipsychotics in DLB (severe neuroleptic sensitivity).45
  • Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up & Go, gait / balance exam) — annually. TUG >12 seconds = increased risk. High priority in vascular (gait apraxia) and DLB (parkinsonism).26
  • Driving safety evaluation (CDR-based) — at diagnosis and annually; sooner if caregiver concern. CDR ≥1 = high risk, recommend cessation; CDR 0.5–1 = on-road evaluation. Particular relevance for DLB (fluctuating cognition) and FTD (impulsivity).3

Functional / surveys

  • Cognitive status testing (MoCA, MMSE, Mini-Cog, or SLUMS) — annually (every visit if change reported); CMS AWV requires annual detection. Decline >2–3 points/year suggests faster progression.2106
  • ADL (Katz) and IADL (Lawton-Brody) — annually and at any reported change; required at CPT 99483. IADL loss marks MCI-to-mild-dementia; ADL loss marks moderate-to-severe.81
  • Dementia staging (FAST or CDR) — annually; at each CPT 99483 encounter. CDR: 0.5 MCI; 1 mild; 2 moderate; 3 severe.83
  • Neuropsychiatric / behavioral symptom screen (NPI-Q, BEHAVE-AD, or PHQ-2) — every visit; required at CPT 99483. Especially relevant in DLB (visual hallucinations, REM sleep behavior disorder), FTD (disinhibition, apathy), vascular (apathy, depression).84
  • Depression screening (GDS-15 or PHQ-9) — annually and at change. PHQ-9 ≥10 warrants treatment. High comorbidity in vascular dementia.16
  • Caregiver burden assessment (Zarit Burden Interview) and caregiver support needs — at diagnosis, then every 6–12 months; ongoing under GUIDE. Zarit ≥21 = moderate burden; trigger respite, education, referral.96
  • Advance care planning / goals of care — at diagnosis, then annually and at stage transitions; required at CPT 99483.89
  • Health-related social needs (HRSN) and psychosocial screening — at enrollment and periodically (GUIDE-enrolled).9
  • Vascular risk-factor control (BP, lipids, glycemia, smoking, AFib anticoagulation) — every visit. BP <130/80 (per ACC/AHA); statin per ASCVD; A1c individualized. High priority in vascular dementia / secondary stroke prevention.26

Notes

  • Population screening. USPSTF 2020 issued an I-statement (insufficient evidence) on screening asymptomatic community-dwelling adults ≥65; CMS nonetheless mandates AWV cognitive impairment detection; AAN and Alzheimer's Association endorse evaluation of symptomatic patients and brief tools when concerns arise. Ongoing tension between population screening and case-finding.710
  • Antipsychotic boundaries. FDA black-box warning (increased mortality in dementia) coexists with APA conditional recommendations for severe / dangerous symptoms; AGS Beers 2023 lists antipsychotics as "avoid" for behavioral problems unless non-pharmacologic options fail. APA permits short-term use with taper; Beers leans more restrictive.45
  • Cholinesterase inhibitors / memantine in non-AD dementias. AAN MCI 2018 recommends against pharmacologic cognitive enhancers in MCI; for DLB and Parkinson's dementia, rivastigmine has labeled evidence; for vascular and FTD, AAN does not endorse routine use.2
  • Routine repeat neuroimaging. AAN endorses one-time structural imaging at diagnosis; no guideline supports surveillance imaging absent clinical change.1

Breast Cancer (Female)

ICD-10: C50.x (malignant neoplasm of breast) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 8.2% (females only) — rank 19 of 30 Category: Cancer (females only; CCW Medicare population is overwhelmingly older women, often post-treatment for stage I-III disease on endocrine therapy) Primary associations consulted: ASCO/ACS Survivorship, NCCN, USPSTF, ACOG, ASCO Cardio-Oncology

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — LFTs / cardiotoxicity labs on active chemotherapy — Lipid panel + Ca / vitamin D counseling (on AI); CBC / chemistry only when symptoms or active therapy — Routine CBC, chemistry, LFTs, and tumor markers (CEA, CA 15-3, CA 27.29) NOT recommended for asymptomatic stage I-III survivors
Imaging — Echocardiogram (LVEF) every 3 months on trastuzumab Echocardiogram every 6 months for 2 years after trastuzumab completion Mammography of intact / ipsilateral breast DEXA every 2 years (on AI; on tamoxifen + GnRH; chemo-induced premature menopause) Breast MRI annually only if ACS high-risk criteria (BRCA1/2, prior chest radiation age 10–30, lifetime risk ≥20%); routine surveillance CT / bone scan / PET NOT recommended in asymptomatic stage I-III survivors
Physical exam Lymphedema assessment + risk-reduction counseling; peripheral neuropathy / AI arthralgia / musculoskeletal review — Cancer-related H&P every 3–6 months years 1–3 Cancer-related H&P every 6–12 months years 4–5, then annually; annual gynecologic assessment (tamoxifen — evaluate AUB promptly, no routine TVUS / biopsy) — VTE / DVT symptom inquiry every visit on tamoxifen
Functional / surveys Distress / depression / anxiety screen (PHQ-9, GAD-7, or NCCN Distress Thermometer); fatigue assessment; cognitive concerns ("chemobrain"); sexual / vaginal symptoms; vasomotor symptoms — — Healthy lifestyle counseling (≥150 min/wk activity, weight, alcohol ≤1 drink/day, smoking cessation); age- and risk-appropriate second-primary screening (CRC, cervical, lung) per USPSTF — Cardiac rehabilitation post anthracycline / trastuzumab cardiotoxicity per ASCO

Summary recommendation

For older women with stage I-III breast cancer in survivorship — the dominant CCW Medicare phenotype — primary-care surveillance is a structured, low-tech, symptom-driven model. ASCO/ACS (Runowicz 2016) and ASCO Follow-Up (Khatcheressian 2013), still the operative US framework and reinforced by NCCN Breast Cancer v5.2025, call for a detailed cancer-related history and physical exam every 3–6 months for the first 3 years, every 6–12 months years 4–5, then annually, plus annual mammography (ipsilateral breast after lumpectomy / radiation; contralateral only after mastectomy).124 Breast MRI is reserved for women meeting ACS high-risk criteria. Routine CBC, chemistries, LFTs, tumor markers, chest imaging, CT, bone scan, and PET are explicitly NOT recommended in asymptomatic survivors. Endocrine therapy adds three monitoring streams: on tamoxifen — annual gynecologic exam with prompt workup of any abnormal vaginal bleeding (no routine TVUS / biopsy) and VTE vigilance;6 on aromatase inhibitor — baseline DEXA and repeat every 2 years, calcium / vitamin D, lipid and cardiovascular risk monitoring, and assessment for AI arthralgia;1 on trastuzumab — baseline echocardiogram, q3-month LVEF during therapy, and q6-month for 2 years after (ASCO 2017 cardio-oncology).3 At every visit, clinicians should screen for lymphedema, fatigue, vasomotor symptoms, sexual dysfunction, depression / distress, cognitive concerns, and peripheral neuropathy; counsel on ≥150 min/wk activity, weight, alcohol ≤1 drink/day, smoking cessation; and confirm age-appropriate second-cancer screening per USPSTF.1

Laboratory

  • Routine CBC, chemistry panel, LFTs, tumor markers (CEA, CA 15-3, CA 27.29) — not recommended for routine surveillance in asymptomatic stage I-III survivors.21
  • Lipid panel — periodically per cardiovascular risk in women on aromatase inhibitor.1
  • Calcium / vitamin D adequacy — ongoing counseling during AI therapy.1

Imaging

  • Mammography of intact breast — annually post-mastectomy (unilateral). Annual bilateral diagnostic mammography post-lumpectomy + radiation.14
  • Breast MRI — annually as adjunct to mammography only if ACS high-risk criteria (BRCA1/2, prior chest radiation age 10–30, lifetime risk ≥20%); NOT routine after breast cancer alone.1
  • Surveillance imaging for distant metastasis (CT, bone scan, PET, chest x-ray, liver / pelvic US) — not recommended in asymptomatic patients.241
  • DEXA (bone mineral density) — baseline at AI initiation; repeat every 2 years. Also for premenopausal on tamoxifen + GnRH agonist; chemotherapy-induced premature menopause.14
  • Echocardiogram with LVEF — baseline before trastuzumab; every 3 months during therapy; every 6 months for at least 2 years after completion; baseline + as clinically indicated for anthracycline. Hold trastuzumab if absolute LVEF drop ≥16% or drop ≥10% to below institutional LLN.3
  • Surveillance imaging in metastatic (Stage IV) disease (CT chest / abd / pelvis, bone scan or FDG-PET/CT, brain MRI if symptomatic) — every 2–6 months (typically every 2–4 cycles of systemic therapy).4

Physical examination

  • Cancer-related history and physical examination — every 3–6 months for first 3 years; every 6–12 months years 4–5; then annually.12
  • Annual gynecologic assessment — on tamoxifen. Evaluate any abnormal vaginal bleeding promptly; routine endometrial biopsy / TVUS NOT recommended in asymptomatic women.16
  • Lymphedema assessment + risk-reduction counseling — every visit (counseling); referral to certified lymphedema therapist on symptoms. Post axillary surgery and / or regional radiation.1
  • Peripheral neuropathy / AI arthralgia / musculoskeletal review — each visit during / after taxane or AI. Function preservation; offer exercise, duloxetine for painful CIPN.1
  • VTE / DVT symptom inquiry — every visit on tamoxifen.41

Functional / surveys

  • Distress, depression, and anxiety screening — at periodic intervals (PHQ-9, GAD-7, or NCCN Distress Thermometer); higher cadence for high-risk groups.1
  • Sexual dysfunction / vaginal symptoms — periodically; identify dyspareunia, dryness, libido; offer non-hormonal therapies first.1
  • Cognitive impairment assessment ("chemobrain") — as indicated by patient report; rule out reversible causes; refer for neurocognitive testing if persistent.1
  • Fatigue assessment — at each clinical encounter; identify treatable contributors; offer aerobic exercise, CBT.1
  • Vasomotor symptom assessment — periodically on endocrine therapy. Offer SSRI / SNRI (avoid strong CYP2D6 inhibitors with tamoxifen), gabapentin, lifestyle.1
  • Healthy lifestyle counseling — ≥150 min/wk moderate or 75 min vigorous + 2 days strength training; nutrition; weight management; alcohol ≤1 drink/day; smoking cessation. Every visit.1
  • Age- and risk-appropriate screening for second primaries (colorectal, cervical, lung if eligible) — per USPSTF.15

Notes

  • USPSTF 2024 mammography (Grade B, biennial 40–74) explicitly excludes women with prior breast cancer — survivors follow surveillance (annual), not screening.51
  • Routine surveillance imaging is not endorsed. ASCO / NCCN / ACS strongly recommend AGAINST routine CT, bone scan, PET, or tumor markers in asymptomatic stage I-III survivors despite common community practice.21
  • Tamoxifen endometrial surveillance. ACOG Committee Opinion 601 and ASCO recommend AGAINST routine TVUS or endometrial biopsy in asymptomatic women on tamoxifen, relying on symptom-triggered evaluation of AUB. ACOG: postmenopausal vaginal bleeding requires endometrial sampling.6
  • DEXA cadence on AI. ASCO / ACS endorses every 2 years; NCCN tailors frequency to baseline T-score and FRAX risk, with shorter intervals (12–24 months) in higher-risk women.14
  • Breast MRI in dense breasts post-treatment. USPSTF I-statement on supplemental MRI / US in dense breasts; ASCO / ACS survivorship restricts MRI to defined high-risk criteria, not density alone.1

  3. Armenian SH et al. Prevention and Monitoring of Cardiac Dysfunction in Survivors of Adult Cancers: ASCO Clinical Practice Guideline. J Clin Oncol. 2017;35(8):893-911. See bibliography.md → asco-cardio-2017. ↩↩

  4. NCCN. Clinical Practice Guidelines in Oncology: Breast Cancer, Version 5.2025. See bibliography.md → nccn-bca-2025. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  5. USPSTF. Breast Cancer Screening: Recommendation Statement. 2024. See bibliography.md → uspstf-bca-2024. ↩↩
