Asthma

ICD-10: J45.x (asthma — mild intermittent / persistent / severe; with / without exacerbation) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 7.4% — rank 20 of 30 Category: Respiratory Primary associations consulted: GINA, NAEPP / NHLBI, ATS, AAAAI / ACAAI, CDC

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — — — FeNO (diagnostic uncertainty, monitoring response in allergic asthma — NAEPP conditional); blood eosinophil count (severe asthma biologic assessment; ≥3 measurements if below threshold); total / specific IgE (omalizumab candidacy; SCIT candidacy); pre-biologic phenotyping panel
Imaging — — — — — Chest x-ray or CT only for atypical features / suspected complication
Physical exam Inhaler technique check (with patient's own device) — — Spirometry (FEV1 / FVC, post-bronchodilator response — at diagnosis, after stabilization, then every 1–2 yr; more often if poor control / declining function / step change) — Peak expiratory flow (PEF) daily home monitoring for moderate-severe asthma, poor symptom perceivers, near-fatal asthma history
Functional / surveys ACT or ACQ; reliever / SABA canister use; exacerbation history (OCS bursts, ED visits, hospitalizations, ICU); adherence assessment; trigger / environmental exposure review (tobacco / vaping / occupational / aeroallergens / pollution); smoking / vaping status; asthma action-plan review or update — — Comorbidity surveillance (allergic rhinitis / CRS, GERD, obesity / BMI, OSA screen, VCD / ILO, anxiety / depression); vaccination status (influenza annually, COVID-19, PCV20 or PCV15 + PPSV23, RSV per ACIP, Tdap) — Step-up / step-down assessment (GINA Track 1 ICS-formoterol AIR / MART preferred); biologic candidacy assessment when severe / uncontrolled despite optimized step 4–5; pulmonary rehabilitation referral when persistent dyspnea / deconditioning; allergen immunotherapy (SCIT) candidacy in mild-moderate allergic asthma

Summary recommendation

US primary-care surveillance of adult asthma follows a structured cadence: initial review 1–3 months after starting or changing controller therapy, then every 3–12 months once stable; within 1 week of any exacerbation; and more frequently for uncontrolled disease or biologic patients.12 At every visit, clinicians administer a validated control instrument (ACT or ACQ), confirm inhaler technique with the patient's own device, review adherence and SABA / reliever canister use, screen for trigger exposures and tobacco / vaping, and update the written asthma action plan.1 Spirometry is obtained at diagnosis, after stabilization, and at least every 1–2 years; home PEF monitoring is added for moderate-severe disease and poor symptom perceivers.21 FeNO is recommended by NAEPP 2020 as an adjunct in diagnostic uncertainty and for monitoring anti-inflammatory therapy in allergic asthma (conditional), but not in isolation; GINA endorses FeNO and blood eosinophils chiefly to phenotype severe asthma and to qualify patients for biologics (BEC ≥150–300 / µL, FeNO ≥20 ppb; total / specific IgE for omalizumab).21 Step-up / step-down is reassessed at every visit using GINA's two-track framework, with Track 1 ICS-formoterol as anti-inflammatory reliever (AIR or MART / SMART) preferred across severity. Annual influenza, COVID-19, and pneumococcal vaccinations are standard. Comorbidities (allergic rhinitis / CRS, GERD, obesity, OSA, VCD / ILO, anxiety / depression) are screened at diagnosis and annually. Severe asthma triggers biologic referral, pulmonary rehabilitation when functionally limited, and SCIT consideration in mild-moderate allergic phenotypes only.2

Laboratory

  • Fractional exhaled nitric oxide (FeNO) — as indicated when diagnosis uncertain or to monitor adherence / response in allergic asthma. FeNO ≥20 ppb supports T2 inflammation (biologic threshold). Diagnostic interpretation per NAEPP cutoffs.21
  • Blood eosinophil count (BEC) — at severe-asthma assessment; repeat ≥3 times if below threshold before excluding T2 phenotype. ≥150 cells/µL suggests T2 inflammation; ≥300 cells/µL typical threshold for anti-IL-5 / anti-IL-4R biologics.1
  • Total serum IgE and allergen-specific IgE / aeroallergen skin testing — once at severe-asthma workup or when allergic phenotype unclear; before initiating omalizumab. Total IgE and weight used in omalizumab dosing nomogram.12

Imaging

No routine imaging is recommended for asthma surveillance. Chest x-ray or CT only for atypical features or suspected complication.1

Physical examination

  • Inhaler technique check (with patient's own device) — every visit. Re-train if any critical step is incorrect.12
  • Spirometry (FEV1, FVC, FEV1 / FVC ratio, post-bronchodilator response) — at diagnosis, at start of therapy, 3–6 months after stabilization, then at least every 1–2 years (more often if poor control, declining lung function, or step-change). FEV1 ≥80% predicted personal best.12
  • Peak expiratory flow (PEF) monitoring with action plan — daily or symptom-triggered home monitoring for moderate-severe asthma, poor symptom perceivers, history of near-fatal asthma, post-exacerbation. PEF ≥80% personal best (green); 50–79% yellow; <50% red.21

Functional / surveys

  • Asthma Control Test (ACT) or Asthma Control Questionnaire (ACQ) — every visit. ACT ≥20 (well-controlled); ACQ <0.75 (well-controlled). 0 daytime symptoms >2x/wk, 0 night waking, 0 activity limit, reliever ≤2x/wk.12
  • Adherence assessment — every visit. ≥75% adherence; address barriers if lower. Mandatory before step-up or biologic referral.12
  • Trigger and environmental exposure review — every visit; detailed at diagnosis and annually. Tobacco / vaping, occupational, aeroallergens, indoor allergens (dust mite / cockroach / mold / pet), air pollution.21
  • Smoking / vaping status and cessation counseling — every visit.1
  • Asthma action plan review / update — every visit and after every exacerbation. Written, personalized; symptom and / or PEF based; OCS step-up criteria.12
  • Step-up / step-down assessment — every visit. GINA Track 1 ICS-formoterol AIR / MART preferred; reliever overuse screen (≥3 SABA canisters/year flag).12
  • Exacerbation history (OCS bursts, ED visits, hospitalizations, ICU / intubation in past 12 mo) — every visit. ≥1 OCS burst/yr triggers step-up and biologic consideration.1
  • Comorbidity surveillance — at diagnosis and at least annually: allergic rhinitis / CRS with or without nasal polyps, GERD, obesity / BMI, OSA screen (STOP-BANG / Epworth), vocal cord dysfunction / ILO, anxiety / depression.12
  • Vaccination status — annually. Influenza, COVID-19 per CDC, pneumococcal (PCV20 or PCV15 + PPSV23 per ACIP), RSV per ACIP age criteria, Tdap.1
  • Biologic candidacy assessment — when severe / uncontrolled despite high-dose ICS-LABA and confirmed adherence / technique. Mepolizumab, benralizumab, reslizumab, dupilumab, omalizumab, tezepelumab.1
  • Pulmonary rehabilitation referral — as indicated for severe asthma with persistent dyspnea, deconditioning, or post-exacerbation.1
  • Allergen immunotherapy (SCIT) candidacy — once at severe-asthma workup if sensitization drives uncontrolled symptoms. Mild-moderate allergic asthma only; NOT severe.2

Notes

  • Mild asthma reliever. GINA 2025 recommends against SABA-only reliever therapy for all adults / adolescents, preferring as-needed low-dose ICS-formoterol (Track 1, Evidence A). NAEPP 2020 endorses intermittent ICS with as-needed SABA at low steps and SMART at Steps 3–4 — retaining a SABA-reliever pathway not preferred by GINA.12
  • FeNO. NAEPP 2020 explicitly recommends FeNO as an adjunct for diagnostic uncertainty (conditional, moderate certainty) and monitoring (conditional, low certainty). GINA's endorsement is more conditional and centers on phenotyping for biologic selection rather than routine monitoring.21
  • LAMA placement. NAEPP 2020 places LABA before LAMA as ICS add-on at intermediate steps; triple therapy (ICS-LABA-LAMA) is the Step 5 preferred option. GINA 2025 reserves add-on LAMA for Step 4–5 after ICS-LABA optimization.12
  • SLIT. NAEPP 2020 conditionally recommends against SLIT for persistent allergic asthma. SCIT is conditionally endorsed by NAEPP in non-severe disease.2
  • Indoor allergen mitigation. NAEPP 2020 conditionally recommends multicomponent mitigation only when sensitization is documented.2

Footnotes

  1. GINA. Global Strategy for Asthma Management and Prevention, 2025 Update. See bibliography.md → gina-2025. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. Cloutier MM et al. 2020 Focused Updates to the Asthma Management Guidelines. J Allergy Clin Immunol. 2020;146(6):1217-1270. See bibliography.md → naepp-2020. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

Stroke / Transient Ischemic Attack

ICD-10: I63.x (cerebral infarction), I61.x (intracerebral hemorrhage), G45.x (TIA), Z86.73 (personal history of TIA / stroke) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 7.0% — rank 21 of 30 Category: Neurologic Primary associations consulted: AHA/ASA, USPSTF, AAN, ACC/AHA, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — INR at least monthly on warfarin (valvular AF / mechanical valve); lipid panel during titration (4–12 wk post-statin start / change) Lipid panel once stable (q3–12 mo) HbA1c (DM); CBC + LFTs + Cr / eGFR (DOAC) — Diabetes screen (HbA1c or FPG) at baseline; INR weekly during DOAC initiation / change; CBC / LFTs with symptoms
Imaging — — — ECG at baseline post-event — Prolonged cardiac rhythm monitoring (≥30 days within 6 mo of event; longer / ILR if cryptogenic with high suspicion); carotid duplex / CTA / MRA (post-CEA / CAS or known symptomatic stenosis); brain CT / MRI for new neurologic symptoms; carotid screening NOT recommended in asymptomatic adults
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI (BP target <130/80) — — — — —
Functional / surveys Antithrombotic adherence; tobacco use + cessation counseling; alcohol use; physical-activity counseling (≥10 min moderate aerobic 4×/wk or ≥20 min vigorous 2×/wk); fall-risk assessment — — Depression screen (PHQ-9, at minimum annually); cognitive screen (MoCA; before discharge, again in first year, then periodically); influenza vaccination — OSA evaluation with polysomnography; multidisciplinary rehab (PT / OT / speech-language pathology — Medicare Part A inpatient rehab + Part B outpatient); short-course DAPT 21–90 days for minor noncardioembolic ischemic stroke (NIHSS ≤3) or high-risk TIA (ABCD2 ≥4) within 24 h; dysphagia / swallow screen pre-PO; NIHSS / mRS at clinical change

Summary recommendation

Primary-care surveillance of adults with prior ischemic stroke or TIA centers on five pillars set out in the AHA/ASA 2021 Secondary Prevention Guideline (Kleindorfer et al.) and reinforced by the 2021 AHA Primary Care After Stroke scientific statement (Kernan et al.):12 (1) tight vascular risk-factor control — office BP <130/80 mm Hg at every visit, LDL-C <70 mg/dL for atherosclerotic stroke (lipid panel 4–12 weeks after statin change then every 3–12 months), and HbA1c ≤7% if diabetic; (2) appropriate, adherence-monitored antithrombotic therapy — antiplatelet for noncardioembolic disease (with a 21–90-day DAPT window only for minor stroke / high-risk TIA), and a DOAC (preferred) or warfarin (INR 2–3) for AF or other cardioembolic source, with annual CBC / LFT / renal function on DOACs and at-least-monthly INR on warfarin; (3) occult-AF surveillance with baseline ECG and prolonged (≥30 days) cardiac monitoring after cryptogenic stroke; (4) routine post-stroke screening for depression (PHQ-9), cognitive impairment (MoCA), dysphagia, and fall risk, with PT / OT / speech referrals for residual deficits — services covered by Medicare Part A (inpatient rehab) and Part B (outpatient PT / OT / SLP);5 and (5) lifestyle and ancillary measures — smoking cessation, weight reduction, Mediterranean diet, sodium ≤2.3 g/day, alcohol limitation, OSA evaluation / CPAP, and annual influenza vaccination. The first primary-care visit should occur 1–3 weeks after hospital discharge.2 Red flags prompting urgent re-evaluation include any new focal deficit (FAST / BEFAST symptoms), headache with neurologic change, new arrhythmia, bleeding on antithrombotic, or unexplained syncope / falls.

Laboratory

  • Fasting lipid panel (LDL-C) — 4–12 weeks after statin initiation or dose change, then every 3–12 months. Target LDL-C <70 mg/dL for atherosclerotic stroke / TIA.1
  • HbA1c — annually (or per ADA — every 3–6 months until controlled). Target HbA1c ≤7% if diabetic.1
  • Diabetes screen (HbA1c or fasting glucose) — at baseline; periodic thereafter for all post-stroke / TIA without known DM.1
  • INR (warfarin) — at least monthly once stable; more frequent during initiation or changes. Target INR 2.0–3.0.1
  • CBC, hepatic and renal function (Cr / eGFR) — baseline, at least annually, and as clinically indicated for patients on DOAC. Dose adjustment thresholds vary by agent.1

Imaging

  • 12-lead ECG — at baseline post-event for all post-ischemic stroke / TIA (screen for AF and cardiac source).1
  • Prolonged / long-term cardiac rhythm monitoring (mobile cardiac telemetry, event monitor, or implantable loop recorder) — ≥30 days within 6 months of event; longer if cryptogenic with high suspicion (ILR).14
  • Carotid duplex ultrasound (or CTA / MRA) follow-up — as indicated post-CEA / CAS or for known moderate stenosis under medical therapy. Detect restenosis ≥50–70%.1
  • Carotid screening in asymptomatic adults — NOT recommended (USPSTF 2021 Grade D).3
  • Brain imaging (CT / MRI) — as indicated for new neurologic symptoms; rule out recurrent ischemia / hemorrhage.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every visit (initial recheck within 1–3 weeks of discharge, then at least every 3–6 months until at goal, then annually). Target <130/80 mm Hg.1
  • Weight / BMI — every visit. BMI 18.5–24.9; weight loss for overweight / obese.1

Functional / surveys

  • Antithrombotic adherence — every visit. Confirm adherence; reassess for bleeding / recurrence.1
  • Short-course DAPT (aspirin + clopidogrel or aspirin + ticagrelor) — 21–90 days only for minor noncardioembolic ischemic stroke (NIHSS ≤3) or high-risk TIA (ABCD2 ≥4) within 24 hours, then single antiplatelet.1
  • Depression screen (PHQ-9 preferred; PHQ-2 trigger) — at first post-stroke visit and periodically thereafter (at minimum annually). PHQ-9 ≥10 prompts further evaluation / treatment.2
  • Cognitive screen (MoCA or MMSE) — before discharge, again during the first year, periodically thereafter. MoCA <26 suggests impairment, prompts referral.2
  • Dysphagia / swallow screen — before any oral intake post-stroke; reassess if status changes.1
  • NIHSS — at acute presentation and on any new event.1
  • Modified Rankin Scale (mRS) — at 90 days post-event and at follow-up. Global disability tracking.1
  • Multidisciplinary rehabilitation (PT, OT, SLP) — at discharge and serially during recovery; reassess on plateau or worsening. Medicare Part A (inpatient rehab requires tolerance of ≥3 hr therapy/day; SNF up to 100 days after qualifying hospital stay); Medicare Part B (outpatient PT / OT / SLP, with 2025 threshold of $2,410 for PT+SLP combined and $2,410 for OT before targeted medical review).5
  • Fall-risk assessment — at every primary-care visit post-stroke.2
  • Obstructive sleep apnea evaluation (polysomnography if positive screen) — consider for all post-stroke; treat if diagnosed.1
  • Tobacco use / smoking cessation counseling — every visit. Complete cessation; avoid secondhand smoke.1
  • Alcohol use screen and counseling — every visit. ≤2 drinks/day (men), ≤1 drink/day (women); eliminate if heavy use.1
  • Physical-activity counseling — every visit. ≥10 min moderate aerobic 4×/wk or ≥20 min vigorous 2×/wk.1
  • Influenza vaccination — annually.1

Notes

  • BP target. AHA/ASA 2021 specifies <130/80 mm Hg universally (Class 1) for secondary prevention; ACC/AHA 2017 hypertension guideline allows <140/90 mm Hg as fallback when <130/80 is poorly tolerated, particularly with orthostasis, polypharmacy, or limited life expectancy.1
  • LDL target. AHA/ASA 2021 endorses <70 mg/dL (statin ± ezetimibe ± PCSK9 inhibitor) for atherosclerotic stroke / TIA. 2018 ACC/AHA Cholesterol Guideline uses ≥50% LDL reduction with a "consider <70" threshold; for non-atherosclerotic (cardioembolic) stroke, <70 mg/dL is not uniformly applied.1
  • DAPT duration. 21 days (CHANCE) vs. up to 90 days (POINT / THALES). AHA/ASA 2021 endorses 21–90 days — Class 1 for 21-day course; Class 2b for extension to 90 days due to bleeding risk after day 21.1
  • Carotid screening. USPSTF 2021 Grade D against screening in asymptomatic adults; AHA/ASA selectively endorses imaging surveillance after CEA / CAS or for known stenosis.31
  • Occult-AF monitoring duration. AHA/ASA 2021 (Class 2a) says long-term monitoring is "reasonable" without specifying duration; 2024 ACC Expert Consensus on Arrhythmia Monitoring After Stroke recommends ≥30 days minimum, ILR for cryptogenic with high suspicion.4

Footnotes

  1. Kleindorfer DO et al. 2021 Guideline for the Prevention of Stroke in Patients With Stroke and Transient Ischemic Attack. Stroke. 2021;52(7):e364-e467. See bibliography.md → ahaasa-stroke-2021. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. Kernan WN et al. Primary Care of Adult Patients After Stroke: AHA/ASA Scientific Statement. Stroke. 2021;52(9):e558-e571. See bibliography.md → aha-pcas-2021. ↩↩↩↩↩

  3. USPSTF. Screening for Asymptomatic Carotid Artery Stenosis: Recommendation Statement. JAMA. 2021;325(5):476-481 (Grade D). See bibliography.md → uspstf-carotid-2021. ↩↩

  4. Chen LY et al. 2024 ACC Expert Consensus Decision Pathway on Practical Approaches for Arrhythmia Monitoring After Stroke. J Am Coll Cardiol. 2025;85(6):657-681. See bibliography.md → acc-arrh-monitor-2024. ↩↩

  5. CMS. Inpatient Rehabilitation Care + Outpatient Therapy Services coverage. See bibliography.md → cms-stroke-rehab. ↩↩

Alzheimer's Disease

ICD-10: G30.x (Alzheimer's disease); F02.x (dementia in AD) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 2.5% — rank 22 of 30 Category: Neurologic Primary associations consulted: AAN, Alzheimer's Association (DETeCD-ADRD; AUR for lecanemab and donanemab), APA, AGS, USPSTF, CMS

AD shares the dementia surveillance bundle (see Non-Alzheimer's Dementia, #18) and adds an AD-specific layer for anti-amyloid antibody therapy (lecanemab, donanemab) when used.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — Caregiver burden assessment (Zarit) every 6–12 mo TSH, vitamin B12 at diagnosis — APOE genotype before initiating lecanemab / donanemab; amyloid PET or CSF (Aβ42/40, p-tau181/Aβ42) before initiating anti-amyloid; follow-up amyloid PET 12–18 mo after donanemab to consider discontinuation; CBC / CMP for delirium workup
Imaging — — — — — Brain MRI (non-contrast preferred; CT if contraindicated) once at diagnosis; baseline MRI ≤12 mo before anti-amyloid initiation; ARIA surveillance MRI before infusions 3, 5, 7, 14 (lecanemab; APOE4 homozygotes may need additional pre-infusion 26 scan); before infusions 2, 3, 4, 7 (and 12 for higher-risk) for donanemab; PRN MRI for any new neurologic symptoms
Physical exam Beers Criteria medication review; antipsychotic appropriateness review (avoid in DLB; in AD use lowest effective dose, taper within 4 mo) — — Fall risk (Timed Up & Go); driving safety (CDR-based); home safety — —
Functional / surveys NPI-Q neuropsychiatric symptom screen; vascular risk-factor control; cholinesterase inhibitor / memantine efficacy + tolerability review — — Cognitive testing (MoCA / Mini-Cog / MMSE / SLUMS); ADL / IADL (Katz / Lawton-Brody); FAST or CDR staging; depression screen (GDS-15 or PHQ-9); advance care planning; CMS CPT 99483 Cognitive Assessment & Care Plan (every 180 days); exercise prescription (~twice weekly aerobic) — CMS Monoclonal Antibodies Registry submission with each anti-amyloid infusion encounter (NCD 200.3 Coverage with Evidence Development); GUIDE Model enrollment for community-dwelling patients (caregiver navigator, 24/7 helpline, respite up to $2,500/yr)

Summary recommendation

AD surveillance layers AD-specific elements on top of the dementia bundle described in Non-Alzheimer's Dementia (#18). The Alzheimer's Association DETeCD-ADRD 2024 guideline and AAN 2001 dementia parameter (reaffirmed 2024) define the diagnostic workup (Tier 1 labs including TSH and B12, structural MRI / CT) and recommend serial cognitive-functional assessment using validated tools (MoCA, MMSE, Mini-Cog), staged with FAST or CDR.12 CMS CPT 99483 operationalizes the care-plan visit in primary care: cognition-focused exam, ADL / IADL, FAST or CDR staging, NPI-Q-type behavioral screen, high-risk medication reconciliation under AGS Beers 2023, home and driving safety, caregiver needs, and advance care planning — billable up to every 180 days.1210 Anti-amyloid antibodies (lecanemab, donanemab) add a separate, intensive surveillance layer: eligibility is restricted to early symptomatic AD (MCI due to AD or mild AD dementia, MMSE 20–30) with amyloid confirmation by PET or CSF; APOE genotyping is performed at baseline to risk-stratify ARIA (~32% ARIA risk for lecanemab in APOE4 homozygotes).56 Baseline MRI within 12 months excludes patients with >4 microbleeds or any superficial siderosis. Surveillance MRI for ARIA is scheduled before infusions 3, 5, 7, and 14 for lecanemab (updated FDA label 2024) and before infusions 2, 3, 4, and 7 (plus 12 for higher-risk) for donanemab.76 Medicare covers therapy only under CMS NCD 200.3 Coverage with Evidence Development, requiring registry participation and submission of cognitive, functional, ARIA, and APOE data.8 Cholinesterase inhibitors and memantine require periodic review for benefit and deprescribing in end-stage disease.

Laboratory

  • TSH, vitamin B12 — at diagnosis (Tier 1 labs); repeat as clinically indicated.12
  • APOE genotyping — once, before initiating lecanemab or donanemab. Homozygotes have ~32% ARIA risk for lecanemab.56
  • Amyloid PET or CSF biomarkers (Aβ42/40, p-tau181 / Aβ42) — once, before initiating anti-amyloid therapy.85
  • Follow-up amyloid PET — 12–18 months after donanemab initiation; consider discontinuation if cleared.6

Imaging

  • Brain MRI (non-contrast preferred; CT if contraindicated) — once at diagnosis. Repeat only with acute change.21
  • Brain MRI (FLAIR, T2* / SWI, DWI) for ARIA surveillance — lecanemab — baseline within 12 months pre-treatment; before 3rd, 5th, 7th, and 14th infusions (FDA label update 2024); additional MRI in APOE4 homozygotes (e.g., before infusion 26); any new neurologic symptoms.75
  • Brain MRI for ARIA surveillance — donanemab — baseline; before 2nd, 3rd, 4th, and 7th infusions (and before 12th in higher-risk); PRN for symptoms.6

Physical examination

  • Beers Criteria medication reconciliation — every visit (CPT 99483 required). Avoid / deprescribe anticholinergics, benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, first-generation antihistamines; address cumulative anticholinergic burden.1012
  • Antipsychotic appropriateness review — every visit if on agent. Lowest effective dose; 4-week response review; periodic deprescribing trial.910
  • Fall-risk assessment (Timed Up & Go) — annually.31
  • Driving safety evaluation (CDR-based) — at diagnosis and annually. CDR ≥1 = high risk.4
  • Home safety evaluation — at diagnosis and each follow-up (CPT 99483 required).12

Functional / surveys

  • Cognitive testing (MoCA, MMSE, Mini-Cog, SLUMS; CDR-SB for anti-amyloid trial outcome tracking) — at diagnosis and annually (every visit if change reported). MMSE 22–30 = MCI / mild AD eligibility threshold for anti-amyloid.312
  • ADL (Katz) and IADL (Lawton-Brody) — annually; required at CPT 99483.123
  • Dementia staging (FAST or CDR / CDR-SB) — annually.12
  • NPI-Q neuropsychiatric / behavioral symptom screen — every visit (required at CPT 99483).129
  • Depression screen (GDS-15 or PHQ-9) — annually.31
  • Caregiver assessment + Zarit Burden — at diagnosis and at least annually; ongoing under GUIDE.1213
  • Advance care planning — at diagnosis; revisit annually and at stage transitions.121
  • Cholinesterase inhibitor / memantine efficacy + tolerability review — each follow-up; consider deprescribing trial in severe / end-stage AD or lack of benefit.14
  • Lifestyle counseling (aerobic exercise ~twice weekly, BP control, hearing / vision, sleep, social engagement) — ongoing.3
  • CMS Monoclonal Antibodies Registry (anti-amyloid) — submission with each infusion encounter and at follow-up cognitive / functional assessments. CMS NCD 200.3 Coverage with Evidence Development requirement.8
  • GUIDE Model engagement — ongoing care-navigator services, training, 24/7 helpline, respite (up to $2,500/yr) for Medicare beneficiaries with dementia and unpaid caregivers.13

Notes

  • Anti-amyloid coverage gaps. CMS NCD 200.3 requires registry participation and amyloid biomarker confirmation; APOE testing reimbursement is uneven, creating access gaps.85
  • Pre-MCI biomarker testing. 2024 NIA-AA revised research framework defines AD biologically (allowing diagnosis in asymptomatic biomarker-positive individuals); DETeCD-ADRD 2024, AA AUR, and CMS coverage restrict biomarker testing and treatment to symptomatic patients (MCI or mild dementia). USPSTF (2020, Grade I) finds insufficient evidence for cognitive screening of asymptomatic older adults.111
  • Cholinesterase inhibitor duration in severe AD. Deprescribing guidelines support trial discontinuation in severe / end-stage AD; many US clinicians continue indefinitely.14
  • Antipsychotics in dementia. FDA black-box warning (increased mortality) coexists with APA conditional recommendations for severe symptoms; AGS Beers 2023 lists antipsychotics as "avoid" unless non-pharmacologic options fail.910

Parkinson's Disease

ICD-10: G20 (Parkinson's disease) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.9% — rank 23 of 30 Category: Neurologic Primary associations consulted: AAN, Movement Disorder Society (MDS), Parkinson's Foundation, AGS, AAS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — DBS programming review, battery check, neuropsych re-evaluation (DBS patients) — — Genetic testing + counseling via PD GENEration (Parkinson's Foundation; offered to all PD patients regardless of family history)
Imaging — — — — — Brain MRI if atypical parkinsonism features; PSG to confirm RBD if treatment considered
Physical exam MDS-UPDRS Parts I–IV (non-motor, motor experiences, motor exam, motor complications); Hoehn & Yahr stage; orthostatic BP (supine + 3-min standing) if symptomatic or on dopaminergic Rx; dopaminergic-regimen review for motor complications (wearing-off, dyskinesia, on-off); avoidance of dopamine-blocking medications (typical / atypical antipsychotics except quetiapine / clozapine / pimavanserin; metoclopramide; prochlorperazine) — — Fall-risk assessment (TUG, pull test); orthostatic BP (annual minimum); dental / oral exam (sialorrhea, dysphagia complication) — Driving safety evaluation (CDR / on-road); referral for driving rehab if MoCA <22 or significant motor impairment
Functional / surveys Psychotic symptoms (visual hallucinations, delusions); impulse-control disorder (QUIP-RS — mandatory on any dopamine agonist); fall query; medication-side-effect / adherence review; advance care planning (intensify with cognitive decline or H&Y ≥4); exercise / PT counseling — — Annual PD diagnosis review (re-confirm against MDS clinical diagnostic criteria); cognitive screen (MoCA preferred; MMSE acceptable); psychiatric symptoms (depression, anxiety, apathy — PHQ-9 / GDS); sleep disturbances (RBD via RBDSQ, ESS, insomnia, RLS); autonomic dysfunction (SCOPA-AUT / NMSQ); swallow / dysphagia (EAT-10); speech / hypophonia (refer SLP for LSVT-LOUD); GI motility / constipation; vaccination status (influenza annual, pneumococcal, RSV ≥60, COVID-19, Tdap, zoster ≥50) — Multidisciplinary rehab — PT, OT, SLP referral (initiate at diagnosis; reassess annually); pimavanserin (Level B AAN) or quetiapine / clozapine for PD psychosis; impulse-control disorder management → reduce / taper dopamine agonist

Summary recommendation

US guideline-aligned surveillance of established Parkinson's Disease centers on a structured visit cadence — every 6 months early, every 3 months with motor fluctuations or new non-motor symptoms, and more frequently in advanced or DBS-managed disease.12 Every visit should include a focused motor exam (MDS-UPDRS Parts III / IV, Hoehn & Yahr) plus structured non-motor screening: falls, psychotic symptoms, and (mandatory on any dopamine agonist) an impulse-control disorder check using QUIP-RS.16 At least annually clinicians should reconfirm the MDS clinical diagnosis, screen cognition (MoCA), mood (PHQ-9), sleep including RBD, autonomic function with a 3-minute orthostatic BP, and dysphagia / speech, and reassess advance care planning, driving safety, and the exercise prescription.15 Medication review at each visit must address motor complications (wearing-off, dyskinesia), screen for dopamine-blocking drugs that worsen PD, and (in adults ≥65) apply the 2023 AGS Beers Criteria.11 Multidisciplinary referral to PT, OT, and SLP is endorsed at diagnosis and reassessed annually. Vaccination should follow ACIP, with prioritization of influenza, pneumococcal, RSV, and COVID-19 given aspiration risk.9 Genetic testing + counseling via PD GENEration is now offered to every person with PD irrespective of family history.10

Laboratory

No routine laboratory monitoring is required for PD surveillance.

Imaging

  • Brain MRI — if atypical parkinsonism features or to rule out alternative diagnoses.3
  • Polysomnography (video PSG) — to confirm RBD if treatment considered.3

Physical examination

  • MDS-UPDRS Parts I–IV (non-motor experiences, motor experiences, motor exam, motor complications) — every visit. Hoehn & Yahr stage informs prognosis / rehab.4
  • Orthostatic blood pressure (supine + 3-min standing) — every visit if symptomatic or on dopaminergic Rx; at least annually otherwise. SBP drop ≥20 mm Hg or DBP ≥10 mm Hg at 3 min = orthostatic hypotension.8
  • Dopaminergic-regimen review for motor complications (wearing-off, dyskinesia, on-off) — every visit. Adjust dosing; consider adjunct (entacapone, opicapone, safinamide, istradefylline).2
  • Avoidance of dopamine-blocking medications — every visit. Avoid typical / atypical antipsychotics except quetiapine, clozapine, pimavanserin; avoid metoclopramide and prochlorperazine (use ondansetron for nausea).111
  • Fall-risk assessment (TUG, pull test) — every visit; annual structured reassessment minimum.15
  • Dental / oral exam — every 6 months.9
  • Driving safety evaluation — annually or with motor / cognitive change. Refer for driving rehab if MoCA <22 or significant motor impairment.1

Functional / surveys

  • Annual PD diagnosis review — re-confirm against MDS clinical diagnostic criteria; reassess for parkinsonism-plus features.13
  • Cognitive screen (MoCA preferred; MMSE acceptable) — at least annually. MoCA <26 triggers further evaluation; consider rivastigmine if PD dementia (Level B AAN).1
  • Psychiatric symptoms (depression, anxiety, apathy — PHQ-9, GDS, BDI-II) — at least annually; every visit if symptomatic. Consider SSRI / SNRI.1
  • Psychotic symptoms (visual hallucinations, delusions) — every visit. Pimavanserin (Level B AAN; FDA-approved PD psychosis / dementia); quetiapine / clozapine acceptable per Beers.211
  • Impulse-control disorder screen (QUIP-RS) — every visit; mandatory on any dopamine agonist (pramipexole, ropinirole, rotigotine); also high-dose levodopa, MAO-B. Positive screen → reduce / taper dopamine agonist.26
  • Sleep disturbances (RBD via RBDSQ; ESS for excessive daytime sleepiness; insomnia, RLS) — at least annually.1
  • Autonomic dysfunction (SCOPA-AUT / NMSQ — constipation, urinary, sexual, sweating, sialorrhea) — at least annually.8
  • Swallow / dysphagia (EAT-10; refer SLP for VFSS if positive) — annually; sooner with weight loss, choking, aspiration.5
  • Speech / hypophonia — annually. Refer SLP (LSVT-LOUD) for speech changes.5
  • GI motility / constipation / gastroparesis — annually; every visit if symptomatic.7
  • Medication review with Beers Criteria — annually in adults ≥65.11
  • Exercise / regular aerobic + resistance + balance regimen counseling — every visit. Target ≥150 min/wk moderate aerobic; PT-supervised if balance impaired.15
  • Multidisciplinary rehab — PT, OT, SLP — initiate at diagnosis; reassess annually.15
  • Advance care planning / goals of care — at diagnosis and at least annually; intensify with cognitive decline or H&Y ≥4.1
  • Vaccination status (influenza, pneumococcal, RSV ≥60, COVID-19, Tdap, zoster ≥50) — per CDC ACIP; aspiration-risk patients prioritized.9
  • Genetic testing (LRRK2, GBA, SNCA, PINK1, PRKN, DJ1, VPS35) + counseling — once at / after diagnosis. PD GENEration offers testing to all PD patients regardless of family history.10
  • DBS programming review, battery check, neuropsych re-evaluation — every 6–12 months post-implant.2

Notes

  • Genetic testing. Parkinson's Foundation (PD GENEration, 2024) recommends offering genetic testing to all PD patients; older AAN / MDS positions reserved testing for early-onset, familial, or specific ancestral populations.10
  • Cognitive enhancers. AAN / FDA support rivastigmine for PD dementia (Level B); donepezil and galantamine are widely used off-label but lack the same evidence grade.1
  • Diagnostic criteria. MDS 2015 Clinical Diagnostic Criteria (Postuma) have largely replaced the older UK Brain Bank criteria.3
  • Antipsychotics in PD psychosis. AAN endorses pimavanserin (Level B) and clozapine (Level B); quetiapine remains widely used despite weaker evidence. AGS Beers 2023 lists antipsychotics as potentially inappropriate but exempts clozapine, pimavanserin, and quetiapine in PD.211
  • Orthostatic protocol. AAS / National Parkinson Foundation consensus uses supine-to-stand 3-minute protocol; some practices substitute sit-to-stand, which under-detects neurogenic OH.8

Colorectal Cancer

ICD-10: C18.x (colon), C19 (rectosigmoid), C20 (rectum) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.5% — rank 24 of 30 Category: Cancer Primary associations consulted: NCCN, ASCRS, ASCO/ACS Survivorship, USPSTF, ACG

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — CEA every 3–6 mo × 2 yr (stage II/III; T2 or higher) CEA every 6 mo through yr 5 (stage II/III); discontinue after 5 yr stable — — CEA NOT routine for stage I; ctDNA emerging (not yet routine)
Imaging — — CT chest / abdomen / pelvis every 6–12 mo × 5 yr (stage II/III/IV); MRI pelvis every 6 mo × 3 yr (rectal cancer, particularly watch-and-wait); proctoscopy / flexible sigmoidoscopy q3–6 mo × 2 yr after rectal transanal excision or nonoperative management CT chest / abd / pelvis annually × 5 yr (stage I-III ACS recommendation); chest CT alternative; PET / CT NOT recommended for routine surveillance Surveillance colonoscopy at 1 yr post-resection (or 3–6 mo postoperatively if pre-op exam incomplete); repeat at 3 yr if normal; then every 5 yr —
Physical exam — H&P every 3–6 mo × 2 yr (stage II/III) H&P every 6 mo through yr 5 H&P annually after yr 5 (stage I-III NED); ostomy site assessment if applicable; peripheral neuropathy / CIPN (post-oxaliplatin) — —
Functional / surveys — — Distress / depression / anxiety screen (NCCN Distress Thermometer, PHQ-9, HADS) Lifestyle counseling (≥150 min/wk activity, BMI, diet, alcohol ≤1 drink/day, smoking cessation); age-appropriate second-cancer screening per USPSTF; Lynch syndrome surveillance if applicable (annual endometrial biopsy + TVUS starting age 35; upper endoscopy; urinary tract); financial / psychosocial assessment — Fatigue screening, sexual function / fertility counseling, ostomy care WOC referral, bowel dysfunction / LARS / proctitis management, CIPN — duloxetine for painful CIPN; survivorship care plan within 1 yr of treatment completion

Summary recommendation

For curatively-resected stage II/III colon and rectal cancer survivors — the dominant CCW Medicare CRC cohort — convergent NCCN v4.2025 (colon) / v2025 (rectal), ASCRS 2021, and ASCO / ACS (El-Shami 2015) guidance specifies: history and physical examination every 3–6 months for the first 2 years and every 6 months through year 5, with paired serum CEA on the same cadence; CT chest / abdomen / pelvis every 6–12 months for 5 years (NCCN / ASCRS) or annually × 5 years (ACS), reserved for patients who would be candidates for further intervention; surveillance colonoscopy at 1 year post-resection (or 3–6 months postoperatively if a complete pre-op exam was not feasible), repeated at year 3 and then every 5 years if normal; rectal-specific pelvic MRI every 6 months for up to 3 years (especially watch-and-wait or high-risk) and proctoscopy / flexible sigmoidoscopy at 3–6 month intervals after transanal excision or nonoperative management.123 Beyond tumor surveillance, guidelines require structured screening for distress / depression, oxaliplatin-induced peripheral neuropathy (offer duloxetine for painful CIPN), bowel dysfunction / LARS, ostomy care, sexual function, fertility (reproductive-age), lifestyle counseling, cardiovascular risk, age-appropriate second-cancer screening (and Lynch-specific surveillance if applicable), and a documented survivorship care plan transitioning to coordinated primary-care follow-up.3 Stage I disease is monitored less intensively (colonoscopy plus H&P; routine CT and CEA generally not required); stage IV NED after metastasectomy is monitored more intensively (CEA and CT every 3–6 months × 2 years).1

Laboratory

  • CEA (carcinoembryonic antigen, serum) — every 3–6 months × 2 years, then every 6 months through year 5 for stage II / III colon and rectal (T2 or higher) candidates for further intervention. Trend over baseline; investigate persistent rise. Discontinue after 5 years (ASCO / ACS) or stage I (NCCN — not routine).123

Imaging

  • CT chest / abdomen / pelvis (contrast-enhanced) — every 6–12 months × up to 5 years for stage II/III/IV colon and high-risk stage II/III rectal (NCCN / ASCRS); annually × 5 years (ASCO / ACS alternative). Detection of resectable metastases.123
  • CT chest / abd / pelvis (intensified) — every 3–6 months × 2 years for stage IV NED post-metastasectomy, then every 6–12 months through year 5.1
  • Surveillance colonoscopy — at 1 year post-resection (or 3–6 months postoperatively if obstructing primary precluded preoperative scope); repeat at 3 years if normal; then every 5 years. All stages I-III post-resection.123
  • MRI pelvis — every 6 months × up to 3 years (then CT pelvis replaces) for rectal cancer, particularly post-nonoperative ("watch-and-wait") management or high-risk locally advanced.1
  • Proctoscopy / flexible sigmoidoscopy (± endorectal ultrasound) — every 3–4 months × 2 years then every 6 months × 5 years (nonoperative management); every 6 months × 3–5 years after transanal excision.12
  • PET-CT — NOT recommended for routine surveillance.123

Physical examination

  • History and physical exam — every 3–6 months × 2 years; every 6 months through year 5; annually thereafter.123
  • Ostomy care — every visit; intensive in first 6–12 months; refer to WOC nurse.3
  • Peripheral neuropathy / CIPN assessment — every visit during / after oxaliplatin-containing therapy.3

Functional / surveys

  • Distress / depression / anxiety screening — at each follow-up visit; minimum annually (NCCN Distress Thermometer, PHQ-9, HADS, CES-D).3
  • Fatigue screening — at each visit; counsel on physical activity.3
  • Bowel / GI dysfunction (LARS, diarrhea, incontinence, chronic proctitis) — ongoing; pharmacologic and dietary management; GI referral if persistent.3
  • Sexual function — ongoing in rectal cancer and pelvic-radiated survivors. Offer PDE-5 inhibitors, vaginal moisturizers / lubricants.3
  • Fertility counseling — pre-treatment and at survivorship visits for reproductive-age survivors.3
  • Lifestyle counseling — every visit. Physical activity ≥150 min/wk moderate; BMI 18.5–24.9; diet high in fruits / vegetables / whole grains, low in red / processed meat; alcohol ≤1 drink/day; smoking cessation.3
  • Cardiovascular monitoring — per primary-care guidelines; heightened in those with cardiac history. Awareness of 5-FU / capecitabine cardiotoxicity.3
  • Age-appropriate second-cancer screening — per USPSTF / ACS (breast, cervical, prostate shared decision, lung if eligible, skin).3
  • Lynch syndrome surveillance — annual endometrial biopsy + transvaginal ultrasound starting age 35; upper endoscopy; urinary tract surveillance. Confirmed Lynch syndrome carriers.3
  • Survivorship care plan — written treatment summary + follow-up plan + late-effect information; within 1 year of treatment completion.23
  • Financial / psychosocial assessment — at survivorship visits.3

Notes

  • CT frequency. NCCN and ASCRS endorse CT C/A/P every 6–12 months × 5 years for stage II/III; ASCO / ACS calls for annual CT × 5 years; ASCRS will accept as few as two CTs (at 12 and 36 months) in lower-risk stage II.123
  • CEA utility / frequency. NCCN / ASCRS retain CEA every 3–6 months × 2 years then every 6 months; ASCO / ACS stops at 5 years; emerging European trial data (PRODIGE-13 / FACS / COLOFOL) question survival benefit of intensive CEA + CT, but US guidelines have not adopted reduced intensity.13
  • CT for stage II low-risk. ASCO / ACS and ASCRS allow less intensive imaging when recurrence risk is low (e.g., T3N0 without high-risk pathology); NCCN treats stage II and III similarly.3
  • Rectal pelvic MRI cadence. NCCN recommends every 6 months × 3 years (particularly nonoperative management); ASCRS does not mandate routine MRI surveillance and accepts CT alone for most resected patients.12
  • Routine colonoscopy after polypectomy vs. cancer resection. US Multi-Society Task Force surveillance intervals after polypectomy (3–10 years depending on findings) are distinct from the 1-year-then-3-year-then-5-year post-cancer-resection schedule. Mixing the two is a common primary-care error.1

Footnotes

  1. NCCN. Clinical Practice Guidelines in Oncology: Colon Cancer v4.2025 and Rectal Cancer v2025. See bibliography.md → nccn-crc-2025. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  2. Vogel JD et al. The American Society of Colon and Rectal Surgeons Clinical Practice Guidelines for Surveillance and Survivorship Care of Patients After Curative Treatment of Colon and Rectal Cancer. Dis Colon Rectum. 2022;65(2):148-177. See bibliography.md → ascrs-crc-2021. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

  3. El-Shami K et al. American Cancer Society Colorectal Cancer Survivorship Care Guidelines (ASCO endorsement). CA Cancer J Clin. 2015;65(6):428-455. See bibliography.md → ascoacs-crc-2015. ↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩↩

Acute Myocardial Infarction

ICD-10: I21.x (acute MI), I22.x (subsequent MI), Z86.711 (personal history of MI) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.3% — rank 25 of 30 Category: Cardiovascular Primary associations consulted: AHA/ACC/SCAI/NAEMSP, 2023 CCD Guideline, CMS

Post-MI surveillance overlaps substantially with Ischemic Heart Disease (#7). This monograph focuses on the acute / subacute and first-year phase. After ~12 months post-event, surveillance generally follows the 2023 CCD framework.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — Lipid panel 4–8 weeks after statin initiation / change, then every 3–12 months — Lipid panel (stable, target LDL <70; <55 very high risk); HbA1c (if DM); influenza + COVID-19 + RSV vaccination review — Troponin / ECG with new or changing symptoms (NOT routine surveillance — Class 3 No Benefit for routine)
Imaging — — — — — Echocardiogram for LVEF reassessment ~40 days post-MI (and ≥90 days post-revasc) if initial LVEF reduced, to guide ICD decision; reassess with clinical change
Physical exam BP, weight / BMI, physical activity — — — — —
Functional / surveys Adherence — aspirin 81 mg + P2Y12 inhibitor (ticagrelor / prasugrel preferred over clopidogrel) through DAPT window; β-blocker (≥1 year minimum; indefinitely if LVEF <50% / angina / arrhythmia / HTN); ACEi / ARB / ARNI (LVEF ≤40% or HTN / DM / CKD); SGLT2i (LVEF ≤40% or T2DM); MRA (LVEF ≤40% + HF / DM); GLP-1 RA if T2DM or BMI ≥27; low-dose colchicine 0.5 mg daily (selected residual inflammatory risk, Class 2b); tobacco cessation; Mediterranean / DASH / low-sodium diet Clinical follow-up visit every 4–6 months in first year, then ≥annually — PHQ-2 / PHQ-9 depression screen; alcohol; physical-activity counseling (150 min/wk moderate or 75 min/wk vigorous + resistance 2×/wk) — Cardiac rehabilitation referral pre-discharge (CMS NCD 20.10: 36 sessions over 12 weeks within 12 mo post-AMI; extendable to 72 if comorbid event); ICD evaluation ≥40 days post-MI AND ≥90 days post-revascularization if LVEF ≤35% NYHA II–III (or ≤30% any class) after GDMT optimization

Summary recommendation

Post-MI ambulatory surveillance is most intensive in the first 30–90 days, when guidelines emphasize cardiac rehab enrollment (CMS NCD 20.10 covers 36 sessions in 12 weeks within the 12 months post-AMI), rapid GDMT titration to target doses within 3 months, and a fasting lipid panel 4–8 weeks after statin initiation.126 After this stabilization phase, visits typically drop to every 4–6 months in year one, then at least annually if stable. DAPT (aspirin 81 mg + ticagrelor or prasugrel preferred) is continued ≥12 months post-ACS (Class I); ticagrelor monotherapy after 1+ month is a Class 2a option in high bleeding risk.1 After 12 months, aspirin monotherapy is the chronic standard. High-intensity statin is lifelong with LDL-C target <70 mg/dL (Class I) and <55 mg/dL for very-high-risk patients, escalating to ezetimibe, PCSK9 inhibitors, inclisiran, or bempedoic acid as needed.13 β-blockers are recommended for at least 1 year post-MI and indefinitely if LVEF ≤50%, angina, arrhythmia, or HTN coexist (the 2023 CCD guideline made indefinite continuation without these indications Class 2b).2 ACEi / ARB and SGLT2 inhibitors are Class I for post-MI patients with LVEF ≤40%; MRA is added when HF symptoms or diabetes coexist.24 ICD evaluation occurs ≥40 days post-MI (and ≥90 days post-revascularization) only after full GDMT optimization, with echo to reconfirm LVEF ≤35% (NYHA II–III) or ≤30%.1 BP target is <130/80, A1c per ADA, and every visit should address PHQ-2 depression screening, tobacco cessation, alcohol use, weight, physical activity, Mediterranean-style diet, and annual influenza / COVID / RSV vaccinations.

Laboratory

  • Fasting lipid panel — 4–8 weeks after statin initiation / dose change, then every 3–12 months. Target LDL-C <70 mg/dL (Class I); <55 mg/dL for very-high-risk ASCVD.13
  • HbA1c (if diabetic) — every 3–6 months. Individualized per ADA (generally <7%).2
  • Troponin / ECG — only with new / changing symptoms; routine surveillance NOT recommended (Class 3 No Benefit).2

Imaging

  • Echocardiogram for LVEF reassessment — ~40 days post-MI (and ≥90 days post-revasc) if initial LVEF reduced, to guide ICD decision; reassess with clinical change.1

Physical examination

  • Blood pressure — every visit. Target <130/80 mm Hg.2
  • Weight / BMI — each visit. BMI 18.5–24.9; consider GLP-1 RA if BMI ≥27 with comorbidity.2
  • Physical activity counseling — each visit.2

Functional / surveys

  • DAPT (aspirin 81 mg + P2Y12 inhibitor — ticagrelor / prasugrel preferred over clopidogrel) — ≥12 months post-ACS (low bleeding risk). Class I.1
  • DAPT de-escalation to ticagrelor monotherapy — ≥1 month post-PCI if tolerated, in high bleeding risk. Class 2a.1
  • Aspirin 75–100 mg monotherapy — indefinitely after DAPT window. Class I.2
  • High-intensity statin (atorvastatin 80 / rosuvastatin 20–40) — indefinitely; add ezetimibe / PCSK9i / inclisiran / bempedoic acid if LDL ≥70 mg/dL. Target LDL <70; <55 if very high-risk.13
  • β-blocker — continue ≥1 year post-MI; re-evaluate at 1 year. Continue indefinitely only if LVEF <50%, angina, arrhythmia, or HTN (Class 2b for continuation beyond 1 yr without other indication; Class I if LVEF <50%).2
  • ACEi / ARB (or ARNI if HFrEF) — indefinitely for post-MI with LVEF ≤40%, HTN, DM, or CKD. Class I; titrate to maximum tolerated.2
  • SGLT2 inhibitor (empagliflozin / dapagliflozin) — indefinitely for post-MI with LVEF ≤40% (regardless of DM) or T2DM with CCD. Class I.24
  • GLP-1 RA — as indicated for post-MI with T2DM or obesity. Class I.2
  • MRA (spironolactone / eplerenone) — indefinitely for post-MI with LVEF ≤40% + HF / DM. Class I.1
  • ICD evaluation — ≥40 days post-MI AND ≥90 days post-revascularization, after GDMT optimized. LVEF ≤35% with NYHA II–III (or ≤30% any class) and expected survival >1 yr. Class I.15
  • Cardiac rehabilitation referral — enroll prior to discharge; 36 sessions over 12 weeks (Medicare NCD 20.10); extendable to 72 if comorbid event. All post-MI, post-PCI, post-CABG (within 12 months). Class I.61
  • Depression screen (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) — at follow-up visits. Treat if positive.2
  • Tobacco cessation counseling + pharmacotherapy — every visit.12
  • Alcohol screening and counseling — ≤1–2 drinks/day. Class 2a.2
  • Mediterranean / DASH diet, low sodium — counseling at visits. Class I.2
  • Influenza, COVID-19, RSV vaccination — annual flu; per CDC schedule for others.2
  • Low-dose colchicine 0.5 mg daily — indefinite in selected patients with residual inflammatory risk post-MI. Class 2b.12
  • Clinical follow-up visit — every 4–6 months in first year; then ≥annually.2

Notes

  • β-blocker indefinite duration. 2023 CCD guideline downgrades indefinite β-blockade after 1 year post-MI to Class 2b in patients with preserved LVEF and no other indication (REDUCE-AMI, REBOOT data). The 2025 ACS guideline aligns with the more conservative 2023 stance.21
  • DAPT shortening / P2Y12 monotherapy. 2025 ACS guideline endorses ticagrelor monotherapy ≥1 month post-PCI (Class 2a) for high bleeding risk; ESC permits even shorter (3-month) DAPT than ACC / AHA.1
  • Aspirin vs. P2Y12 monotherapy after DAPT window. 2023 CCD keeps aspirin 75–100 mg as default chronic monotherapy (Class I); emerging trial data (HOST-EXAM, PANTHER) suggest clopidogrel monotherapy may be superior — guidelines have not yet adopted.2
  • ICD timing. 40-day post-MI / 90-day post-revascularization waiting periods are retained despite trial data suggesting earlier wearable defibrillator bridging in selected patients.1
  • LDL target stringency. ACC / AHA Class I threshold to add non-statins is LDL ≥70 mg/dL with a <55 mg/dL goal for very-high-risk; ESC sets a universal <55 mg/dL target post-ACS.31

Footnotes

  3. 2026 ACC/AHA/Multisociety Guideline on the Management of Dyslipidemia. Circulation. 2026. See bibliography.md → accaha-dyslip-2026. ↩↩↩↩

  4. Maddox TM et al. 2024 ACC Expert Consensus Decision Pathway for Treatment of HFrEF. J Am Coll Cardiol. 2024. See bibliography.md → acc-hfref-ecdp-2024. ↩↩

Pneumonia (All-Cause)

ICD-10: J12-J18 (bacterial / viral / unspecified pneumonia) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.3% — rank 26 of 30 Category: Respiratory Primary associations consulted: IDSA / ATS, CDC ACIP, USPSTF

CCW classifies pneumonia as a chronic-condition rubric reflecting recurrent / persistent disease burden in older adults. This monograph covers post-CAP surveillance, recurrent pneumonia workup, and vaccination-based prevention.

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — Pneumococcal vaccination status (PCV20 or PCV21 single dose, or PCV15 followed ≥1 yr later by PPSV23 — adults ≥50 / risk-based 19-49); influenza vaccine annually; RSV ≥75 (or 50–74 with risk); COVID-19 per CDC; Tdap — Blood cultures × 2 (severe CAP / MRSA / Pseudomonas risk / hospitalization with parenteral antibiotics in past 90 days); sputum Gram stain + culture (same severe CAP indications); urinary S. pneumoniae and Legionella antigens (severe CAP); procalcitonin (NOT to initiate antibiotics; conditional for de-escalation / duration); recurrent-pneumonia workup labs (serum immunoglobulins IgG/IgA/IgM + IgG subclasses, HIV test)
Imaging — — — — — Follow-up chest x-ray at 6–8 wk in selected patients (persistent symptoms, smokers ≥50 eligible for lung-cancer screening, recurrent same-lobe pneumonia — CT preferred); chest CT for structural lung disease / bronchiectasis / malignancy / sequestration; routine repeat CXR NOT recommended in patients improving within 5–7 days
Physical exam — — — BMI / nutritional status; COPD optimization — Pulse oximetry / SpO2 (acute); arterial blood gas if SpO2 ≤92% or for LTOT qualification; swallow evaluation for aspiration; delirium screening during hospitalization
Functional / surveys Smoking-cessation counseling — — Vaccination status review (comprehensive); cognitive change post-pneumonia surveillance in older adults — Post-CAP clinical resolution visit 1–2 weeks after diagnosis; recurrent-pneumonia (≥2 episodes/yr or 3 lifetime) workup — immunodeficiency assessment, structural lung disease, aspiration evaluation

Summary recommendation

Per the 2019 IDSA / ATS CAP guideline (Metlay et al.), management of adults diagnosed with community-acquired pneumonia centers on a clinical resolution follow-up visit at approximately 1–2 weeks; routine follow-up chest x-ray is NOT recommended for patients improving by 5–7 days, but selective imaging at 6–8 weeks is appropriate in patients with persistent symptoms, smokers eligible for lung cancer screening, or recurrent same-lobe pneumonia.1 Routine sputum cultures, blood cultures, and pneumococcal / Legionella urinary antigens are reserved for severe CAP or specific risk groups, not outpatient CAP. Procalcitonin is NOT recommended to initiate antibiotics but may guide de-escalation / duration. Prevention rests on CDC / ACIP vaccinations: a single dose of PCV15, PCV20, or PCV21 for all PCV-naïve adults ≥50 years (lowered from ≥65 in October 2024), with PPSV23 ≥1 year later only if PCV15 was chosen; risk-based pneumococcal vaccination applies to adults 19–49 with chronic heart / lung / liver / kidney disease, diabetes, immunocompromise, alcoholism, or smoking.2 Annual influenza vaccination is universal (HD / adjuvanted / recombinant preferred ≥65 years). RSV vaccination is a single non-annual dose for all adults ≥75 and for adults 50–74 with risk conditions (expanded April 2025).4 COVID-19 vaccination for 2025–26 moved to shared clinical decision-making. Smoking cessation counseling is integral. Recurrent pneumonia (≥2 episodes/year) warrants workup for immunodeficiency (immunoglobulin levels, HIV), structural lung disease (chest CT, bronchoscopy), and aspiration. Older adults hospitalized for CAP have substantial post-discharge cognitive / functional decline risk and warrant delirium screening and post-discharge cognitive surveillance.

Laboratory

  • Blood cultures (× 2 sets) — at admission for hospitalized CAP — severe CAP, empirically treated for MRSA / Pseudomonas, prior MRSA / Pseudomonas infection, hospitalization with parenteral antibiotics in past 90 days.1
  • Sputum Gram stain and culture — at admission; same severe CAP / MRSA / Pseudomonas risk groups. NOT routine outpatient.1
  • Urinary Streptococcus pneumoniae antigen — at admission for severe CAP only (not routine).1
  • Urinary Legionella antigen + lower respiratory Legionella NAAT / culture — at admission for severe CAP; suspected Legionella outbreak / exposure / recent travel.1
  • Procalcitonin — NOT recommended to initiate antibiotics in suspected CAP (strong recommendation against); may guide duration if LOS >5–7 days (conditional, for de-escalation only).1
  • CBC, BMP, SpO2 / arterial blood gas — at presentation; repeat during hospitalization.1
  • Recurrent pneumonia workup — serum immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgG subclasses), HIV testing, chest CT, swallowing evaluation, COPD / asthma evaluation.1

Imaging

  • Routine follow-up chest x-ray — NOT recommended in adults whose symptoms resolved within 5–7 days.1
  • Selective repeat chest x-ray at 6–8 weeks — persistent symptoms, smokers ≥50 yr (lung cancer screening candidates), recurrent pneumonia same lobe (CT preferred).1
  • Chest CT — for structural lung disease / bronchiectasis / malignancy / sequestration evaluation in recurrent or persistent pneumonia.1

Physical examination

  • Pulse oximetry / SpO2 — at presentation; hypoxia <92% triggers admission consideration.1
  • BMI / nutritional status, COPD optimization — at follow-up.1

Functional / surveys

  • Post-CAP clinical resolution visit — 1–2 weeks after diagnosis. Symptomatic resolution, return to baseline functional status.1
  • Smoking cessation counseling — at every CAP encounter and follow-up.1
  • Pneumococcal vaccination — PCV15, PCV20, or PCV21 (single dose); if PCV15 used, follow with PPSV23 ≥1 yr later (≥8 wk if immunocompromised). All PCV-naïve adults ≥50 (effective Oct 2024); adults 19–49 with risk conditions.2
  • Annual influenza vaccine — universal adults ≥6 months; HD-IIV3, RIV3, or aIIV3 preferred for ≥65 yr.3
  • RSV vaccine — single dose (not annual). All adults ≥75; adults 50–74 with risk conditions.4
  • COVID-19 vaccine (2025–26 formulation) — per shared clinical decision-making; emphasis on ≥65 yr and high-risk.5
  • Recurrent pneumonia evaluation — after ≥2 episodes/year or 3 lifetime. Components: serum immunoglobulins, HIV testing, chest CT for structural lung disease, swallowing evaluation.1
  • Delirium screening + cognitive / functional assessment — during admission and at post-discharge follow-up in older adults (≥65) hospitalized with CAP. Detect new cognitive impairment / functional decline.6

Notes

  • Follow-up chest x-ray. IDSA / ATS 2019 explicitly does NOT recommend routine repeat CXR in patients improving by 5–7 days; many US clinicians still order 6–8 week films in smokers ≥50 — partially reconciled by lung cancer screening (USPSTF B recommendation for LDCT in eligible smokers) rather than CAP follow-up per se.1
  • Procalcitonin. IDSA / ATS 2019 strongly recommends AGAINST procalcitonin to initiate antibiotics in CAP (moderate evidence); IDSA / ATS 2016 HAP / VAP conditionally supports for antibiotic de-escalation only.1
  • PCV20 / PCV21 vs PCV15 + PPSV23. ACIP gives equal status, but single-dose PCV20 / 21 has shifted most US practice away from the sequential schedule.2
  • COVID-19 vaccination. 2025–26 ACIP move to shared clinical decision-making is at odds with positions from ACP, AAP, AAFP, and IDSA, which continue to recommend routine COVID vaccination for older and high-risk adults.5
  • Post-pneumonia cognitive surveillance. Supported by strong observational evidence (Davydow, Girard); not yet a formal recommendation of IDSA / ATS or AGS.6

Footnotes

  2. CDC / ACIP. Pneumococcal Vaccine Recommendations. Updated 2024 (≥50 years threshold effective Oct 23, 2024). See bibliography.md → cdc-pneumo-2024. ↩↩↩

  3. CDC / ACIP. Prevention and Control of Seasonal Influenza with Vaccines: 2025-26 Recommendations. MMWR 2025;74(32). See bibliography.md → cdc-flu-2025. ↩

  4. CDC. RSV Vaccine Guidance for Adults. Updated April 2025. See bibliography.md → cdc-rsv-2025. ↩↩

  5. CDC / ACIP. 2025-2026 COVID-19 Vaccination Guidance (shared clinical decision-making). See bibliography.md → cdc-covid-2025. ↩↩

Lung Cancer

ICD-10: C34.x (malignant neoplasm of bronchus and lung) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.3% — rank 27 of 30 Category: Cancer Primary associations consulted: NCCN, USPSTF, CMS, ASCO, ATS, ACCP

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — irAE labs (TSH, LFTs, glucose, cortisol) prior to each immunotherapy infusion; response assessment imaging every 6–12 wk on active systemic therapy — Broad molecular profiling at diagnosis of advanced / metastatic nonsquamous NSCLC (EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, KRAS G12C, MET exon 14, RET, NTRK, ERBB2 / HER2, NRG1, PD-L1 IHC); repeat at progression — Circulating biomarkers (ctDNA, CEA) NOT recommended for routine surveillance
Imaging — — Chest CT with contrast every 6 mo × 2–3 yr (stage I-II NSCLC); every 6 mo × 3 yr (stage III NSCLC); ASCO 2019 every 6 mo × 2 yr; chest CT every 2–6 mo × 1 yr then every 3–6 mo × 2–3 yr (SCLC); brain MRI every 3–4 mo × 1 yr then every 6 mo × 2 yr (limited-stage SCLC) Annual low-dose non-contrast chest CT after initial 2–3 yr surveillance window; annual LDCT lung-cancer screening for high-risk adults 50–80 yr with 20+ pack-year history (USPSTF Grade B; CMS NCD 210.14 ages 50–77, requires one-time shared decision-making visit / G0296) — Brain MRI NOT recommended routinely in stage I-III NSCLC (use only when symptomatic); PET / CT NOT recommended for routine surveillance
Physical exam Dyspnea and cough assessment — H&P + chest CT every 6 mo × 2–3 yr H&P annually after initial window — Dental / oral evaluation prior to thoracic RT; periodic on bone-modifying agents (zoledronic acid, denosumab for bone metastases)
Functional / surveys Smoking-cessation counseling + pharmacotherapy (combined behavioral + pharmacotherapy); distress screening (NCCN Distress Thermometer) — — Cancer-related fatigue screening; depression / anxiety (PHQ-9, GAD-7); chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy (post-platinum / taxane); dietary counseling / weight management; age-appropriate other-cancer screening per USPSTF; vaccinations per ACIP — Pulmonary rehabilitation pre-op (prehab) and post-op (6–12 wk program) for lung resection; symptomatic survivors with dyspnea / deconditioning

Summary recommendation

Post-curative NSCLC surveillance per NCCN v.2025 consists of H&P plus contrast-enhanced chest CT every 6 months for the first 2–3 years (longer interval for stage III), transitioning to annual low-dose non-contrast chest CT thereafter for life; ASCO 2019 endorses the same 6-mo × 2 yr then annual schedule.15 PET / CT and routine circulating biomarkers are explicitly NOT recommended for asymptomatic surveillance, and routine brain MRI is not recommended in stage I-III NSCLC (reserve for symptoms). Limited-stage SCLC requires intensified surveillance: chest CT every 2–6 months and brain MRI every 3–4 months in year 1, every 6 months in year 2 (NCCN SCLC v.2.2026).2 Metastatic NSCLC on active therapy requires response imaging every 6–12 weeks plus comprehensive molecular profiling at diagnosis (EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, KRAS G12C, MET ex14, RET, NTRK, HER2, NRG1, PD-L1) to guide targeted therapy and IO selection.1 Population screening with annual LDCT applies to adults 50–80 with ≥20 pack-years currently smoking or quit ≤15 years (USPSTF 2021 Grade B; CMS NCD 210.14, 2022, requires a one-time shared decision-making visit / G0296).34 Survivorship care addresses smoking cessation at every visit (combined behavioral + pharmacotherapy), pulmonary rehabilitation pre / post-resection, surveillance for platinum-induced peripheral neuropathy, screening for depression, fatigue, distress, dyspnea, dietary counseling, and age-appropriate other-cancer screening.6

Laboratory

  • Broad molecular profiling: EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, KRAS (incl G12C), MET exon 14, RET, NTRK1/2/3, ERBB2 (HER2), NRG1, PD-L1 IHC — at diagnosis of advanced / metastatic nonsquamous NSCLC; consider at progression with repeat biopsy / liquid biopsy. Select squamous (never / light smokers, small biopsy).1
  • PD-L1 IHC (tumor proportion score) — at diagnosis; repeat at progression. Inform pembrolizumab / IO selection.1
  • Immune-related adverse event labs (TSH, LFTs, glucose, cortisol) — prior to each immunotherapy infusion.8
  • Circulating biomarkers (ctDNA, CEA) — NOT recommended for routine surveillance.5

Imaging

  • Chest CT with contrast (NSCLC post-curative) — every 6 mo for 2–3 yr; longer interval (every 6 mo × 3 yr) for stage III; transition to annual low-dose non-contrast CT for life.15
  • Chest CT (SCLC post-curative) — every 2–3 mo × yr 1, every 3–6 mo × yr 2–3, every 6 mo × yr 4, then annually.2
  • Brain MRI (preferred) or brain CT with contrast (SCLC) — every 3–4 mo × yr 1, every 6 mo × yr 2 (regardless of PCI status).2
  • Brain MRI in NSCLC — NOT recommended routinely in stage I-III; use only when symptomatic.5
  • PET / CT — NOT recommended for routine surveillance.51
  • Low-dose CT (LDCT) lung-cancer screening — annual for high-risk adults 50–80 yr, 20+ pack-year history, currently smoking or quit ≤15 yr (USPSTF Grade B). CMS NCD 210.14 covers ages 50–77 with one-time shared decision-making visit (G0296).34
  • Response assessment imaging (chest / abdomen CT ± brain MRI) — every 6–12 wk during active systemic therapy (drug- and protocol-specific) for metastatic NSCLC and extensive-stage SCLC. RECIST response.12

Physical examination

  • Dyspnea and cough assessment — at every clinical encounter for all NSCLC / SCLC survivors and patients on active therapy.16
  • H&P + chest CT — per surveillance interval above.1
  • Dental / oral evaluation — baseline prior to thoracic radiation; periodically thereafter on bone-modifying agents (zoledronic acid, denosumab) for bone metastases.6

Functional / surveys

  • Smoking-cessation counseling + pharmacotherapy (combined behavioral + pharmacotherapy) — at every visit; every patient who smokes or has quit <12 mo. All lung cancer patients (any stage, any time point) and all screening candidates. NCCN Smoking Cessation v.2.2025 Category 1.74
  • Pulmonary rehabilitation — pre-operative (prehab) and post-operative; typical 6–12 wk program. Patients undergoing lung resection; symptomatic NSCLC survivors with dyspnea / deconditioning.9
  • Distress screening (NCCN Distress Thermometer) — at every visit; minimum at key transitions.6
  • Cancer-related fatigue screening — at every visit.6
  • Chemotherapy-induced peripheral neuropathy assessment — every visit during / after platinum or taxane therapy.6
  • Depression and anxiety screening (PHQ-9, GAD-7) — annually minimum; more often during active treatment.6
  • Dietary counseling / weight management — at each follow-up. Maintain healthy BMI; address sarcopenia.6
  • Age-appropriate other-cancer screening (CRC, breast, cervical, prostate per USPSTF) — per organ-specific guideline.6
  • Immunizations (influenza annual, pneumococcal, COVID-19, RSV per age, Tdap) — per ACIP schedule, particularly post-resection.6
  • Counseling and shared decision-making visit (G0296) — once, prior to first LDCT (Medicare-eligible high-risk population).4

Notes

  • CT surveillance interval, stage I NSCLC. NCCN allows every 6 mo for 2–3 yr (some institutions extending to 6–12 mo); ASCO 2019 specifies every 6 mo × 2 yr then annual. Observational data show no OS benefit comparing 3-, 6-, and 12-month intervals after stage I-III resection.15
  • LDCT vs. contrast CT for post-curative surveillance. NCCN transitions to annual LDCT non-contrast after the initial high-frequency window; some institutions continue contrast-enhanced CT lifelong, especially for stage III.1
  • PET / CT. ASCO and NCCN agree PET / CT should not be used for routine asymptomatic surveillance, but practice patterns frequently include PET — a known guideline-practice gap.51
  • Brain MRI for stage III NSCLC. ASCO 2019 does not recommend routine surveillance brain MRI in stage I-III NSCLC; some thoracic oncology programs perform baseline and surveillance brain MRI in stage III given higher CNS relapse risk, particularly after chemoradiation and durvalumab consolidation.5
  • Upper age limit for screening. USPSTF and CMS cap at 80 and 77 respectively; NCCN Lung Cancer Screening v.1.2025 allows continued screening beyond 80 if patient remains fit.34
  • ctDNA / MRD testing. Not endorsed by ASCO 2019 or NCCN as routine surveillance, but increasing institutional and trial use for molecular residual disease detection.51

Hip / Pelvic Fracture

ICD-10: S72.x (hip fracture), S32.x (pelvic fracture), M84.x (pathologic fracture), Z87.81 (personal history of pathologic fracture) CCW prevalence (2022 Medicare FFS): 1.2% — rank 28 of 30 Category: Musculoskeletal Primary associations consulted: AAOS, ASBMR, BHOF, AGS, ACS GSV, CDC STEADI, CMS

At-a-glance schedule

Type Every visit Quarterly Every 6 months Annually Every 2–3 years As indicated
Labs — — — 25-OH vitamin D (post-repletion); calcium intake assessment — Baseline secondary-causes labs (Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alk phos, Cr / eGFR, Mg, TSH, CBC, 24-hr urine Ca) before anti-resorptive initiation; serum Ca prior to IV zoledronate / denosumab dose; CTX / P1NP for adherence + response 3–6 mo after therapy start
Imaging — — — DEXA every 1–2 yr on therapy (AACE) or 1–3 yr (Endo Society) until stable — Baseline DEXA at index hospitalization or 1st outpatient visit if not previously osteoporotic; targeted radiographs only when clinically indicated (pain, function decline, suspected nonunion / hardware failure); vertebral fracture assessment (VFA) per BHOF triggers
Physical exam Pain assessment; mobility / PT / OT progress Multidisciplinary rehabilitation (PT, OT, SLP as indicated) — daily IRF (≥3 hr/day); SNF therapy ≥5 d/wk; outpatient PT ongoing — Standing height measurement; Timed Up and Go (TUG) screening; fall-risk reassessment via CDC STEADI Screen-Assess-Intervene — Comprehensive geriatric assessment (cognition, function, nutrition, goals of care, polypharmacy); CAM delirium screen daily / per shift through post-op hospital stay (age ≥75); surgical-site / hardware imaging only when clinically indicated
Functional / surveys Pain control; medication review for falls / orthostasis / sedation (AGS Beers Criteria) — Denosumab continuity check (no gap >7 mo) FRAX 10-yr fracture-risk recalculation; depression screen (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) at 1, 3, 6, 12 mo PCP visits; secondary fracture risk score — FLS (Fracture Liaison Service) / Own the Bone enrollment during index hospitalization; anti-resorptive initiation (oral bisphosphonate first-line; IV zoledronic acid annual; denosumab q6 mo) within months of fracture; VTE prophylaxis (LMWH / DOAC / aspirin) minimum 10–14 days, extendable 28–35 days post-op

Summary recommendation

Post-hip / pelvic fracture surveillance in adults ≥65 follows a phased trajectory anchored by interdisciplinary co-management.12 Acute inpatient and IRF care (Medicare Part A, no 3-day rule for IRF) includes ACS-GSV-aligned daily delirium screening, multimodal pain control, 10–35-day VTE prophylaxis, and ≥3 hr/day therapy.1012 Post-discharge SNF coverage (up to 100 days / benefit period; cost share after day 20 in 2026) or home-health continues PT / OT, with primary-care visits typically at 1, 3, 6, and 12 months.12 At those visits, clinicians repeat the CDC STEADI fall-risk screen (TUG <12 s target), apply the 2023 AGS Beers Criteria to deprescribe sedatives, anticholinergics, orthostasis-inducing agents, and other fall-risk drugs, and assess vitamin D and calcium adequacy (1,000–1,200 mg Ca / day; vitamin D supplementation in adults ≥75).711 If not previously diagnosed, DEXA is obtained (USPSTF 2025 grade B in women ≥65 and high-risk postmenopausal women).4 The ASBMR Secondary Fracture Prevention coalition, BHOF, and AOA's Own the Bone program direct prompt anti-resorptive initiation (IV zoledronic acid annually — strongest mortality / fracture-reduction evidence from HORIZON-RFT; oral alendronate or risedronate first-line; denosumab when bisphosphonates contraindicated), ideally coordinated through a Fracture Liaison Service.28 Ongoing surveillance includes depression screening, cognition monitoring, FRAX recalculation, and serial BMD on therapy.

Laboratory

  • Secondary-causes workup at index hospitalization — Ca, 25-OH vit D, PTH, alk phos, Cr / eGFR, Mg, TSH, CBC, 24-hr urine Ca; consider SPEP, celiac serology, testosterone (men).23
  • 25-OH vitamin D — baseline; repeat after repletion; vitamin D supplementation in adults ≥75 (Endocrine Society 2024 no longer endorses a universal 30 ng/mL threshold for healthy adults <75; ASBMR / BHOF still advise sufficiency).92
  • Serum calcium (corrected) — prior to each IV zoledronate or denosumab dose. Avoid hypocalcemia, especially with CKD.26
  • Bone-turnover markers (CTX or P1NP) — baseline; 3–6 months after starting / changing therapy; during drug holiday.63

Imaging

  • Central DEXA / BMD — baseline post-fracture if not previously diagnosed (USPSTF 2025 B in women ≥65). Repeat every 1–2 years on therapy (AACE) or 1–3 years (Endocrine Society) until stable.465
  • Targeted radiographs — only when clinically indicated (pain, function decline, suspected nonunion / hardware failure). Not routine.1
  • Vertebral fracture assessment (VFA) via DXA or lateral spine radiograph — at baseline if height loss ≥1.5 in historical or ≥0.8 in prospective, kyphosis, chronic glucocorticoids, or T-score ≤ −2.5.36

Physical examination

  • Standing height measurement (stadiometer) — annually. Trigger VFA if loss ≥2 cm prospective or ≥4 cm historical.3
  • Timed Up and Go (TUG) — at rehab discharge, then annually or with status change. TUG <12 sec (STEADI threshold); ≥12 sec increased fall risk.7
  • CDC STEADI Screen-Assess-Intervene (fall history, gait / balance, postural BP) — at every primary-care visit after fracture; minimum annually.72
  • Interdisciplinary functional assessment with PT / OT — daily during inpatient stay / IRF; multiple sessions/week in SNF; periodic outpatient follow-up.110
  • CAM (or equivalent) delirium screen — minimum daily / per shift in adults ≥75 through post-op hospital stay; reassess at discharge.10
  • Multimodal pain assessment — each visit during rehab and primary-care follow-up. Minimize opioids / Beers-listed agents.111

Functional / surveys

  • AGS Beers Criteria deprescribing review (fall-risk meds, orthostasis, sedation) — at each transition of care and at least annually. Discontinue / reduce benzodiazepines, anticholinergics, sedating antidepressants, alpha-blockers.11
  • Calcium intake — ongoing. Target 1,000–1,200 mg / day.23
  • Anti-resorptive initiation — promptly post-fracture (within months). Zoledronic acid annual; denosumab q6 mo; oral bisphosphonate weekly / monthly. HORIZON: ≥2 weeks post-op (median ~6 wk); 35% fracture risk reduction, 28% mortality reduction with zoledronic acid.28
  • Fracture Liaison Service (FLS) / Own the Bone enrollment — during index hospitalization; coordinator follow-up over 1 yr. Target ≥80% of fracture patients evaluated, treated, and adherent at 1 yr.23
  • VTE prophylaxis (LMWH, fondaparinux, DOAC, warfarin, or aspirin) — minimum 10–14 days; extend up to 28–35 days post-op.1
  • Depression screening (PHQ-2 / PHQ-9) — at rehab transition and at 1, 3, 6, 12 mo primary-care visits.2
  • FRAX 10-year fracture-risk recalculation — post-fracture and on follow-up; with each BMD.32
  • Comprehensive geriatric / orthogeriatric assessment — pre-op (when feasible), in-hospital, and post-discharge.101

Notes

  • Anti-resorptive timing. HORIZON-RFT supports zoledronic acid at ≥2 weeks post-op (median ~6 wk); some orthopaedic groups historically delayed bisphosphonates 3 months over fracture-healing concerns — now largely refuted. AAOS does not specify exact start; ASBMR / BHOF recommend "prompt" initiation within months.82
  • Vitamin D target. 2024 Endocrine Society guideline no longer endorses 25-OH-D ≥30 ng/mL and discourages empiric testing in healthy adults <75; ASBMR 2019 and BHOF still advise sufficiency (≥30 ng/mL, with ≥20 ng/mL as a more conservative threshold per IOM).92
  • FLS coverage. No dedicated CMS bundled benefit; reimbursement is through component E/M, care-management codes, and DEXA — a recognized gap in BHOF policy commentary.3
  • Drug-of-choice. ASBMR lists alendronate / risedronate as first-line on cost / tolerability; HORIZON-RFT gives zoledronic acid a unique mortality-reduction advantage.28
  • TUG cutoff. STEADI / general fall screening uses ≥12 sec; post-hip-fracture-specific literature has used 24 sec to predict 6-month falls.7
